# Module 1 — Classical CV Foundations

> **Prerequisite calibration:** You already know deep learning. Nothing here re-teaches gradients-as-in-backprop. "Gradient" in this module means *image gradient* — a spatial derivative of pixel intensity. The entire module is about what an image *is* geometrically and photometrically, and how to find repeatable structure in it without learning anything.
>
> **Why a DL-fluent person still must own this module:** every modern vision system that touches 3D — SLAM, AR, robotics, autonomous driving, photogrammetry, 3D Gaussian Splatting pipelines — still runs classical geometry at its core. Interviewers use Module 1 + Module 5 as the *filter* question set precisely because most candidates skipped them.

## 1.1 Image Representation & Formation 🟡

### Intuition

In deep learning, you're used to an image just showing up as a tensor — a fancy word for "a grid of numbers arranged in a shape a neural network can multiply." You feed it in and never ask where those numbers came from. This lesson is about stopping and asking exactly that question: **where did those numbers actually come from, and what real-world physical quantity is each one measuring?** The answer matters because it decides which kinds of math you're allowed to do on those numbers and still get a sensible result.

Here's the core idea, stated as plainly as possible: a digital image is not "a picture" in the way a painting is a picture. It is a **grid of measurements of how much light landed on a tiny bucket, over a short slice of time.** Think of each pixel as a tiny rain gauge, except instead of catching raindrops it catches particles of light (called photons) for a fraction of a second, and then reports "this many landed on me."

That one sentence explains a surprising number of things that feel like weird bugs in computer vision:
- Why a shadow falling across an object can break a color-based detector that worked fine in bright light.
- Why simply making an image brighter can ruin the smooth blending you were trying to do (we'll see exactly why later in this lesson).
- Why the same red shirt looks like a slightly different color depending on whether you're standing near a window or under indoor lighting.

All three of these trace back to the fact that a pixel value is a *measurement*, made under specific physical conditions, not an abstract "color label."

**A real-life anchor.** Your phone's camera does not actually store a red, a green, and a blue number at every single point on the sensor. Physically, each individual light-sensing spot on the sensor chip (called a photosite — think of it as one individual "light bucket" on a grid of millions of them) is covered by a tiny colored filter and can only measure *one* of red, green, or blue at that spot. The other two colors at that same spot are *guessed* by a piece of software that looks at the neighboring buckets and interpolates (a math term for "estimates a value in between known values, like guessing the temperature at 2:30pm if you know it was 20°C at 2pm and 22°C at 3pm"). So when your phone hands you a "12 megapixel color photo," roughly a third of those numbers were actually measured, and the rest were mathematically guessed. Keep that in your back pocket — we'll return to it later in this lesson.

### The formation chain

Let's trace the entire journey a piece of light takes, from bouncing off an object in the real world, to becoming the number `247` sitting in a pixel of a JPEG file. This journey has several stages, and at *every* stage, some information either gets thrown away or gets warped in a predictable way. Those two things — information being thrown away, and information being warped — are exactly the things that come back to bite you in real CV code, so we'll flag each one as we go.

Here is the chain of stages, written out left to right:

$$
\text{Light leaving the scene} \;\xrightarrow{\text{lens, aperture}}\; \text{Light hitting the sensor} \;\xrightarrow{\text{measured over a short time, per color}}\; \text{electrical charge} \;\xrightarrow{\text{analog-to-digital conversion}}\; \text{raw number} \;\xrightarrow{\text{camera's internal processing}}\; \text{final pixel value } I(x,y)
$$

Let's unpack every one of those arrows in plain English, because each is a technical term hiding a simple idea:

- **"Light leaving the scene"** (technically called *radiance*) is just: how much light is bouncing off (or being emitted by) a point in the world, in every direction. A white wall in sunlight has high radiance; a black cat in a dim room has low radiance.
- **"Light hitting the sensor"** (technically called *irradiance*) is: how much of that bounced light actually arrives at the camera's sensor, after passing through the lens and the aperture (the adjustable hole that controls how much light gets let in — like the pupil of your eye).
- **"Measured over a short time, per color"**: the sensor doesn't take an instantaneous snapshot. It sits there and collects light for a chosen duration (the shutter speed, e.g. 1/125th of a second), and it does this separately behind each color filter. How sensitive a filter is to each specific wavelength of light (i.e., each specific color) is called its *spectral sensitivity* — think of it as "how strongly does the red filter respond to a beam of orange light versus a beam of deep red light."
- **"Electrical charge"**: each light bucket converts the light it caught into a small electrical signal, the way a solar panel converts sunlight into electricity.
- **"Analog-to-digital conversion" (ADC)**: that continuous electrical signal (which could in principle be any value, like 3.7 or 3.71829...) gets rounded into a plain digital number the computer can store, like the number 142.
- **"Camera's internal processing" (the ISP, short for Image Signal Processor)**: a chip inside your camera or phone that takes those raw numbers and does a bunch of cleanup — guessing the missing colors per pixel (mentioned above), adjusting the overall brightness curve, sharpening, compressing — before finally handing you the image file you actually open in Python.

Now let's write this as one equation for a single pixel, in a single color channel $c$ (where $c$ is just "which color are we talking about" — red, green, or blue):

$$
I_c(x,y) \;=\; \gamma\!\left( \int_{\lambda} E(x,y,\lambda)\, s_c(\lambda)\, d\lambda \cdot \Delta t \right)
$$

Don't panic at the symbols — here is exactly what each one means, in words:

- $x, y$: the pixel's location in the image (its column and row).
- $\lambda$ (the Greek letter "lambda"): the wavelength of light, i.e., which specific color of light we're talking about at the finest physical level (light isn't really "red" or "green" — it's a wave with a specific length, and our eyes/sensors bucket different wavelengths into color categories).
- $E(x,y,\lambda)$: how much light of wavelength $\lambda$ is landing on the sensor at position $(x,y)$ — this is the "light hitting the sensor" quantity from above, but broken down wavelength by wavelength.
- $s_c(\lambda)$: the spectral sensitivity we just defined — how strongly channel $c$'s filter responds to wavelength $\lambda$.
- $\int_\lambda (\ldots) \, d\lambda$: this is an integral, which here just means "add up the contribution from every possible wavelength of light." You can't sense wavelength directly, so the sensor "blurs together" the entire spectrum of incoming light into one number per channel by weighting each wavelength by the filter's sensitivity, and adding it all up.
- $\Delta t$: the exposure time — how long the shutter stayed open, collecting light.
- $\gamma(\cdot)$ (gamma): a warping function applied at the very end. We're about to spend an entire section on why this exists and why it matters so much — hold that thought.

So in plain English, this equation says: *"the number you get for one color channel at one pixel is: take all the incoming light of every wavelength, weight each wavelength by how sensitive this color filter is to it, add it all up, multiply by how long you collected light for, and then run the whole thing through a warping function called gamma."*

Three consequences of this equation cause almost all the practical headaches in computer vision. Let's go through each one slowly.

**1. Adding up all the wavelengths into one number throws away information — this is called metamerism.**

Because the equation adds together the *entire spectrum* of incoming light into a single red number, a single green number, and a single blue number, two physically completely different mixtures of light wavelengths can produce the *exact same* $(R, G, B)$ triple. This effect — two different physical spectra giving identical measured colors — is called **metamerism**. It's the same idea as: if I only tell you "the total of two numbers is 10," you cannot tell whether those numbers were 5 and 5, or 1 and 9. The camera only tells you the "total" (the RGB triple), not the full wavelength-by-wavelength story.

A concrete example: two paint chips can look identical under your office's fluorescent lighting but look noticeably different colors when you step outside into sunlight, because the light source has a different mix of wavelengths, and the paints reflect those wavelengths differently even though they matched under one specific light. This is exactly why "figure out this exact paint color from a photo" is a mathematically ill-posed problem (a fancy way of saying: there isn't enough information in the photo to uniquely determine the answer, no matter how clever your algorithm is) — the camera already threw away the information you'd need.

**2. Exposure time and aperture are choices you (or the camera) make, and different photos almost never use the same choices.**

$\Delta t$ (how long the shutter was open) and the aperture size are settings a camera picks, often automatically, and they change from photo to photo depending on lighting. This means: if you take two photos of the same scene a second apart, they are very likely to have different overall brightness, purely because the exposure settings changed — not because anything in the world actually changed. This is exactly why, later in the course (topic 1.9, panorama stitching — the technique that glues multiple overlapping photos into one wide image), you'll need a step called "gain compensation" that specifically corrects for the fact that two overlapping photos of the same wall might have different overall brightness even though it's the same wall.

**3. $\gamma(\cdot)$ is a non-linear warp — and this is the one that trips people up the most.**

"Non-linear" just means: it's not as simple as "multiply by a constant number." A linear function is one where doubling the input doubles the output (like `output = 3 × input`). A non-linear function bends the relationship — doubling the input might, say, only increase the output by 50%. We're about to spend the whole next section on exactly why this matters, because it is the single most common source of subtle bugs in image-processing code.

### Gamma: the trap

Here's the key fact to hold onto: the *raw* electrical signal coming off the sensor is (approximately) **directly proportional to the number of photons that landed** — twice as many photons in, twice as big a number out. That's what "linear in light" means, and it matches your intuition: twice the light should mean twice the signal.

But the 8-bit image file you load with `cv2.imread` (the numbers from 0 to 255 you actually work with in code) is **not** that raw linear signal. Somewhere in the pipeline, that non-linear warp $\gamma(\cdot)$ we saw above gets applied, and the specific warp almost every photo and screen in the world uses is called **sRGB** (a standard for how brightness gets encoded, used by essentially every phone, camera, and monitor). Roughly:

$$
I_{\text{sRGB}} \approx L_{\text{linear}}^{1/2.2}
$$

In words: take the true linear light value $L_{\text{linear}}$ (a number from 0 = no light to 1 = maximum), and raise it to the power $1/2.2$ (roughly the same as taking something close to a square root) to get the stored pixel value $I_{\text{sRGB}}$. (The real sRGB formula is slightly more fiddly — a straight line near black, then a curve of $1.055 \times L^{1/2.4} - 0.055$ above that — but "raise to the power 1/2.2" is the version everyone uses as a good-enough approximation, and it's the one we'll reason with here.)

**Why would anyone deliberately warp the data like this?** Because of a fact about human eyeballs: our eyes are much better at telling apart *dark* shades from each other than *bright* shades from each other. If you split a scene's brightness range into, say, 256 evenly-spaced physical light levels (0 photons, 1 unit, 2 units, ..., all the way up), almost all of those 256 steps would fall in the bright end, where your eye can barely tell two neighboring steps apart — and you'd be left with only a handful of steps to describe the entire range of shadows, where your eye is actually most sensitive to small differences. That would cause visible ugly stripes/bands in dark areas of images (this banding is a real visual artifact you can see with your own eyes if you try it). By applying the power-of-$1/2.2$ curve, the camera spends more of its limited 256 numbers on the dark end, matching where your eye actually needs the precision, and fewer on the bright end where your eye can't tell the difference anyway. This warp is a deliberate, useful compression scheme matched to human vision — not some incidental quirk of the display hardware.

**Now let's work out, step by step, why this warp causes real bugs**, rather than just being told the punchline.

A large number of everyday image operations — averaging two pixels, blurring, shrinking an image to a smaller size, and smoothly fading one image into another (called alpha-blending) — are all what's called **linear operations**. A linear operation just means: you compute a weighted sum, i.e. you multiply each input by some weight and add the results (a plain average is the simplest example: multiply each value by $\tfrac{1}{2}$ and add).

Here's the catch: a weighted sum only gives you a physically correct answer when the quantity you're averaging is something that *actually adds up in the real world*. And the thing that actually adds up in the real world is **photons** — the raw, linear light measurement — not the gamma-warped stored pixel value.

Let's prove this with actual algebra instead of just asserting it. Say $f$ is our gamma warp function ($f(L) = L^{1/2.2}$), and we have two linear light values $a$ and $b$. We want to know: is averaging the *encoded* (gamma-warped) values the same as encoding the average of the *linear* values? In symbols, is

$$
f\!\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right) \;\overset{?}{=}\; \frac{f(a) + f(b)}{2}
$$

For this to be true in general, $f$ would need to be a straight line (a linear function). But $f(L) = L^{1/2.2}$ is a *curve*, not a straight line — so in general, the two sides are **not** equal. There's actually a neat piece of math (called Jensen's inequality — you don't need to memorize the name, just the consequence) that tells us not just that they differ, but *which direction* the error goes: because the curve $x^{1/2.2}$ bends "downward" as $x$ increases (mathematicians call this "concave" — picture a hill shape, curving down from a rising slope), averaging two already-encoded values always gives you a result that's a bit *lower* (darker) than the correct encoding of the true average. Averaging in the wrong space systematically makes things too dark.

**Pause:** you average an sRGB `0` and an sRGB `255`. You get `128`. Is that the correct half-brightness pixel?

No — and here's the full step-by-step reasoning. The stored values `0` and `255` are gamma-encoded, so before we can average anything meaningfully, we need to think in terms of actual light. `0` corresponds to $L = 0$ (no light) and `255` corresponds to $L = 1$ (maximum light, since 255 is the max of the 0–255 scale, which represents the linear range 0 to 1). The true "half the light" value is simply $L = 0.5$ — literally half as many photons as the maximum. Now we need to *encode* that true halfway point back into sRGB using our formula: $255 \times 0.5^{1/2.2}$. Working that out: $0.5^{1/2.2} \approx 0.5^{0.4545} \approx 0.73$, so $255 \times 0.73 \approx 186$. That's the *correct* half-brightness stored pixel value — noticeably brighter than the naive average of 128. The gap between 128 (what naive averaging gives you) and 186 (what's physically correct) is 58 levels out of 255 — nearly a quarter of the entire range. That is not a rounding error; that's a real, visible bug.

This single fact explains a very concrete, visible problem: **when you shrink an image (downsampling) using naive averaging, thin bright lines on a dark background tend to fade out or disappear.** Here's why, using the same logic: a thin bright line surrounded by dark background gets averaged together with its dark neighbors when you shrink the image. Because the averaging happens in the gamma-encoded (warped) space instead of the true linear-light space, the bright line's contribution gets systematically under-weighted, exactly the way our 128-vs-186 example showed. The fix in real code is: convert the image out of its gamma-encoded form back into linear light first, do your averaging/blurring/resizing there, and then re-apply the gamma encoding at the end.

### Colour spaces

So far we've been assuming an image is stored as Red, Green, Blue (RGB) numbers. But RGB is just one *way* of writing down a color — one "coordinate system," the same way you could describe a point on a map using (latitude, longitude) or using (distance from downtown, compass direction). Different color coordinate systems make different problems easy or hard. Here's a comparison table, with every term defined right after:

| Space | What it actually is | Good for | Fails at |
|---|---|---|---|
| **RGB** | Three numbers: how much red light, green light, and blue light — these are called "additive primaries" because you build any color by adding light of these three colors together (like stage lighting) | Displaying on screens, feeding into CNNs | If the lighting on a scene changes, all three numbers shift together in a way that's hard to separate from an actual color change |
| **HSV / HSL** | Picture a cylinder (like a soup can) standing upright. Going *around* the can is Hue (which color, e.g. red vs. green vs. blue — measured as an angle, like a position on a clock face). Going *outward* from the center is Saturation (how vivid/pure vs. washed-out/grey the color is). Going *up* the can is Value (how bright/dark). | Picking out a specific color while ignoring how bright or washed-out it is, e.g. "find anything reddish" regardless of shadow | Hue is meaningless when Saturation is 0 (a pure grey has no "direction" of color at all — more on this below), and Hue behaves oddly ("wraps around") because it's an angle, not a plain number |
| **LAB (CIELAB)** | Three numbers: $L^*$ = lightness (dark to light), $a^*$ = how green vs. how red, $b^*$ = how blue vs. how yellow. These are called "opponent axes" because human vision itself seems to work this way — you can't perceive a color as both red and green at once. | Measuring how *different two colors look to a human eye*, grouping pixels by color similarity, transferring the color "mood" of one photo onto another | Requires assuming what counts as a "pure white" reference point for the scene; more computation to produce |
| **YCbCr** | One number for overall brightness ("luma," similar in spirit to grayscale) plus two numbers describing the color on top of that brightness ("chroma") | Video and JPEG compression — because the human eye is less sensitive to color detail than brightness detail, you can store the two chroma numbers at lower resolution and save file size (called "4:2:0 chroma subsampling") | The numerical distances between colors don't match how different they actually look to a human eye |

**Converting RGB to grayscale (a single brightness number) is NOT the same as just averaging the three color numbers.** Two commonly used formulas: the older "Rec.601" standard, $Y = 0.299R + 0.587G + 0.114B$, and the newer HD standard "Rec.709," $Y = 0.2126R + 0.7152G + 0.0722B$. Notice green gets by far the biggest weight in both. Why? Because the human eye has more light-sensing cells (called cone cells) tuned to wavelengths near green than to red or blue — roughly twice as many "green-ish" cone cells as "blue-ish" ones — so our eyes are simply more sensitive to green light's brightness than to the same physical amount of red or blue light. A formula that wants its "brightness" number to match what a human perceives as bright has to weight green more heavily to match that biological reality. OpenCV's `COLOR_BGR2GRAY` conversion uses the older Rec.601 weights.

**Converting RGB to HSV.** The formula below looks like it was pulled out of thin air until you picture what it's actually doing geometrically. Imagine the RGB color cube — a cube where each of the three axes is how much red, green, and blue you have, so the corner at (0,0,0) is black and the opposite corner at (max,max,max) is white. Now imagine looking straight down the diagonal line running from that black corner to that white corner (this diagonal line represents every possible shade of pure grey, since grey is equal parts R, G, B). Looking down this line, every color in the cube appears to project onto a flat hexagon shape around it.

Given that picture:
- **How far along the black-to-white diagonal you are** tells you the brightness — this becomes $V$ (Value).
- **How far out from the center diagonal line you are** tells you how vivid/colorful (as opposed to washed-out/grey) the color is — this becomes $S$ (Saturation).
- **Which direction around the hexagon you're pointing** tells you which color it is (red-ish? green-ish? blue-ish?) — this becomes $H$ (Hue), measured as an angle.

So all three of these numbers can be computed just by looking at the biggest of the R/G/B values, the smallest of them, and the gap between those two. Here's why that makes sense: the *biggest* channel tells you how bright the "most dominant" color is (which sets $V$), and the *gap* between the biggest and smallest channel — call it $C$ — tells you how far this color has strayed from being perfectly grey (if R, G, and B were all equal, the gap would be zero, and zero gap means "sitting exactly on the grey diagonal"). Let $M = \max(R,G,B)$, $m = \min(R,G,B)$, and $C = M - m$ (the gap):

$$
V = M, \qquad S = \begin{cases}0 & M = 0\\ C/M & \text{else}\end{cases}, \qquad
H = 60^\circ \times \begin{cases}
\frac{G-B}{C} \bmod 6 & M = R\\
\frac{B-R}{C} + 2 & M = G\\
\frac{R-G}{C} + 4 & M = B
\end{cases}
$$

(The symbol $\bmod$ means "remainder after division" — e.g. $7 \bmod 6 = 1$ — used here just to keep the angle wrapped correctly within one full turn.)

Don't let the three-case formula for $H$ intimidate you — all three cases are doing the exact same thing: "measure the angle, starting your count from whichever of red/green/blue is currently the biggest value, and use the other two channels to fine-tune exactly where in between you are." The $60°$ multiplier appears because the hexagon we pictured earlier has six sides, and a full circle is $360°$, so each side of the hexagon covers exactly $360° \div 6 = 60°$.

Now notice something important: $C$ (the gap between biggest and smallest channel) sits in the *denominator* of both the $S$ and $H$ formulas. As a color gets closer and closer to a pure grey, $C$ shrinks toward zero — and dividing by a number that's shrinking toward zero makes the result explode toward infinity, then become undefined (mathematically meaningless) exactly at $C = 0$. This isn't a bug in the formula — it's the geometrically honest answer: a point sitting *exactly on* the black-to-white grey diagonal genuinely has no "direction around the hexagon" to point toward, the same way the North Pole has no meaningful "compass direction" — you can't say "which way is north" when you're standing exactly on it. **Practical consequence: any time you build a mask or filter based on hue (e.g. "find everything reddish"), you must also require the saturation to be above some minimum threshold, or your mask will behave erratically on near-grey pixels.**

**Converting RGB to LAB** is a two-step process — first you convert to an in-between coordinate system called CIE XYZ (a standard reference space designed to match human color perception in general, not tied to any particular camera or screen), and then from XYZ to LAB:

$$
\begin{bmatrix}X\\Y\\Z\end{bmatrix} = M_{\text{sRGB}\to\text{XYZ}} \begin{bmatrix}R_{\text{lin}}\\G_{\text{lin}}\\B_{\text{lin}}\end{bmatrix},\qquad
L^* = 116\,f(Y/Y_n) - 16,\quad a^* = 500\,[f(X/X_n) - f(Y/Y_n)]
$$

Here $M_{\text{sRGB}\to\text{XYZ}}$ is just a fixed grid of numbers (a matrix) that, when multiplied against linear R, G, B values, produces X, Y, Z — this multiplication is a linear operation, exactly the kind we discussed in the gamma section. $Y_n$ and $X_n$ are reference values representing "pure white" under some assumed lighting condition, used to rescale things. $f(t) = t^{1/3}$ (cube root) for most values, with a small linear patch used for very small $t$ to avoid a mathematical blow-up near zero.

**Here is the single most common bug in color-space code, stated as a rule: you must undo gamma (convert back to linear light) before doing that matrix multiplication.** Why? For exactly the same reason as in the gamma section: that matrix multiplication is a *linear* operation, defined to work correctly on true physical (linear) light quantities. If you instead feed it the gamma-warped sRGB numbers straight out of your image file, you're applying a linear operation to numbers that have already been non-linearly distorted — and just like averaging two sRGB values gave the wrong answer earlier, multiplying gamma-warped values by this matrix gives a wrong, distorted result too.

Why the cube root in the LAB formula, specifically? Because the goal of LAB is that plain straight-line (Euclidean) distance between two LAB triples should approximately match how *different two colors look to a human eye*. This match is good enough that there's a standard formula for "perceptual color difference," $\Delta E_{76} = \|\Delta \mathbf{Lab}\|_2$ (just the ordinary straight-line distance between two LAB points), and a $\Delta E$ of about 2.3 is roughly the smallest color difference an average human can notice. That's precisely why algorithms that group pixels by color similarity — like k-means color quantization (reducing an image to a small palette of colors), color transfer (making one photo adopt another's color mood), and superpixel algorithms like SLIC (grouping neighboring similar-colored pixels into small blobs) — all do their distance math in LAB space rather than RGB, where straight-line distance doesn't correspond well to perceived difference at all.

### Sensor-level facts worth knowing

A few more physical facts about how cameras actually work, each defined from scratch:

- **The Bayer mosaic (RGGB) and demosaicing.** Remember from the intuition section that each photosite (light bucket) can only sense one color? The specific, extremely common pattern of colored filters laid over the grid of photosites is called a Bayer mosaic: for every group of 4 buckets, 2 are green, 1 is red, 1 is blue (50% green, 25% each red and blue — extra green because, as we discussed, human eyes are most sensitive to green, so the camera manufacturer devotes more of the resolution budget there). The process of filling in the two missing colors at every pixel by guessing from neighbors is called **demosaicing**. Because it's a guess (an interpolation), it introduces two side effects: (1) noise (random speckle) that is correlated between neighboring pixels in an unnatural way, and (2) color fringing — faint rainbow-colored edges — near sharp boundaries in the image. Some specialized fields (astronomy, scientific imaging) skip demosaicing entirely and work directly with the raw, single-color-per-pixel sensor data to avoid this.

- **Sensor noise has two distinct sources, and one of them gets *worse* in bright areas.** The first source: light itself arrives in discrete individual particles (photons), and the number of photons that land in a tiny bucket in a fixed time window is random, even if the true brightness is constant — the same way if you count raindrops landing in a cup during one second, you won't get the exact same count every time even in perfectly steady rain. This kind of "random count of discrete arriving events" is called a **Poisson process**, and it has a specific mathematical property: if the average count is $\mu$, the amount of random spread (the variance) is *also* $\mu$. In plain terms: brighter pixels (higher average photon count) have *more* absolute random wobble than dark pixels, purely from counting statistics — it's not a flaw in the sensor, it's an unavoidable fact about counting random discrete events. On top of this, there's a second, separate source of noise called read noise — random electrical wobble introduced by the sensor's own circuitry when it reads out the charge — which does *not* depend on how bright the pixel is. Put together, the total noise variance follows a pattern like $\sigma^2 = a\mu + b$ (a constant amount from read noise, plus an amount that grows with brightness from the counting effect). The practical takeaway: any denoising algorithm that assumes noise is the same flat amount everywhere in the image (a single number for all pixels) will systematically get it wrong — it should instead expect more noise in brighter regions and less in darker ones. There's also a math trick (called the Anscombe transform) that reshapes this brightness-dependent noise into an approximately flat, brightness-independent form, making certain algorithms easier to apply — you don't need to know the formula, just that it exists and why (to turn brightness-dependent Poisson noise into ordinary flat noise).

- **Rolling shutter.** Most modern camera sensors (CMOS-type, which is nearly all phone and webcam sensors) don't capture the entire image in one simultaneous instant. Instead, they scan through the image row by row, exposing and reading out one row slightly after the previous one — like a photocopier scanning down a page rather than flashing the whole page at once. If something is moving fast during that scan (a spinning propeller, a car driving past), different rows of the image end up capturing the scene at slightly different moments in time, producing a skewed or wobbly look. This matters a lot in Module 5 (3D vision), because a foundational assumption there — that a camera captures the whole scene from one single position at one single instant (the "pinhole" assumption) — is technically broken by rolling shutter, and it's a real, practical source of errors in tasks like a drone or phone estimating 3D structure from its own video.

### Connect it

Every tool coming up in the next couple of lessons (Section 1.2 on filtering, Section 1.3 on edge detection) works by treating the image $I(x,y)$ as a smooth, well-behaved field of numbers that you're allowed to do calculus-like operations on (like measuring how fast it changes from one pixel to the next). Whether "the difference between two pixel values" corresponds to anything physically meaningful in the real scene depends entirely on the gamma encoding and color space choices we just covered. Later, in Module 2, this same issue resurfaces as a specific practical detail: when you normalize images for a CNN (subtracting a mean and dividing by a standard deviation before feeding them in), the standard ImageNet mean/std numbers you'll use were computed on gamma-encoded sRGB images — meaning the network itself learns to operate around this non-linear warp rather than around true linear light. And in Module 5, the "pinhole camera" model of geometric image formation picks up exactly where this lesson's formation chain left off.

**In your own words:** why is averaging two pixels not the same as averaging the light they represent?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

A surface-level answer just lists off "RGB, HSV, LAB" as if reciting vocabulary. A strong answer demonstrates you understand the mechanics underneath:

1. **"HSV is not illumination-invariant, it is illumination-*decoupled* — and only for plain brightness scaling, not for a change in the color of the light source itself."** In other words, HSV cleanly separates out "how bright is the light" (that's $V$) from "what color is the object" (that's $H$ and $S$) — but only when the light source's *own color* stays the same and just gets dimmer or brighter. A warm-toned (tungsten) light bulb doesn't just dim things — it also shifts the actual hue of everything it illuminates, and HSV alone cannot correct for that. Fixing that requires something called white balance or chromatic adaptation (a step, either automatic in-camera or computed afterward, that estimates the color of the light source and compensates for it) — this is a genuinely separate problem from what HSV solves.
2. **Hue is an angle, not a plain number — so ordinary statistics on it are wrong.** If you have hue values of 350° and 10° (both close to red, just on either side of the 0°/360° wraparound point), a naive average gives you 180° (which is cyan — nowhere near either original color!) because plain averaging doesn't know the scale wraps around. To correctly average angles, you need what's called circular statistics: convert each angle into an (x, y) point on a circle using $(\cos\theta, \sin\theta)$, average those points normally, then convert back to an angle with $\bar{\theta} = \operatorname{atan2}(\overline{\sin\theta}, \overline{\cos\theta})$ (atan2 is just the standard function for "what angle does this x,y point sit at"). Any time you're tempted to take a mean, a variance, a Euclidean distance, or a Gaussian blur directly on raw hue values, you're making this exact mistake.
3. **Gamma discipline.** Any blending or resizing operation should happen in linear light (i.e., after undoing the gamma warp), not directly on the stored gamma-encoded values. Being the person in an interview who volunteers "I'd linearize before averaging" — without being prompted — is a strong, specific signal that you understand the physics, not just the API calls.
4. **Metamerism and color constancy.** Because a camera fundamentally cannot recover the full wavelength spectrum from just three RGB numbers (as we showed earlier), figuring out "what color is this object, independent of the lighting" is a mathematically ill-posed problem — there just isn't enough information in a photo alone to solve it with certainty. This is exactly why white balance algorithms work by making an assumption (a "prior," such as "assume the average color in the scene is grey") rather than by directly computing an answer from the data — there is no purely data-driven answer available.

### ✅ Mastery check

You're using an HSV-based threshold (a rule like "keep only pixels whose hue is in this range") to detect a green ball. It works fine indoors, but it fails near a bright window, where part of the ball sits in direct sunlight and part sits in shadow. Both regions are physically the same green ball. Using the H, S, V equations from earlier, explain precisely *which* of the three channels shifts in each region, and why a mask based purely on a hue range still fails specifically at the bright, shiny highlight spot (not just in the shadow).

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>

**In the shadow region:** less light is reaching that part of the ball, which means $R$, $G$, and $B$ are all scaled down by roughly the same factor (a shadow doesn't change what color something is, just how much light is bouncing off it). Look back at the formulas: $V = M = \max(R,G,B)$ drops, because everything got dimmer. But $S = C/M$ stays roughly the same, because both $C$ (the gap between max and min channel) and $M$ shrink by the same proportion, so their ratio is unchanged. And $H$ (which only depends on the *ratios* between channels, via that $60° \times (\ldots)/C$ formula) also stays roughly the same, because scaling all three channels by the same factor doesn't change their relative proportions to each other. This is exactly the illumination-decoupling property mentioned earlier — a mask based on hue and saturation, ignoring $V$, correctly survives the shadow region.

**At the bright highlight (the shiny spot where light directly glints off the ball's surface):** this is a different physical effect called a specular reflection — light bouncing directly off the surface like a mirror, rather than being absorbed and re-emitted in the object's actual color. A specular highlight adds a roughly white/colorless contribution on top of the ball's normal green color, hitting all three of R, G, and B channels roughly equally. Adding an equal amount to all three channels raises the *minimum* channel value $m$ specifically (the smallest channel gets boosted proportionally the most, since it had the most "room" relative to itself), which shrinks the gap $C = M - m$. Since $S = C/M$, a shrinking $C$ means $S$ collapses toward zero. And recall from the earlier discussion: as $C \to 0$, the hue formula (which divides by $C$) becomes numerically unstable and then mathematically undefined.

So the mask doesn't fail at the highlight because the ball's hue actually "changed color" — it fails because hue *stopped being a well-defined quantity* at that spot, the same way "which direction is north" stops being well-defined exactly at the North Pole. **The fix:** require a minimum saturation ($S > S_{\min}$) before trusting the hue value at all, and handle low-saturation pixels (both true greys and specular highlights) as a separate special case — or use a more detailed physical model of how light reflects off surfaces (called the dichromatic reflection model, which explicitly separates the "true object color" component of reflected light from the "mirror-like white glint" component) to remove the specular contribution before thresholding.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build (one sitting):** Write a script called `linear_vs_srgb_blend.py`. Load an image and shrink it to a quarter of its size (a 4× downsample) two different ways: (a) directly average groups of pixels on the raw uint8 sRGB values as loaded, and (b) first undo the gamma encoding to get linear light values, then average, then re-apply the gamma encoding. Compute the pixel-by-pixel difference between the two results and display it as an error map (an image where brighter = bigger difference) so you can see exactly where and how much the naive method got wrong. Then, without using `cv2.cvtColor`, implement the RGB→HSV and RGB→LAB conversion formulas from scratch using the equations in this lesson, and check that your output matches OpenCV's built-in conversion to within 1 unit out of 255 (called "1 LSB," short for "least significant bit" — just means "off by at most the smallest possible rounding step").

**Read:** Szeliski, *Computer Vision: Algorithms and Applications* 2nd ed., sections 2.2–2.3 (these cover exactly how light physically becomes an image, called "photometric image formation") and section 3.1. Then watch Shree Nayar's "First Principles of Computer Vision" YouTube series — specifically the Imaging / Radiometry playlist, which walks through this same light-to-pixel pipeline with helpful diagrams.

---

## 1.2 Filtering & Convolution

### Intuition

Let's start with a basic fact about digital images: a single pixel, by itself, tells you almost nothing.

A "pixel" is just one tiny square of color or brightness in a picture — a number (or a few numbers, one per color channel) sitting in a grid. If you zoom into any photo far enough, that's all you see: a grid of numbers. Now imagine a camera sensor that's slightly noisy (all real sensors are). That noise can bump any single pixel's value up or down by several levels for no meaningful reason at all — just electrical randomness in the sensor. So if you look at *one* pixel in isolation, you can't tell whether its brightness is "real" or just noise jitter.

What actually carries information is how a pixel relates to the pixels around it. A patch of sky is meaningful because a whole neighborhood of pixels is consistently blue-ish and smooth. An edge (like the boundary of a table) is meaningful because pixels on one side are consistently different from pixels on the other side. So the natural question becomes: **what is the simplest possible mathematical rule that makes a pixel's new value depend on its neighbors, instead of standing alone?**

The answer computer vision arrived at, decades ago, is beautifully simple: a *weighted sum*. Take a small grid of numbers — call it a "kernel" or a "filter" — and slide it across the image. At every position, multiply each neighboring pixel by the corresponding weight in the kernel, add all those products up, and that sum becomes the new value for the pixel in the center. That's it. That single mechanical recipe, repeated at every pixel, is called **filtering** (when we're being generic) or **convolution** (when we mean this specific recipe, with a small technical twist we'll get to).

Here's the everyday analogy: imagine you're grading a batch of exams, and instead of trusting any one student's raw score, you assign each student a "smoothed" score equal to a weighted average of their score and their neighbors' scores in the seating chart. If you weight everyone equally, you get something smooth and blurry (loud individual differences get averaged away) — this is **blurring**. If instead you weight in a way that highlights *how different* someone's score is from their neighbors, you exaggerate contrasts — this is **sharpening**. Every classical image filter is a variation on this same idea: choose the weights, and you choose what property of "differing from your neighbors" gets emphasized or erased.

Now, here's the part that should feel very familiar: you already know this operation from the other direction. In a convolutional neural network (CNN), a "conv layer" does exactly this weighted-neighborhood-sum operation — the only difference is that in a CNN, the weights (the kernel values) are *learned* from data via backpropagation, rather than *designed* by a human who understands the math of images. This section is about the "designed by hand" side of that same coin: what people figured out about which weights do what, before neural networks existed, and why. Understanding this tells you, very concretely, what a CNN's very first layer is spending its learning capacity rediscovering from scratch.

**Real-life anchor:** the soft, blurred background you get in "portrait mode" photos on a phone is a blur filter — but not a uniform one. The blur gets stronger the farther something is from the focus plane, and the phone figures out "how far" using a separately estimated depth map (often from a second camera, or a tiny learned neural network). So portrait mode is literally: a classical, hand-designed blur filter, whose strength at each pixel is controlled by the output of a machine learning model. Classical filtering and modern deep learning working together.

### The math

**First, let's carefully define correlation and convolution, side by side, so the difference is completely concrete.**

Picture the kernel as a small square grid of numbers, say $3\times3$ or $5\times5$, sitting on top of the image, centered on the pixel we're currently computing. We'll index positions within that kernel using two integers $i$ and $j$, both running from $-k$ to $k$ (so the kernel has side length $2k+1$; e.g. $k=1$ for a $3\times3$ kernel). $I(x,y)$ means "the image's brightness value at column $x$, row $y$." $w(i,j)$ means "the weight sitting at offset $(i,j)$ inside the kernel."

**Correlation** is the "obvious" version: line the kernel up on the image exactly as it's drawn, multiply each weight by the image value directly underneath it, and add everything up:

$$
(w \star I)(x,y) = \sum_{i=-k}^{k}\sum_{j=-k}^{k} w(i,j)\, I(x+i,\, y+j)
$$

Read the double sum as: "loop over every row offset $i$ and every column offset $j$ inside the small kernel window, and for each one, multiply the kernel's weight there by the image's pixel value at that shifted position, then add up all of those products." The result is a single number — the new value for pixel $(x,y)$.

**Convolution** does the same thing, but first *flips the kernel* — rotates it 180 degrees (equivalently, negates both offsets) — before sliding it over the image:

$$
(w * I)(x,y) = \sum_{i}\sum_{j} w(i,j)\, I(x-i,\, y-j)
$$

Notice the only difference from the formula above is that $I(x+i, y+j)$ became $I(x-i, y-j)$ — we're now looking at the pixel on the *opposite* side of center for each weight. That flip is the entire distinction between correlation and convolution. It sounds like a technicality because, for a lot of common kernels, it genuinely doesn't matter:

- If the kernel is **symmetric** (its value at offset $(i,j)$ equals its value at offset $(-i,-j)$ — think of a kernel that looks the same if you spin it 180°, like a Gaussian blob, a plain averaging box, or a Laplacian), then flipping it changes nothing, because you're just relabeling weights that were already mirror-images of each other. Correlation and convolution give identical results.
- If the kernel is **asymmetric** (not mirror-symmetric — for example, a kernel meant to detect "brightness increasing to the right," like the Sobel edge kernel we'll meet in 1.3), flipping it actually flips the *sign* of the result. Correlation and convolution genuinely disagree.

Here's a fact worth knowing purely so you can say it out loud in an interview and sound like you've actually read how this works: **the operation that CNN libraries call "convolution" is, mathematically, cross-correlation, not true convolution** — they skip the 180° flip. This is harmless in practice because a CNN *learns* its weights from scratch anyway; if the "correct" kernel for a task happened to need flipping, the network would just learn the already-flipped version instead, and the loss function can't tell the difference. But knowing the terminology is technically wrong, and *why* it doesn't matter, is exactly the kind of thing that distinguishes someone who has internalized the math from someone reciting a definition.

**Properties of convolution that make it powerful (and that you should be able to use, not just recite):**

- **Commutative**: $A * B = B * A$ — order doesn't matter.
- **Associative**: $(A * B) * C = A * (B * C)$ — you can group the operations however is convenient.
- **Distributive over addition**: $A * (B + C) = A*B + A*C$.
- **Shift-equivariant**: if you shift the input image sideways by some amount, the output shifts by exactly the same amount, with no other change. (This is *why* a CNN filter that learns to detect, say, a cat's ear in one part of an image will also detect it if the ear appears somewhere else — the same learned kernel just slides along.)

Why does associativity matter practically? Because it means if you're going to apply two filters back-to-back — first blur with kernel $G_1$, then blur with kernel $G_2$ — you can instead *pre-combine* $G_1$ and $G_2$ into a single kernel first, and apply that one combined kernel once:

$$
(G_1 * G_2) * I = G_1 * (G_2 * I)
$$

Both sides give the exact same final image. The left side says "combine the two kernels into one filter first, then run it over the image once." The right side says "run the image through the first filter, then run the result through the second filter." They're mathematically identical, but the left side can be much cheaper if the combined kernel is small, or if you're going to apply the same combination to many images (you only pay to combine the kernels once, ever).

**Linearity and shift-invariance — and why they let you use frequency math.** A filter is called "linear" if scaling or adding inputs scales or adds outputs correspondingly (this is the same notion of linear you know from linear algebra: $f(aX + bY) = a f(X) + b f(Y)$). It's called "shift-invariant" if, as mentioned above, sliding the input around doesn't change *what* the filter does, only *where* it does it. Ordinary convolution with a fixed kernel has both properties — we call such a system **LSI** (linear and shift-invariant).

Here's why that combination is a big deal: for an LSI system, its entire behavior — no matter how complicated the kernel looks — is completely captured by how it responds to a single idealized "impulse" (a hypothetical image that's zero everywhere except one pixel, which is 1). Once you know that one response, you know everything the filter will ever do to any image, because any image can be thought of as a sum of scaled, shifted impulses, and linearity + shift-invariance means the filter's response to that sum is just the sum of its responses to each impulse.

This connects to a completely different way of describing signals: the **Fourier domain**. Any image (or 1-D signal, like audio) can be decomposed into a sum of simple wave patterns of different frequencies — think of "frequency" here the same way you'd think of it for sound: a low musical note is a slow wave, a high-pitched note is a fast wave, and any complex sound is really many pure tones layered together. The Fourier transform, written $\mathcal{F}\{\cdot\}$, is the mathematical machine that takes a signal (or image) and tells you "how much of each frequency is present in this." The remarkable fact — which we won't derive here, just state, because it's one of the most important facts in all of signal processing — is:

$$
\mathcal{F}\{w*I\} = \mathcal{F}\{w\}\cdot\mathcal{F}\{I\}
$$

In words: convolving two things in the ordinary pixel/space domain is *exactly the same* as multiplying their frequency-domain representations together, frequency by frequency. This is huge for intuition: it means a filter's "true nature" is completely described by which frequencies it lets through and which it blocks or kills — exactly like how a bass boost or treble cut on a stereo works by turning some frequencies up and others down. We'll use this idea concretely in a moment when comparing Gaussian blur to box blur.

**Separability — the trick that makes big blurs computationally cheap.**

First, some quick linear-algebra refreshers, defined the moment we need them, since this genuinely is "basic linear algebra you've done before but might not have fresh."

A $k \times k$ kernel — a square grid of $k$ rows and $k$ columns of numbers — is nothing more than a $k \times k$ **matrix**, the same object you know from linear algebra.

The **rank** of a matrix, loosely, measures how many independent "directions of information" it contains. A matrix has **rank 1** if every one of its rows is just some scalar multiple of a single common row — meaning all the information in that whole grid of numbers is really just one row's worth of information, repeated with different overall scaling per row.

An **outer product** of two vectors $\mathbf{u}$ (a column of numbers, length $k$) and $\mathbf{v}$ (another column, length $k$) is exactly the way you build a rank-1 matrix: you form a $k \times k$ grid $W$ where entry $(i,j)$ is just $u_i$ times $v_j$ — i.e. $W = \mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^\top$ (the little $\top$ means "transpose," turning the column $\mathbf{v}$ into a row so the multiplication produces a full grid instead of a single number). Concretely: every entry in the grid is "this row's number from $\mathbf{u}$" times "this column's number from $\mathbf{v}$."

Here's why this matters enormously for cost. If your 2-D kernel $W$ happens to be exactly this kind of outer-product / rank-1 matrix, then plug $w(i,j) = u_i v_j$ into the double-sum convolution formula from earlier:

$$
\sum_i \sum_j w(i,j) I(x+i, y+j) = \sum_i \sum_j u_i v_j \, I(x+i,y+j) = \sum_i u_i \left(\sum_j v_j\, I(x+i, y+j)\right)
$$

Look at what just happened: because $u_i$ doesn't depend on $j$, we could pull it outside the inner sum over $j$. That means the *inner* sum — over $j$ — is just a 1-D convolution using the small vector $\mathbf{v}$, applied along one row at a time. Then the *outer* sum — over $i$ — is a second 1-D convolution using $\mathbf{u}$, this time run down columns, combining the row-results together. In symbols:

$$
W * I = \mathbf{u} * (\mathbf{v}^\top * I)
$$

In plain terms: instead of one expensive 2-D pass with a $k \times k$ grid, you do one cheap 1-D pass along rows with a length-$k$ vector, followed by one cheap 1-D pass down columns with another length-$k$ vector — and the two results are mathematically *identical*, with zero approximation or loss of accuracy.

Let's count the actual arithmetic savings, because "cheaper" should mean something concrete. A "multiply-add" (often written MAC, for multiply-accumulate) is one multiplication plus one addition — the basic unit of cost in this kind of computation. The naive 2-D approach needs one multiply-add per weight in the kernel, per pixel: that's $k \times k = k^2$ multiply-adds. The separable (1-D twice) approach needs $k$ multiply-adds for the row pass plus $k$ for the column pass: just $2k$. For a big blur kernel, say $k = 61$ (a $61\times61$ grid, which is a real, commonly-needed blur size): naive is $61^2 = 3721$ multiply-adds per pixel, while separable is $2 \times 61 = 122$ multiply-adds per pixel. That's roughly a **30× speedup**, for the exact same output, bit for bit (up to floating-point rounding). This is not an approximation trick — it's an algebraic identity.

**How do you know if a given kernel is separable (i.e., is secretly rank-1)?** The tool for this is called the **Singular Value Decomposition (SVD)** — a standard linear-algebra technique that takes *any* matrix and rewrites it as a sum of rank-1 pieces, each piece weighted by a number called a **singular value** (always non-negative, and the SVD lists them from largest to smallest). If a matrix is genuinely rank-1, the SVD will find that only the *very first* singular value, $\sigma_1$, is non-zero — every other piece has weight zero, meaning the matrix really is just that one outer product in disguise. When that's the case, you recover the two 1-D vectors by splitting the one non-zero singular value evenly (via its square root) between the two factors, so that neither one is arbitrarily larger than the other: $\mathbf{u} = \sqrt{\sigma_1}\,u_1$ and $\mathbf{v} = \sqrt{\sigma_1}\,v_1$, where $u_1$ and $v_1$ are the corresponding vectors the SVD hands you (each already scaled to length 1). If more than one singular value is non-zero, the kernel is *not* separable, and you cannot do the row-then-column trick without approximation.

Two concrete, important examples of separable kernels:

- **The 2-D Gaussian is separable**, and you can see exactly why just by looking at the exponent in its formula (which we'll write out fully in a moment): whenever a sum appears inside an exponent, that's the same as a *product* of two separate exponentials outside it — this is just the basic algebra rule $e^{a+b} = e^a \cdot e^b$. Since the Gaussian's exponent is $-(x^2+y^2)/2\sigma^2$, which is a sum of an $x$-only term and a $y$-only term, the whole thing splits:
$$e^{-(x^2+y^2)/2\sigma^2} = e^{-x^2/2\sigma^2}\cdot e^{-y^2/2\sigma^2}$$
One factor depends only on $x$, the other only on $y$ — which is exactly the definition of an outer product. So a big 2-D Gaussian blur can always be done as two small 1-D Gaussian blurs (one horizontal, one vertical), for free.
- **The Sobel edge-detection kernel** (which we'll cover properly in 1.3) is also separable: it factors as $[1,2,1]^\top [-1,0,1]$ — a column vector times a row vector.

**The Gaussian kernel, spelled out precisely.**

The Gaussian is the classic "bell curve" you've seen in statistics, just extended to two dimensions (across the image plane instead of along a single number line):

$$
G_\sigma(x,y) = \frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}\exp\!\left(-\frac{x^2+y^2}{2\sigma^2}\right)
$$

Here $(x,y)$ is the offset from the center of the kernel (so the center pixel is at $x=0,y=0$, where the exponent is zero and the value is at its peak). $\sigma$ (sigma) is the **standard deviation** — it controls how wide or narrow the bump is: small $\sigma$ means a tight peak that barely touches nearby pixels (a gentle blur), large $\sigma$ means a wide, spread-out bump that heavily mixes in far-away neighbors (a strong blur). $\exp(\cdot)$ is just the exponential function ($e$ raised to that power). The $\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^2}$ out front is a normalizing constant, chosen so that if you summed this function over the *entire, infinite* plane, you'd get exactly 1 — that keeps the total "weight" being distributed to neighbors equal to 1, i.e., it preserves overall image brightness on average.

A few very practical, need-to-remember facts about using Gaussians in code:

- You can't actually build an infinitely large kernel, so you **truncate** it — cut it off — at some distance from the center. A standard choice is $\pm 3\sigma$, because for a Gaussian, about 99.7% of the total area under the curve lies within 3 standard deviations of the center (this is the same "68-95-99.7 rule" from basic statistics, just applied to a filter kernel instead of a data distribution). That means a kernel of size $2\lceil 3\sigma \rceil + 1$ pixels wide (the $\lceil \cdot \rceil$ means "round up to the nearest whole pixel," and the $+1$ accounts for the center pixel itself) captures essentially all of the meaningful weight, with negligible error from throwing away the tiny tails beyond that. As a concrete data point, the popular vision library OpenCV, when you ask it to auto-pick a $\sigma$ for a given kernel size $k$, uses the relationship $\sigma \approx 0.3\left((k-1)/2 - 1\right) + 0.8$.
- Because you only kept a *finite* chunk of the theoretically-infinite bell curve, the weights you actually have won't sum to *exactly* 1 anymore (you dropped the tiny tails). So before using the kernel, you should **renormalize** it — divide every weight by the sum of all the weights, forcing the total back to exactly 1. If you skip this step, the filtered image will get systematically brighter or darker than the original, which is an easy, embarrassing bug to introduce.
- **The "cascade" or "semigroup" property.** ("Semigroup" is just a name from abstract algebra for "a set of things with a combining operation that's associative" — you don't need the formal definition, just the concrete fact below.) If you blur an image once with standard deviation $\sigma_1$, and then blur the *result* again with standard deviation $\sigma_2$, the combined effect is *exactly* the same as if you had blurred the original image just once, but with a new standard deviation equal to:
$$G_{\sigma_1} * G_{\sigma_2} = G_{\sqrt{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2}}$$

  **Pause:** blur an image with $\sigma=3$, then blur the result with $\sigma=4$. What single blur would have done the same job — $\sigma=7$?

  No — and this is the whole point of the identity above: $\sigma = \sqrt{3^2+4^2} = \sqrt{9+16} = \sqrt{25} = 5$. Not $7$. Here's the deep reason why, and it comes from probability, not image processing, so you already have all the tools to understand it: think of a Gaussian blur as physically nudging each point of light in the image by a random offset, where that offset is drawn from a Gaussian (bell-curve) probability distribution with standard deviation $\sigma$. Blurring once with $\sigma_1$ and then again with $\sigma_2$ is the same as applying *two independent random nudges in a row* — first one drawn from a $\sigma_1$-wide distribution, then a second one drawn from a $\sigma_2$-wide distribution, and adding the two nudges together. There's a standard fact from probability (which you've likely used in the context of noise or randomness in training): **when you add two independent random variables, their variances add** (variance is standard deviation squared, $\text{variance} = \sigma^2$). So the *variance* of the combined double-nudge is $\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2$, meaning the combined *standard deviation* is $\sqrt{\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2}$ — square root of the sum of squares, not a plain sum. In short: **variances add; standard deviations do not.** Blurring twice with the same $\sigma$ therefore equals blurring once with $\sigma\sqrt{2}$ (since $\sqrt{\sigma^2+\sigma^2} = \sigma\sqrt{2} \approx 1.41\sigma$), not with $2\sigma$ as naive intuition might suggest.

  This identity is also useful read backwards, as a recipe for building things incrementally instead of from scratch. Suppose you already have an image blurred with $\sigma_1$, and you want a *more* blurred version at $\sigma_2$ (where $\sigma_2 > \sigma_1$). Instead of throwing away your $\sigma_1$ result and re-blurring the original image with the full $\sigma_2$ (which needs a big, expensive kernel), you can just blur your *already-blurred* $\sigma_1$ image with a *small* additional amount, $\sqrt{\sigma_2^2 - \sigma_1^2}$ — much smaller and cheaper than $\sigma_2$ itself, especially when $\sigma_1$ and $\sigma_2$ are close together. **This is arguably the single most useful identity in this whole module** — it's exactly how "Gaussian pyramids" are built level by level without redoing expensive work at each level (covered in 1.4), and it's exactly how the SIFT algorithm (1.6) avoids repeatedly re-blurring all the way from the original sharp image every time it needs a slightly-more-blurred version.

**Viewing filters through frequency — what a blur really does, mathematically.**

We established earlier that convolving in the ordinary pixel domain is the same as multiplying in the frequency domain. That means: to understand what a filter *does* to an image, you can just look at what its own frequency-domain representation looks like — the filter's transform tells you exactly how much it turns up or down each frequency, the same way a graphic equalizer on a music player has a slider for each frequency band.

For the Gaussian, this transform happens to be another Gaussian (a rare, elegant mathematical coincidence — the Gaussian is "its own Fourier transform," up to some rescaling):
$$\mathcal{F}\{G_\sigma\} = e^{-2\pi^2\sigma^2 \|f\|^2}$$
Here $f$ represents frequency (how rapidly brightness oscillates across the image, in cycles per pixel), and $\|f\|$ is just its magnitude. Notice this function is: (1) always strictly positive — it never goes negative or crosses zero, no matter what frequency you plug in; (2) monotonically decreasing — the higher the frequency, the more it gets suppressed, smoothly, with no bumps or wiggles; (3) "no side lobes" — meaning it doesn't have any secondary humps further out that let some higher frequency sneak back through more strongly than a nearby lower one. In plain language: the Gaussian is a "clean" **low-pass filter** — a filter that lets low (slow, smooth) frequencies through mostly unchanged, and steadily crushes high (fast, detailed) frequencies down toward zero, with no weird artifacts along the way. It never flips the sign of any frequency component — it can only shrink them, never invert them.

You might reasonably guess that a **box blur** — the simplest possible blur, where every pixel in a square neighborhood just gets averaged together with equal weight — behaves basically the same way, just a bit rougher around the edges. It does not, and the difference matters a lot in practice. The frequency-domain transform of a box filter is a function called a **sinc function** (short for "sine cardinal," defined as $\text{sinc}(x) = \sin(x)/x$) — and crucially, a sinc function doesn't stay positive. It oscillates, crossing zero repeatedly and dipping *negative* in what are called its "side lobes" (the smaller ripples that continue out past the main central hump).

Why does a negative value in the frequency response actually matter? Because a negative gain at some frequency means that frequency component comes back out of the filter with its **sign flipped** — bright-to-dark patterns at that specific spatial frequency get inverted into dark-to-bright. Concretely, this means a box blur can literally invert the contrast of certain fine repeating textures (imagine a tight striped pattern that comes out of the blur with its stripes' brightness reversed), and the oscillating lobes create a visible rippling artifact near sharp edges, called **ringing** — faint repeated edges "echoing" outward from a real edge, purely as a mathematical side-effect of the filter, not because anything like that existed in the original image. So the honest answer to "why not just use a cheap box blur instead of a Gaussian?" is not merely "it looks a bit rougher" — it's that a box blur can actively **manufacture fake structure that was never in the image to begin with**. (Keep this specific fact in mind — in section 1.4, this exact same flaw reappears in a more formal guise, as a violation of something called the "scale-space causality axiom.")

**Non-linear filters — filters that are not just a weighted sum, and therefore cannot be written as a convolution at all.**

Everything above — the Fourier view, separability, the semigroup property — depended entirely on the filter being **linear** (a plain weighted sum). So why would anyone give that up? Because linearity is *also* precisely what forces a blur to smear indiscriminately across real edges in the image. A weighted sum has no way to ask a smarter question like "wait, is this neighboring pixel even part of the same object as me, or is it across an edge on a totally different surface?" It just blindly mixes everyone in the neighborhood together, edge or no edge. Every filter below buys the ability to ask that smarter question — but the price is giving up linearity, and with it, every convenient property discussed above (the Fourier-domain trick, the row/column separability trick, and the semigroup cascade property all stop working).

- **Median filter** — instead of averaging the neighborhood, sort all the neighboring pixel values and take the middle one (the "median," same statistical concept as a median income). This turns out to be the mathematically optimal choice for removing "salt-and-pepper noise" (random pixels that got corrupted to pure black or pure white, like static on an old TV) — because a single wildly-wrong pixel value has almost no effect on where the middle of a sorted list falls. Even better: a median filter preserves sharp step-edges *exactly*, because the median of a region straddling a clean step (say, half the neighborhood is dark, half is bright) is still cleanly dark or cleanly bright depending on which side has the majority — no blending happens. A Gaussian blur, by contrast, would smear that same step into a soft gradual ramp.

- **Bilateral filter** — this is the classic "edge-preserving blur." Its formula:
  $$
  I'(p) = \frac{1}{W_p}\sum_{q \in \Omega} \underbrace{G_{\sigma_s}(\|p-q\|)}_{\text{spatial}} \cdot \underbrace{G_{\sigma_r}(|I(p)-I(q)|)}_{\text{range}} \cdot I(q)
  $$
  Let's unpack every symbol. $p$ is the pixel we're currently updating; $q$ ranges over every neighboring pixel in some window $\Omega$ around $p$. $I(p)$ and $I(q)$ are the current brightness values at those two pixels. The formula computes a weighted average of all the neighbors $q$'s brightness values $I(q)$, and the weight given to each neighbor is a *product of two separate Gaussian terms* — think of each term as answering one yes/no-ish question about that neighbor:
  - $G_{\sigma_s}(\|p-q\|)$ — the **spatial** term — asks "how physically close is this neighbor to me, in the image grid?" ($\|p-q\|$ is just the ordinary distance between the two pixel positions.) This is exactly the same kind of Gaussian weighting an ordinary blur uses.
  - $G_{\sigma_r}(|I(p)-I(q)|)$ — the **range** term — asks a completely different question: "how similar is this neighbor's *brightness value* to mine?" ($|I(p)-I(q)|$ is just the absolute difference in brightness between the two pixels — not a spatial distance at all, a *value* distance.)

  A neighbor only gets a large overall weight if it answers "yes, close" to *both* questions — nearby in the image **and** similar in brightness. This is exactly the smarter question a plain linear filter can't ask: if a neighbor is spatially close but on the *other side* of a sharp edge (so its brightness is very different), the range term shrinks its weight down toward zero, effectively excluding it from the average — which is exactly what keeps the edge crisp instead of blurred across. $W_p$ is nothing exotic — it's just the sum of all the weights used, so dividing by it renormalizes everything back to an honest weighted average (same reason we renormalize the Gaussian kernel earlier, so we don't shift the image's overall brightness).

  Cost-wise, computed the naive way this is $O(k^2)$ per pixel (same as an ordinary 2-D convolution) — but crucially, it is **not separable**, because the range term depends on the actual image content at every position, not just on fixed geometric offsets, so the row/column trick from before doesn't apply. People have built clever accelerated versions (the "bilateral grid" and something called a "permutohedral lattice," which are beyond what we need here) to speed this up. **Known failure mode:** if you push $\sigma_r$ (the range parameter) too high, you can get **gradient reversal** (subtle unnatural inversions in smooth brightness gradients) and **cartoon-like banding** (the image starts looking like flat posterized regions with hard boundaries, instead of natural smooth shading).

- **Guided filter** — a faster, more modern relative of the bilateral filter. It runs in $O(1)$ time per pixel (meaning the cost per pixel doesn't grow with the filter's size, unlike the options above) by fitting a simple linear model that relates the output to a separate "guide" image (which can even be the input image itself). It's commonly used for tasks like image matting (cleanly separating a foreground subject from its background) and dehazing (removing atmospheric haze from photos), and often serves as a fast drop-in substitute for the bilateral filter.

**Boundary handling — what to do at the edges of the image, where the kernel "hangs off" the picture.**

Whenever the sliding kernel window reaches the border of the image, part of the window has no real pixels underneath it — there's nothing there. You have to decide what to pretend is there. Common choices: `zero` (pretend everything outside the image is pure black — this creates a visible dark halo artifact near the borders), `replicate` (repeat the value of the nearest real edge pixel outward), `reflect` (mirror the image back on itself at the border, like a reflection in a mirror), `reflect_101` (a slight variant of reflect that does *not* duplicate the very edge pixel — this happens to be OpenCV's default), and `wrap` (treat the image as if it tiles infinitely, wrapping around to the opposite edge, useful mainly for textures meant to repeat). The practical trap to remember: if you pick `zero` and then run edge-detection on the result (covered next, in 1.3), you'll get a bright fake "frame" of detected edges running around the entire border of your image — an artifact of your boundary choice, not a real edge in the picture. It's a small, easy mistake, and plenty of real shipped code still makes it.

### Connect it

Convolution is the computational substrate underneath edge detection (covered next, in 1.3) and underneath the idea of "scale" in images (1.4) — and it is literally the exact operation that Module 2 replaces with *learned* weights instead of hand-designed ones. The separability trick you just learned — splitting a big 2-D kernel into two cheap 1-D passes — reappears later as $1{\times}k$ and $k{\times}1$ "factorized" convolutions inside the Inception-v3 architecture, and again as "depthwise-separable" convolutions inside MobileNet (2.7) — the exact same rank-1 factorization argument you just worked through, just applied across the *channel* dimension of a feature map instead of across spatial rows and columns. The frequency-domain view you just learned also comes back later, when we discuss aliasing during image downsampling (1.4) and when we discuss why "stride" in CNNs has some of the same risks (2.3).

**In your own words:** why do two blurs of $\sigma=3$ and $\sigma=4$ compose into $\sigma=5$ rather than $\sigma=7$?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

- **Why Gaussian rather than box, precisely:** the strong answer is not "it looks smoother" — it's that the Gaussian is (a) separable (cheap to compute exactly), (b) strictly positive everywhere in its frequency-domain representation, so it can never cause ringing or contrast inversion, (c) — as we'll prove properly in 1.4 — the *unique* kind of kernel that satisfies a specific mathematical wishlist called the "scale-space axioms" (roughly: blurring more should never invent brand-new fake structure that wasn't visible at a finer scale), and (d) "closed under composition" — the semigroup property — meaning you can build any larger blur cheaply and incrementally from smaller ones, with an exact, known formula.
- **Cost accounting, three ways:** a naive $k\times k$ convolution costs $O(k^2)$ per pixel (grows with the *square* of the kernel size); the separable version costs $O(2k)$ (grows only *linearly* with kernel size); and for very large kernels, there's a third approach using the FFT (Fast Fourier Transform, a fast algorithm for computing frequency-domain transforms) that costs roughly $O(\log N)$ per pixel once you've paid a fixed up-front cost, and only becomes worth it for very large kernels (roughly $k \gtrsim 30$ or so). Knowing where that crossover point roughly sits — not just that "FFT convolution exists" — is the kind of systems-level detail that signals real depth in an interview.
- **The bilateral filter is not a "projection."** (A projection, in the math sense, is an operation that, if you apply it a second time to its own output, gives you back exactly the same thing again — it "snaps" onto some well-behaved target and stays there.) The bilateral filter has no such property: applying it repeatedly does *not* smoothly converge toward some clean, well-behaved, flat-region image. In practice, "just apply the bilateral filter 5 times in a row" tends to produce ugly artifacts rather than a nicer result, precisely because there's no guaranteed stable target it's converging toward.

### ✅ Mastery check

You need to blur an image with $\sigma = 10$. Naively that requires a $61\times61$ kernel, which is $3721$ multiply-adds per pixel. Give **three** different ways to get the same result substantially cheaper, and state the exact cost of each. Then state which one you would use if you also need $\sigma = 2, 4, 6, 8$ of the same image.

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>
(1) <b>Separable</b>: since the Gaussian factors into a row-vector times a column-vector, do two 1-D passes of length 61 instead of one 2-D pass → 122 multiply-adds per pixel instead of 3721 (roughly 30× cheaper), and mathematically exact — no approximation at all.
(2) <b>Repeated box filter</b>: run a cheap box filter (which can be computed in constant time per pixel using running sums, a technique called an "integral image") 3-4 times in a row. By the Central Limit Theorem (repeatedly averaging things tends to produce something bell-curve-shaped, no matter what you started with), several box blurs stacked together closely approximate a true Gaussian blur. Cost is roughly 4-8 operations per pixel — cheaper than even the separable approach, but only an approximation, not exact. This is what many real-time graphics blurs actually use under the hood.
(3) <b>Downsample, blur small, upsample</b>: shrink the image down (say, to 1/4 its original width and height), apply a much smaller Gaussian blur there (around $\sigma=2.5$ at that reduced size), then enlarge the result back up. Because a strong blur destroys fine detail anyway, doing the blur at low resolution loses essentially nothing, and the total cost drops roughly 16× (shrinking both width and height by 4× each cuts total pixel count, and hence cost, by about $4\times4=16$). This works because Gaussian blurring and resizing approximately "commute" — doing them in either order gives nearly the same answer.
For the multi-$\sigma$ case ($\sigma = 2,4,6,8$): use the <b>semigroup property</b> and build each one incrementally from the previous, rather than computing each from the original image separately. Get $\sigma=2$ first; then to reach $\sigma=4$, convolve the $\sigma=2$ result with an additional small blur of $\sigma=\sqrt{4^2-2^2}=\sqrt{12}\approx3.46$; continue the same way for 6 and 8. Because each additional step only needs a small extra kernel, the total cost across all four blurs ends up far below computing each one from scratch at full size. This incremental-build idea is exactly how Gaussian pyramids are constructed in section 1.4.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Implement a function `my_conv2d(img, kernel, border)` that performs 2-D convolution by hand, supporting all four boundary modes described above (`zero`, `replicate`, `reflect`, `wrap`). Then implement a function `separable_check(K)` that uses SVD to test whether a given kernel `K` is separable, returning the two 1-D vectors $(\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{v})$ if it is, or `None` if it isn't. Then empirically verify the "repeated box blur approximates a Gaussian" claim from the mastery check by plotting the 1-D brightness profiles after 1, 2, 3, and 4 successive box-blur passes and watching the shape approach a bell curve. Finally, time all three approaches from the mastery check on a real image and compare.

**Read:** Szeliski, *Computer Vision: Algorithms and Applications*, §3.2 (linear filtering) and §3.3.1 (bilateral filtering) — this is the free textbook referenced throughout this course. Then, once that's comfortable, the paper "A Fast Approximation of the Bilateral Filter using a Signal Processing Approach" or the original "Fast Bilateral Filtering for the Display of High-Dynamic-Range Images" (Paris & Durand, ECCV 2006) for the "bilateral grid" acceleration trick mentioned above.

---

## 1.3 Edge Detection (Gradients, Sobel, Canny)

### Intuition

Let's start from the very beginning: what actually is an image, to a computer? It's just a big grid of numbers. If it's grayscale, each little square (a "pixel," short for "picture element") holds one number — how bright that tiny spot is. Black might be 0, white might be 255, and everything in between is some shade of gray. There is no built-in idea of "this is a table" or "this is a wall" anywhere in that grid. The computer just sees numbers sitting next to other numbers.

So when we say we want to find "edges" — the outlines of objects, like the border between a table and the wall behind it — we have a real problem. The computer doesn't know what a table or a wall is. All it can do is look at the numbers. So the question becomes: **is there some pattern in the numbers themselves that tends to show up at the boundary between two objects?**
