We just derived this relationship by fixing $\sigma$ and asking "for what $R$ is the response extremal?" But the real detector does the reverse: it fixes the object (with some fixed true radius $R$) and asks "for what $\sigma$ is the response extremal?" It turns out these give you *the same underlying curve, just read in the opposite direction* — because, as we noted with the covariance property above, the normalized response only ever depends on $R$ and $\sigma$ through their *ratio* $R/\sigma$ (if you scale both $R$ and $\sigma$ up by the same factor $s$, absolutely nothing about the shape of the response changes — that's exactly what "covariance" meant). Since the relationship is purely about the ratio between the two, there's really only one stationary (extremum) condition linking them, and it doesn't matter which one you treat as fixed and which one you vary — you land on the same equation, $R = \sqrt{2}\sigma$, either way.

(As a bonus fact, worth knowing but not deriving here: in $n$ dimensions more generally, this relationship becomes $\sigma = R/\sqrt{n}$ — the $n$ shows up because the Laplacian, in $n$ dimensions, is a sum of $n$ separate second-derivative terms, one along each dimension. In the simplest possible case — a single 1-D step edge or ridge, i.e. $n=1$ — this reduces to just $\sigma = R$.)

**Putting it together: the detected $\sigma$ directly tells you the object's size.** A blob that the detector flags at scale $\sigma$ has a real radius of approximately $\sqrt{2}\,\sigma$. This is the exact formula you'd use to draw correctly-sized circles around detected blobs in a demo (and you'll actually implement and verify this yourself in the Build task below — seeing the circles land exactly on the true object boundaries is a genuinely satisfying "it actually works!" moment). This is also a favorite kind of interview question, precisely because it's easy to *memorize* "$R = \sqrt2 \sigma$" without understanding it — the real test of understanding is whether you can explain *why* the factor is $\sqrt2$ rather than, say, exactly 1 or exactly 2, by walking through the zero-crossing argument above.

### Pyramids: making all of this affordable to actually compute

Building the full, continuous scale-space we described above — every possible blur level, at full image resolution — is a beautiful idea but a hopelessly expensive one to compute directly. At full resolution, every single blur level costs a full-image convolution, and (annoyingly) it's the large-$\sigma$, heavily-blurred levels — the ones covering the biggest area of pixels — that cost the most to compute directly.

But here's an important source of waste we can exploit: an image that's been blurred with, say, $\sigma = 8$ pixels has no meaningful detail finer than roughly 8 pixels across anymore — all the fine texture got smoothed away. So storing that heavily-blurred image at the same full resolution as the original is keeping around a huge number of pixel samples that carry essentially no extra information (neighboring pixels barely differ from each other anymore, since they've all been smeared together). The natural fix: once an image has been blurred enough that fine detail is gone, shrink it down (reduce its resolution) to match how little detail is actually left. That act of "blur, then shrink," repeated over and over, is exactly what's called a **pyramid** (the name comes from picturing a stack of images getting smaller and smaller as you go up, like the layers of an actual pyramid).

**The Gaussian pyramid.** Build this by repeating two steps over and over: (a) blur the current image a bit with a Gaussian, then (b) **decimate** it — a technical word that just means "keep only every other pixel in each row and column," which shrinks the image down to half its width and half its height (a quarter of its total pixel count). It's tempting to think you could skip the blurring step and just throw away every other pixel directly — but that step is not optional, and the reason comes from a fundamental result called the **sampling theorem**, not from aesthetics or convention.

Here's the intuition for the sampling theorem's relevance: think of a striped pattern, like a brick wall with narrow mortar lines, photographed at just barely enough resolution to make out the individual bricks. If you now throw away every other pixel (shrinking the image to half resolution) without blurring first, that fine brick pattern is *too fine* to be represented properly at the new, lower resolution — there simply aren't enough remaining pixels left to faithfully draw those narrow lines anymore. Here's the key, counterintuitive part: that missing fine detail doesn't just quietly vanish — it **aliases**, meaning it gets mathematically "folded back" and reappears disguised as an entirely different, *low-frequency* pattern that was never actually present in the real scene. This is exactly the swirly, rainbow-like **moiré pattern** you may have noticed appears on photos of finely striped shirts, fences, or brick walls when they're resized carelessly. It's worth connecting this back to Axiom 5 (causality) from earlier in this section: aliasing is a way of sneaking in brand-new structure that wasn't really there — exactly the thing that axiom was designed to forbid, just showing up here through a different door (downsampling, rather than blurring itself).

Blurring the image *before* shrinking it removes exactly those too-fine, dangerous frequencies before they get the chance to fold back and cause trouble — this is called "anti-aliasing," and it's why the blur step is mandatory, not optional. The right amount of blur to use before shrinking by exactly a factor of 2 works out to roughly $\sigma \approx 0.8$–$1.0$ pixels (a classic, simple, and effective choice for this exact blur is the 5-number weighting pattern $[1,4,6,4,1]/16$, known as the Burt–Adelson kernel, named after the researchers who popularized this pyramid technique).

**The Laplacian pyramid.** Notice that a plain Gaussian pyramid, as just described, is a little wasteful in two directions at once: each level throws away fine detail (that's inherent to blurring), *and* neighboring levels of the pyramid end up sharing most of their content with each other (a slightly-blurred image and a very-slightly-more-blurred image look almost identical, just with the finest details missing from the second one). So here's a smarter idea: instead of storing *each entire level* of the pyramid, what if we only store *what's new* — the extra detail each level adds on top of the next coarser level?

That "extra detail" for level $i$ is computed as: $L_i = G_i - \text{expand}(G_{i+1})$ — meaning, take level $i$ of the Gaussian pyramid, and subtract from it the *next* (coarser, smaller) level $G_{i+1}$, first stretched back up ("expanded," using simple pixel-duplication or interpolation) to match level $i$'s resolution. Because you're subtracting a coarser version from a finer version, what's left over is exactly the range of detail frequencies present at level $i$ but missing at level $i+1$ — a so-called "band-pass residual," meaning it captures one specific band of detail sharpness, neither the very finest nor the very coarsest. You do this at every level except the very coarsest, tiny top level, which you keep in full (with nothing coarser to subtract it from) so you always have a starting point to rebuild everything back up from later.

You should notice something familiar here: this "subtract a coarser blurred image from a finer one" operation is *exactly* the same DoG subtraction from the derivation earlier in this section — except there, we used it to hunt for blobs (an extremum-finding tool), and here, we're using the very same subtraction to *represent* the image efficiently and compactly (a compression/representation tool). Same operation, two very different uses.

The genuinely useful property of a Laplacian pyramid is that it's **invertible** — meaning you can perfectly reconstruct the original image by simply adding all the levels back together in reverse order, with zero loss. This is exactly why it's the classic tool used for **multi-band blending** when stitching together panorama photos (a topic covered in section 1.9): when you're trying to seamlessly merge two overlapping photos into one panorama, you blend their broad, low-frequency content (overall brightness and color) over a wide transition zone, but blend their fine, high-frequency content (sharp texture and edges) only over a very narrow transition zone right at the seam — and doing this correctly, level by level, is exactly what makes stitched panoramas look seamless instead of showing an obvious visible seam or ghostly double-edges.

**SIFT's specific pyramid bookkeeping — every number here is *forced* by a real requirement, not arbitrary, so let's derive each one rather than just memorizing it.**

- An **octave** is defined as one full doubling of $\sigma$ (i.e., going from some blur level $\sigma_0$ up to $2\sigma_0$). This particular choice — using a doubling, rather than say adding a fixed amount — is natural because scale (size) is fundamentally a *multiplicative* quantity, not an *additive* one: saying "twice as big" is a meaningful, useful statement about size, whereas "10 pixels bigger" often isn't (10 pixels is enormous for a tiny object and negligible for a huge one). Because of this, we want to sample the $\sigma$-axis in equal *multiplicative* steps (each step multiplies $\sigma$ by the same ratio), not equal additive steps. If we decide we want $s$ such equal steps to fit within one full octave (one doubling), then $s$ copies of some fixed multiplying ratio $k$, applied one after another, must together equal exactly 2: $k^s = 2$, which means $k = 2^{1/s}$. SIFT's standard choice is $s=3$.
- **Why we need more blurred images than the number of scales we actually test.** To confidently call some point a genuine 3-D extremum (a peak or valley in $x$, $y$, *and* $\sigma$ all together), you have to compare it against its neighbors in every direction — including its neighbors one step *up* and one step *down* along the $\sigma$-axis. This means every DoG image you actually want to test needs a DoG image directly above it and a DoG image directly below it in the stack, purely to serve as comparison neighbors. But the very topmost and very bottommost DoG images in an octave have no such neighbor on one of their two sides (there's nothing above the top one, nothing below the bottom one) — so those two extreme images can never actually be tested themselves; they only exist to support the testing of their neighbors. So: if you want $s$ DoG images that can actually be properly tested, you need $s+2$ DoG images total per octave (the $s$ testable ones, plus one extra untestable image above and one extra untestable image below). And since every single DoG image is a subtraction between two *adjacent* blurred images, needing $n$ DoG images requires $n+1$ blurred images to produce them (think of it like fence posts and fence panels: $n$ panels need $n+1$ posts). So: $s+2$ DoG images require $(s+2)+1 = s+3$ blurred images per octave.
- Plugging in SIFT's choice of $s=3$: that's **6 blurred images per octave, producing 5 DoG images, of which only 3 are actually tested** for being extrema. Each tested point gets compared against all 26 of its neighbors: 8 neighbors in the same DoG image (the ring of pixels surrounding it), plus 9 neighbors in the DoG image directly above (the same pixel location, plus its 8 surrounding neighbors), plus another 9 in the DoG image directly below — together forming a complete $3\times3\times3$ cube of neighboring values, minus the center point itself (which is the candidate being tested).
- Here's a clever efficiency trick used to move from one octave to the next: the very last (most blurred) image within an octave has $\sigma = 2\sigma_0$ (twice the octave's starting blur). Instead of re-blurring the *original, full-resolution* image all over again from scratch to start the next octave, SIFT simply **takes that already-computed $2\sigma_0$ image and shrinks it down by half (downsamples it)** — and this shrunken image automatically becomes the correct starting point for the next octave, already at the right effective blur level $\sigma_0$! Why does this trick actually work correctly? Because when you shrink an image's resolution by half, any given blur amount, when re-measured in the new, smaller image's own pixel units, also automatically halves. So a $2\sigma_0$-blurred image, once you shrink it by half, effectively *becomes* a $\sigma_0$-blurred image in the new pixel grid — which is exactly the correct starting blur level the next octave is supposed to begin with. This trick is really the *semigroup property* from earlier in this section (property 3, "blurring composes step by step") doing genuinely useful work in practice: the entire pyramid ends up being built entirely out of small, cheap, incremental blur-and-shrink steps — never by repeatedly running one enormous, expensive blur directly on the full-resolution original image.
- One more subtlety worth knowing: SIFT assumes that whatever camera took the input photo has already applied a small amount of unavoidable natural blur, roughly $\sigma_{\text{camera}} \approx 0.5$ pixels (from lens imperfections, sensor characteristics, etc. — real-world images are never perfectly sharp down to the pixel), and SIFT wants its very first pyramid image to have blur $\sigma_0 = 1.6$. You might think you could simply blur the input directly with a Gaussian of width $1.6$ to get there — but you can't, because the image already *starts out* with some blur baked in from the camera, and blur amounts don't combine by simple addition. Instead, they combine "in quadrature" — meaning their *squared* values (their variances, i.e. their $\sigma^2$ values) add together, similar to how you'd combine independent random errors in statistics. So, to figure out how much *additional* blurring to apply, you must subtract the *existing* variance (not the existing $\sigma$ directly) from the *target* variance, then take a square root: the actual amount of additional blur SIFT applies works out to $\sqrt{1.6^2 - (2\times 0.5)^2}$ (the extra factor of 2 multiplying the camera's $0.5$ accounts for the fact that SIFT first doubles the image's resolution via upsampling before this step, which — following the same "shrinking a blur level" logic from the previous bullet point, just running in reverse — doubles the camera blur's effective size when measured in the new, larger pixel grid).

### Beyond linear scale-space (a quick, worth-knowing extension) 🟢

Everything above uses ordinary (Gaussian, "linear") blurring, and that has one real downside: Gaussian blurring smears brightness across *every* nearby structure equally, including *across* true edges in the image — so as you blur more, you gradually lose the ability to tell precisely *where* an edge really sits, even while you're gaining the ability to find bigger structures. This trade-off (better big-picture detection, worse fine localization) is inherent to the linear approach.

**Anisotropic diffusion** (an important extension due to Perona and Malik) is a fix for this. Instead of the plain heat/diffusion equation from earlier ($\partial L/\partial t = \nabla^2 L$, which spreads brightness equally in every direction, everywhere), it uses a modified version: $\partial L/\partial t = \operatorname{div}(c(\|\nabla L\|)\nabla L)$, where $c$ is a function that gets *smaller* wherever the local brightness gradient (how sharply brightness is changing, $\|\nabla L\|$) is *large* — meaning: **diffuse (blur) freely in flat, low-contrast regions, but slow down or stop diffusing right at strong edges**, so blurring flows *along* edges rather than smearing *across* them (imagine heat spreading easily through open water but being blocked by a thin wall — it flows around the wall rather than straight through it). This is the underlying technique behind a pair of feature detectors called **KAZE** and **AKAZE**, which use this edge-aware blurring instead of a plain Gaussian pyramid — and it's the reason AKAZE often noticeably outperforms detectors like ORB (mentioned in the next section, 1.6) specifically on blurry or low-texture images, where preserving edge locations really matters. You don't need to derive any of this in depth, but it's worth being able to say one clean sentence about it in an interview — it signals that you understand the *cost* of the plain Gaussian approach, not just its benefit.

### Connect it

- Section 1.3 left us stuck on the question "which $\sigma$ should I pick?" — this section's answer is: don't pick just one — build all of them, and let each individual structure in the image reveal its own best-fitting scale.
- Section 1.5 covers "Harris corners," a detector that (as you'll see there) does *not* automatically handle scale the way we've built here (it's "scale-variant," meaning its behavior isn't consistent as you zoom in or out). Combining Harris's precise spatial localization with this section's LoG-based scale selection gives a hybrid method called **Harris–Laplace**.
- Section 1.6 covers SIFT, which — now that you've read this section — you can understand as simply "find extrema of the DoG stack (exactly as derived above), then compute a descriptor (a numerical fingerprint) around each one." You now completely understand SIFT's entire first stage.
- **Looking ahead to Modules 2 and 4:** CNNs run into this exact same "objects come in different sizes" problem, and they solve it with a version of a pyramid that's *learned from data* rather than hand-designed — stacking layers of strided convolution (convolutions that also shrink the image as they go) builds up a hierarchy of features at different scales, layer by layer. And in Module 4, you'll meet **FPN (Feature Pyramid Network)**, a component of many modern object detectors, which explicitly brings back the Laplacian-pyramid idea from this section (a "top-down pathway" plus "lateral connections," which is really just "combine a coarse, deep layer with a finer, shallow layer," dressed in different terminology) — precisely because plain CNN feature hierarchies tend to lose fine spatial detail in their deepest layers. When you reach FPN, this section is what will make it click as an obvious, sensible idea instead of an arbitrary architectural choice someone invented.
- **Looking ahead to Module 6:** Vision Transformers (ViTs) initially throw away the whole idea of multiple scales — they chop the image into equal-sized patches and process everything at one single resolution. This is exactly why later, more advanced hierarchical ViT designs (like "Swin Transformers") reintroduce a pyramid-style, multi-scale structure on top of the basic ViT idea. The underlying problem this whole section addresses — "objects come in many sizes, and any single fixed processing scale will fail on some of them" — never fully goes away; it just keeps resurfacing in new architectures wearing new names.

**In your own words:** why does subtracting two blurred images give you (something proportional to) a Laplacian, and why doesn't the leftover $(k-1)$ factor matter?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

Five things that separate a genuinely strong answer from a merely adequate one:

1. **Be able to derive $\partial G/\partial\sigma = \sigma\nabla^2 G$, and get to "DoG $\approx (k-1)\sigma^2\nabla^2 G$" on the spot, from scratch.** Most people can only say "DoG approximates LoG" as a fact they memorized somewhere. Actually deriving it — and being able to explain *why* the fact that $(k-1)$ is the same constant at every scale is precisely what stops it from disturbing the extrema — is what separates a strong candidate.
2. **Explain clearly why the $\sigma^2$ normalization step is mandatory, not a nice extra.** Because raw derivative magnitudes shrink like $\sigma^{-n}$ purely due to the math of differentiating a wider kernel (regardless of image content), skipping this normalization ($\gamma$-normalization) means every single scale-space extremum would collapse down to the very finest scale available, making genuine scale selection completely impossible.
3. **Be able to state the uniqueness theorem and the causality axiom in your own words.** A sentence like "the Gaussian is the only blurring kernel that can never create brand-new local maxima or minima as you blur more" immediately signals real understanding.
4. **Know the specific pyramid bookkeeping cold** ($s+3$ blurred images needed for $s$ tested scales, $k=2^{1/s}$, and reusing the $2\sigma_0$ image — after shrinking it — as the starting point of the next octave). Knowing these concrete numbers, and *why* each one is forced, is the difference between having merely read about SIFT once versus having actually implemented it yourself.
5. **Know the blob-size relationship $R=\sqrt2\,\sigma$, and be able to explain where it comes from** (the LoG kernel's zero crossing), not just quote the formula.

**The trap question an interviewer might ask:** *"Is scale-space scale-invariant?"* The correct, careful answer is: **No.** The scale-space representation itself is only scale-**covariant** — meaning, if you scale the input image up or down, the detected $\sigma$ value scales proportionally along with it, but the raw scale-space representation by itself doesn't magically become "the same" for different sized versions of the same object. True scale-**invariance** (getting genuinely matching, comparable results regardless of an object's size in the image) only shows up once you go one step further: you **select** extrema jointly across position and scale, and then you **normalize** the region you extract around each keypoint by its own detected $\sigma$ before computing a descriptor from it (this second, normalization step is what you'll see in full detail when you get to SIFT in section 1.6). In short: the underlying representation is covariant; it's the complete detection-plus-normalization *pipeline* built on top of it that achieves true invariance. Candidates who blur this distinction together — treating "covariant" and "invariant" as interchangeable words — get caught out by this question.

### ✅ Mastery check

You are building a SIFT-like detector with $s = 4$ scales per octave, across 5 octaves, on a $1024\times1024$ image.

**(a)** How many Gaussian-blurred images, and how many DoG images, do you compute per octave — and explain exactly why those specific numbers, from the reasoning above (don't just plug into a memorized formula)?
**(b)** What is the value of $k$ (the fixed ratio between consecutive blur levels) in this setup?
**(c)** You detect an extremum in octave 2 (meaning the image has already been downsampled twice by the time you reach this octave) at scale index 1 within that octave, with the pyramid's base blur set to $\sigma_0 = 1.6$. What is the *effective* $\sigma$, measured back in the coordinates of the original, full-resolution image? And what real-world blob radius does that correspond to?
**(d)** Suppose a colleague removes the $\sigma^2$ normalization factor from the response calculation, to "simplify the code." Predict, in concrete detail, exactly what would break, and explain why, using the reasoning from this section.

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>

**(a)** $s+3 = 4+3 = 7$ blurred images per octave, producing $s+2 = 4+2 = 6$ DoG images, of which $s = 4$ are actually tested for extrema. Reasoning: testing a point as an extremum requires a DoG neighbor immediately above it and immediately below it along the $\sigma$-axis, which is why the very top and very bottom DoG images of an octave can never themselves be tested (they exist only to support their neighbors' tests). And since each DoG image comes from subtracting two adjacent blurred images, producing $n$ DoG images always requires $n+1$ blurred images (like fence posts and panels).

**(b)** $k = 2^{1/4} \approx 1.189$ — this is the fixed multiplicative step size that, applied 4 times in a row, exactly doubles $\sigma$ across one full octave.

**(c)** First find the blur level within the octave itself: $\sigma_{\text{oct}} = \sigma_0 \cdot k^{1} = 1.6 \times 1.189 \approx 1.902$ (using scale index 1, meaning one step of $k$ up from the octave's base). Being in octave 2 means the image has been downsampled by a total factor of $2^2 = 4$ compared to the original full-resolution image by the time we reach this octave — so, to express this blur amount back in terms of the *original* image's pixels, we multiply by that same factor: effective $\sigma$ in original-image pixels $= 1.902 \times 4 \approx 7.61$. Using the blob-size relation from this section, the corresponding real blob radius is approximately $\sqrt{2}\times 7.61 \approx 10.8$ pixels (measured in original-image pixels).

**(d)** Without the $\sigma^2$ normalization, every raw response magnitude decays proportionally to $\sigma^{-2}$ as $\sigma$ increases (this is exactly the mathematical shrinkage effect discussed above, and it is completely independent of what's actually in the image). This means the entire DoG response volume becomes monotonically decreasing as you move to larger $\sigma$ — in other words, larger scales will *always* look weaker than smaller scales, purely due to this artifact. As a result, every single scale-space extremum collapses down to whatever the very finest (smallest) scale available is, everywhere in the image, regardless of the true size of the actual structures present. Scale selection then returns essentially the same tiny $\sigma$ for every detected feature, and the detector has effectively lost all scale invariance — it degenerates into little more than a noisy detector that only ever finds fine-scale blobs. Practically, this means matching the same object across, say, a 2× zoom between two photos would drop to something close to random chance, since the detector can no longer correctly identify which scale each real object actually lives at.

</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Implement a Gaussian pyramid and a DoG pyramid completely from scratch, making sure your octave bookkeeping exactly matches the reasoning above (the "$s+3$ blurred images per octave" rule, and correctly reusing the $2\sigma_0$ image — after shrinking it by half — as the starting point of the next octave, rather than re-blurring from the original image every time). Then implement scale-normalized LoG blob detection on top of it: find local extrema in $(x, y, \sigma)$ across your pyramid, and **draw circles of radius $\sqrt{2}\,\sigma$** on top of a synthetic test image made of solid disks whose true radii you know exactly in advance. Check that your detected circle radii match the true, known radii to within a few percent — this is a genuinely satisfying test to pass, because it directly confirms you got the scale normalization right, not just the basic detection logic. As an extension, also implement a Laplacian pyramid, and verify that adding all its levels back together perfectly reconstructs the original image (the maximum absolute pixel error after reconstruction should come out very close to zero).

**Read:** Lindeberg, "Scale-space theory: A basic tool for analysing structures at different scales" (1994) — focus specifically on the sections covering $\gamma$-normalized derivatives and automatic scale selection, since those are exactly the parts this section built up from scratch. Then read Lowe's original SIFT paper (IJCV, 2004), section 3, which is essentially the fully applied, engineering version of everything derived in this section. Optional but excellent: Shree Nayar's "First Principles of Computer Vision" video episodes covering scale-space, for another angle on the same intuition.

---

## 1.5 Harris Corners

### Intuition

Let's start with the actual question we're trying to answer, because it's easy to lose it under the math.

Imagine you have two photos of the same scene taken from slightly different angles (like two frames of a panorama, or two views a robot's camera sees a second apart). You want to find points in photo A that you can *reliably re-find* in photo B. Which points make good "landmarks"?

Your first guess might be "bright points" or "points with lots of contrast." That's wrong, and it's worth seeing exactly why, because the reasoning is the whole lesson in miniature.

Here's the test that actually works. Take a small square patch of the image — literally just crop out, say, a 7x7-pixel window — centered on some point. Now slide that little window around a tiny bit, in every direction, and each time compare what's under the window now to what was under it originally. Ask: **does the patch under the window change when I nudge it?**

Try this mentally in three kinds of places in a photo:

1. **A flat, plain wall.** Every pixel in that region is roughly the same color. If you slide your little window left, right, up, or down by a pixel or two, the patch still looks like... the same flat wall. Nothing changed. This means if I handed you just this patch and said "find this exact spot in the photo," you'd have no way to know where it came from — it matches everywhere on the wall equally well. Zero useful information.

2. **A point on a straight edge** (say, the border between a wall and the sky). If you slide the window *along* the edge (parallel to it), the patch still looks the same — wall on one side, sky on the other, unchanged. But if you slide the window *across* the edge (perpendicular to it), the patch changes a lot — suddenly there's more sky and less wall, or vice versa. So an edge point gives you partial information: it tells you *which line* you're on, but not *where along the line* you are. This is exactly the situation a straight line of curb, a table edge, or a horizon presents in real photos.

3. **A corner** (imagine the corner of a window frame, or a checkerboard corner). Now slide the window in *any* direction — up, down, left, right, diagonally — and the patch changes noticeably every single time. There is no direction you can nudge the window in and have it look the same. That means a corner pins down your position in *both* directions at once. If you find this exact patch again in another photo, you know precisely where the corresponding point is.

So the real definition of "a good point to track" is: **a point whose surrounding patch looks different no matter which direction you slide it.** That's it. That's the entire idea. Everything below is just turning "looks different when I slide it" into a number we can compute for every pixel in an image, cheaply, so a computer can scan a whole photo and find these corner points automatically.

**A real-world reason this matters:** this is exactly why camera calibration (a topic we'll hit in section 5.3) uses a checkerboard pattern as its target. A checkerboard is *made of* corners — dozens of them, precisely and predictably arranged — because corners are the most exactly locatable structures you can put in front of a camera. If you used a plain gray card instead, the calibration software would have no distinctive points to lock onto at all.

### The math

Our whole job now is to take the sentence "the patch looks different when I slide it" and turn it into an actual formula, then simplify that formula until it becomes something a computer can compute in a handful of operations, for every pixel, on a full image.

**Step 0 — write down "how different" as a number.**

Let's define some notation first, plainly:
- $I(x, y)$ just means "the brightness value of the image at pixel location $(x, y)$." That's all $I$ is — a function that, if you plug in a pixel coordinate, gives you back a number for how bright that pixel is.
- $w(x, y)$ is a "weighting function" — think of it as a mask laid over our little window that says how much each pixel inside the window should count. We'll come back to exactly what shape this should be later; for now just think of it as "1 for pixels inside our window, 0 outside," a simple on/off mask.
- $(u, v)$ is the small shift we're testing — "slide the window $u$ pixels sideways and $v$ pixels up/down."

Now, "how different does the patch look after I shift it by $(u,v)$" is naturally measured by: for every pixel in the window, take the difference between the shifted brightness and the original brightness, square that difference (so positive and negative differences don't cancel out, and big differences count extra), and add all those squared differences up, weighted by $w$. Written as a formula:

$$
E(u,v) = \sum_{x,y} w(x,y)\,\bigl[I(x+u, y+v) - I(x,y)\bigr]^2
$$

Don't be intimidated by the summation sign — it just means "add this quantity up over every pixel $(x,y)$ in our little window." $E(u,v)$ is a single number that tells us: "if I shift the window by $(u,v)$, how much does the content change, total?"

Notice immediately: $E(0,0) = 0$ always — shifting by nothing changes nothing, obviously. And our earlier intuitive question — "does this patch look different in every direction?" — has now become the precise mathematical question: **"is $E(u,v)$ large for every direction of $(u,v)$, even small ones?"** We've converted a fuzzy visual idea into a concrete function we could, in principle, compute.

The problem: computing $E(u,v)$ for every possible shift $(u,v)$, at every single pixel of a megapixel photo, is far too slow to ever run in practice. We need a shortcut — a way to summarize the *shape* of $E$ near the origin (near $u=0, v=0$) using just a few numbers, instead of computing the whole function. The standard mathematical tool for approximating the local shape of a function using a few numbers is called a **Taylor expansion**. If you haven't used one in a while: a Taylor expansion just says "near a point, most smooth functions can be well-approximated by a simple polynomial (a straight line, then a curve, etc.), and the more terms you keep, the better the approximation." We only need the simplest version here.

**Step 1 — approximate the image itself as locally flat (a linear approximation), not the error $E$ directly.**

For a *small* shift $(u,v)$, we can approximate the brightness at the shifted location using the brightness and the local slope (the gradient) at the original location:

$$
I(x+u, y+v) \approx I(x,y) + u\,I_x + v\,I_y
$$

Here $I_x$ and $I_y$ are the **image gradients** — plain-language: $I_x$ is "how fast the brightness is changing as you move one step in the $x$ (horizontal) direction," and $I_y$ is the same thing for the $y$ (vertical) direction, both measured at the point $(x,y)$. These are exactly the quantities that an edge-detection filter like Sobel (which you've already seen in section 1.3) computes for you — no new concept there, just reusing the horizontal and vertical brightness-slope at each pixel.

This approximation — "the brightness near here is roughly the brightness here, plus a correction based on the local slope times how far you moved" — is just the same idea as saying "if I know my current speed, I can guess my position a moment from now by extrapolating in a straight line." It's only accurate for *small* movements, which is exactly our situation, since we only care about tiny nudges of the window.

Plugging this into $E(u,v)$, the $I(x,y)$ terms cancel out (the difference $I(x+u,y+v) - I(x,y)$ becomes just $uI_x + vI_y$), leaving:

$$
E(u,v) \approx \sum_{x,y} w(x,y)\,(u I_x + v I_y)^2
$$

**Step 2 — notice something slightly surprising: this expression is quadratic (a squared/curved shape), even though we only used a first-order (straight-line) approximation of the image.**

This is worth pausing on because it can feel like a contradiction the first time you see it: we only expanded $I$ to *first order* (a straight-line approximation, no curvature). But the error $E$ involves *squaring* that linear expression, $(uI_x + vI_y)^2$, and squaring a straight-line expression produces a curved (quadratic) one — the same way $f(t) = t$ is a straight line, but $f(t)^2 = t^2$ is a parabola. That's the entire reason a first-order approximation of the image gives us a second-order (curved, bowl-shaped) approximation of the error function $E$.

Let's actually expand that square, term by term, the way you would with $(a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2$:

$$
(u I_x + v I_y)^2 = u^2 I_x^2 + 2uv\,I_x I_y + v^2 I_y^2
$$

Look at the structure here: every single term is built from multiplying $u$ or $v$ by $u$ or $v$ (i.e., $u \cdot u$, $u \cdot v$, or $v \cdot v$), with some image-derived coefficient attached. Any expression with exactly this shape — a sum of $u^2$, $uv$, and $v^2$ terms — can always be written as a "sandwich": a row vector, times a matrix, times a column vector. Concretely, if we want $\begin{bmatrix}u & v\end{bmatrix} M \begin{bmatrix}u\\v\end{bmatrix}$ to expand out to $au^2 + 2buv + cv^2$, then $M$ must have $a$ in the top-left slot, $c$ in the bottom-right slot, and $b$ split evenly between the two off-diagonal slots (so the matrix stays symmetric — same value above and below the diagonal). That's just bookkeeping, not a new idea. So we can write:

$$
E(u,v) \approx \begin{bmatrix}u & v\end{bmatrix} M \begin{bmatrix}u\\v\end{bmatrix}
$$

for some $2\times2$ matrix $M$ that we're about to pin down exactly. A quick side note on vocabulary: an expression of the form $\begin{bmatrix}u&v\end{bmatrix}M\begin{bmatrix}u\\v\end{bmatrix}$ is called a "quadratic form" — it's just a fancy name for "a curved bowl-shaped function built from a matrix," and it's the same kind of object you'd see if you expanded $x^2 + y^2$ or an ellipse equation.

**Step 3 — read off exactly what $M$ is.**

Here's the key move: the shift amounts $(u,v)$ don't depend on which pixel $(x,y)$ we're summing over — they're the same for every pixel in the window (we're testing one single shift of the whole window at once). That means we can pull $(u,v)$ completely out of the summation, and everything that's left over — everything that *does* depend on the pixel, namely $I_x$, $I_y$, and the weight $w$ — gets collected together into the matrix $M$:

$$
M = \sum_{x,y} w(x,y) \begin{bmatrix} I_x^2 & I_x I_y \\ I_x I_y & I_y^2 \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} \langle I_x^2\rangle & \langle I_x I_y\rangle \\ \langle I_x I_y\rangle & \langle I_y^2\rangle \end{bmatrix}
$$

(The angle-bracket notation $\langle \cdot \rangle$ here is just shorthand for "the weighted sum/average of this quantity over the whole window" — it saves us from writing the summation sign three more times.)

This matrix $M$ has a name you'll see everywhere in this field: the **structure tensor**, also called the **second-moment matrix**. Don't let the fancy names intimidate you — "tensor" here just means "a matrix that we build out of an average of products at each point," and it's called the "second moment" because it's built from *squares and products* of the gradients (statisticians call an average-of-squares a "second moment," the same word used for the variance of a distribution).

Here's the payoff, stated plainly: we started with $E(u,v)$, a function that in principle takes a different value for every possible shift direction $(u,v)$ — infinitely many numbers. We've now shown that, at least for small shifts, all of that information is completely captured by just **four numbers per pixel** (and since the matrix is symmetric — the top-right and bottom-left entries are always equal — really just three independent numbers: $\langle I_x^2\rangle$, $\langle I_y^2\rangle$, and $\langle I_x I_y\rangle$). That's an enormous compression, and it's what makes the whole method fast enough to run on every pixel of a real photo.

One more way to think about what $M$ *is*, which will matter later: at every pixel, you have a gradient vector $\nabla I = (I_x, I_y)$ — literally an arrow pointing in the direction of steepest brightness increase at that pixel, with a length telling you how steep it is. $M$ is the (weighted) average, over the window, of the outer product of this gradient vector with itself. If you haven't seen "outer product" before: it just means multiplying the vector by itself in a way that produces a matrix rather than a single number (as opposed to the ordinary "dot product," which multiplies two vectors into a single number). Practically, $M$ is a summary of **which directions the gradient arrows in this little window tend to point**. Are they scattered every which way? Do they all point roughly the same way? Are there gradients at all? Everything from here on is just reading that information back out of $M$.

**Step 4 — eigenvalues: what they are, and why they're exactly the right tool here.**

This is the step that trips people up if they've only seen eigenvalues as an abstract linear-algebra exercise, so let's build the geometric picture from scratch.

Take any symmetric matrix $M$ (ours qualifies — you can check $M$ is symmetric because $I_xI_y = I_yI_x$, so the off-diagonal entries match). A general fact from linear algebra is: any symmetric matrix has two special directions, called its **eigenvectors**, which are at right angles to each other (in 2D). What makes these directions special? If you take $M$ and use it to "transform" one of its own eigenvectors, the result points in *exactly the same direction* as the original vector — it only gets stretched or shrunk, never rotated. The amount it gets stretched or shrunk by is called the **eigenvalue** for that direction, usually written $\lambda$ (lambda).

So concretely: for our matrix $M$, there are two perpendicular directions $\mathbf{e}_1$ and $\mathbf{e}_2$ (the eigenvectors), and two numbers $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$ (the eigenvalues), such that moving along $\mathbf{e}_1$ or $\mathbf{e}_2$ is "aligned" with how $M$ naturally stretches space.

Why do we care about this here specifically? Because $M$ shows up sandwiched inside our error function $E(u,v) = \begin{bmatrix}u&v\end{bmatrix}M\begin{bmatrix}u\\v\end{bmatrix}$. If we choose our shift direction $(u,v)$ to literally *be* one of the eigenvectors $\mathbf{e}_i$, something clean happens: plugging $\mathbf{e}_i$ in for the shift gives $E = \lambda_i \|\mathbf{e}_i\|^2$ (where $\|\mathbf{e}_i\|$ is just the length of that eigenvector — a constant that doesn't matter for our purposes). In plain words: **$\lambda_i$ directly tells you how fast the patch-difference $E$ grows when you slide the window along direction $\mathbf{e}_i$.**

So now we have a completely concrete geometric meaning:
- The two eigenvectors $\mathbf{e}_1, \mathbf{e}_2$ are the directions of *fastest* and *slowest* change of the patch as you slide the window — they're always at right angles to each other.
- The two eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$ are numbers telling you *how fast* the change is in each of those directions. A big eigenvalue means "sliding this way changes the patch a lot, fast." A small (near-zero) eigenvalue means "sliding this way barely changes the patch at all."

There's also a nice picture if you draw the set of all points $(u,v)$ that give the *same* value of $E$ — say $E = c$ for some constant $c$. This traces out an ellipse, and that ellipse's two axes point exactly along $\mathbf{e}_1$ and $\mathbf{e}_2$, with the length of each axis proportional to $1/\sqrt{\lambda_i}$. Why an inverse relationship? Because if $\lambda_i$ is small (slow change in that direction), you have to slide *very far* in that direction before $E$ builds up to the value $c$ — so the ellipse is long and stretched out along that axis. If $\lambda_i$ is large (fast change), you only need to slide a tiny bit before $E$ reaches $c$ — so the ellipse is short and squashed along that axis. Long axis = uninformative direction; short axis = highly informative direction. This matches our intuition exactly: a small eigenvalue is a direction you can slide a long way without the patch telling you much, which is precisely an "ambiguous" direction like sliding along a flat wall or along an edge.

**Pause:** given that reading, what must the two eigenvalues look like at a point on a straight edge?

One eigenvalue large, the other close to zero. Think back to the edge example from the Intuition section: sliding *along* the edge barely changes the patch (that's the slow direction — small $\lambda$), while sliding *across* the edge changes it a lot (that's the fast direction — large $\lambda$). There's also a clean reason this happens inside the matrix itself: at every pixel along a straight edge, the gradient (the arrow pointing toward increasing brightness) points in essentially the *same* direction — straight across the edge, perpendicular to it. When you average a bunch of outer products of gradient vectors that all point the same way, you get a matrix that only "knows about" that one direction and has no information at all in the perpendicular direction — mathematically, this produces what's called a "rank-1" matrix, meaning it effectively only captures one dimension's worth of information, which is exactly what gives you one big eigenvalue and one that's essentially zero.

With that reasoning in hand, the whole table below isn't something to memorize — it just falls straight out of "what do the gradient arrows in the window look like":

| Eigenvalues | Structure |
|---|---|
| $\lambda_1 \approx \lambda_2 \approx 0$ | flat region (no gradient in any direction — nothing to change) |
| $\lambda_1 \gg \lambda_2 \approx 0$ | edge (gradient in one direction only) |
| $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$ both large | corner (gradients pointing in multiple directions — sliding any way changes the patch) |

**Turning this into a fast, practical test — the Harris response.**

We now know exactly what we want: a point is a good corner when *both* eigenvalues are large. The obvious way to check this is to actually compute the eigenvalues of $M$ at every pixel. The problem is that eigendecomposition (the process of finding a matrix's eigenvalues) is a somewhat expensive operation, and doing it once per pixel, for every pixel in a photo, was completely impractical on 1988-era hardware — and it's still wasteful today when we don't have to.

So Harris and Stephens (the researchers who invented this method in 1988) asked a clever question: **can we test "are both eigenvalues large" without ever actually computing the eigenvalues?**

It turns out yes, using two quantities that are trivial to compute directly from the matrix entries (just a few multiplications and additions, no eigendecomposition needed):
- The **determinant** of $M$, written $\det(M)$, happens to equal the *product* of the eigenvalues: $\det(M) = \lambda_1 \lambda_2$.
- The **trace** of $M$, written $\operatorname{tr}(M)$, happens to equal the *sum* of the eigenvalues: $\operatorname{tr}(M) = \lambda_1 + \lambda_2$. (The trace of any matrix, as a reminder, is just the sum of the entries on its main diagonal — trivial to compute.)

Between the product and the sum of two numbers, you actually have enough information to distinguish the cases we care about. $\det(M) = \lambda_1\lambda_2$ is already almost exactly the test we want: it comes out small whenever *either* eigenvalue is small (since anything times something-near-zero is near-zero), and it's only large when *both* eigenvalues are reasonably large. That's the corner-detecting behavior we're after.

But $\det(M)$ alone has a flaw: imagine a very strong edge where $\lambda_1$ is enormous and $\lambda_2$ is just slightly above zero rather than exactly zero — their product $\lambda_1\lambda_2$ can still come out fairly large purely because $\lambda_1$ is huge, even though this is clearly an edge, not a corner. We need to penalize this "lopsided" situation. That's what the second term does: $(\operatorname{tr}\,M)^2 = (\lambda_1+\lambda_2)^2$ grows *quadratically* with whichever eigenvalue is bigger, while $\det(M)$ only grows *linearly* in the bigger eigenvalue (since the other one is multiplied in as a small factor). So when one eigenvalue dominates, the penalty term $(\operatorname{tr}\,M)^2$ outpaces $\det(M)$, and subtracting it correctly pushes the overall score down for edges. This gives us the **Harris corner response**:

$$
R = \det(M) - \kappa\,\bigl(\operatorname{tr} M\bigr)^2 = \lambda_1\lambda_2 - \kappa(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)^2, \qquad \kappa \in [0.04, 0.06]
$$

Here $\kappa$ (kappa) is just a small tunable constant — a "sensitivity knob" that controls exactly how lopsided the eigenvalues are allowed to be before we call something an edge instead of a corner. Reading off the sign of $R$: if $R$ is positive and large, we're at a corner (both eigenvalues sizeable and roughly balanced). If $R$ is negative, we're at an edge (the penalty term wins because one eigenvalue is much bigger than the other). If $|R|$ (the size of $R$ ignoring sign) is small, we're in a flat region (both terms are tiny because both eigenvalues are near zero).

Once you've computed $R$ at every pixel, you keep only the pixels where $R$ is above some threshold, and then apply **non-maximum suppression** — meaning, in any small neighborhood where several nearby pixels all score high, keep only the single highest-scoring one, so you don't end up with a dozen "corner" detections clustered on top of what is really just one corner.

It's worth being honest about $\kappa$: there's no principled derivation for why it should be exactly 0.04–0.06 — it's an empirically-chosen knob, and its arbitrariness is precisely the flaw that the alternative formulas below were invented to remove.

**Variants (other ways to combine $\lambda_1$ and $\lambda_2$ into one score):**
- **Shi–Tomasi (1994, "Good Features to Track")**: $R = \min(\lambda_1, \lambda_2)$ — literally just "take whichever eigenvalue is smaller." This requires you to actually compute the eigenvalues (a bit more expensive than Harris's trick), but in exchange you get a score with no arbitrary $\kappa$ knob, and it behaves more sensibly right at the boundary between "edge" and "corner." This is the exact method implemented by OpenCV's `cv2.goodFeaturesToTrack` function.
- **Noble / harmonic mean**: $R = \dfrac{\det M}{\operatorname{tr} M + \epsilon}$ (where $\epsilon$ is just a tiny constant added to avoid dividing by zero) — this version has the nice property that its scale behaves consistently, and again there's no $\kappa$ to tune.

**Why the window $w$ has to be Gaussian-shaped, not a plain box.**

It's tempting to think the window's exact shape doesn't matter much — it's "just" saying which nearby pixels get included in the average. But consider what a plain square (box) window actually *is*, geometrically: it's a shape with corners of its own, and with edges that are aligned to the horizontal and vertical axes. That means it has "preferred directions" baked into its own shape.

Here's the problem this causes. Suppose you take a photo, compute Harris corners on it, then rotate the whole photo by 45 degrees and compute Harris corners again. You'd want the *same* real-world corners to be detected both times (rotating the photo shouldn't change what counts as a corner in the scene). But if your window is a square, then after rotating the image by 45°, that square window now overlaps a *different* set of underlying pixels relative to the scene's structure than it did before — the corners of the square window "catch" different amounts of the surrounding structure depending on the image's orientation relative to the window's own axes. That means $M$ comes out different after rotation, purely due to the window shape, not due to any real change in the scene — and so $R$ changes too. A box-windowed Harris detector is therefore **not rotation-invariant**: it can fire differently on a rotated copy of the exact same scene, which defeats the whole purpose of finding *reliable*, re-findable points.

A **Gaussian window** — a weighting that's shaped like a smooth, circular bell curve, heaviest at the center and fading out symmetrically in every direction — has no preferred direction at all; it looks exactly the same from every angle (this property is called "radially symmetric" or "isotropic," meaning "the same in all directions"). Because of that symmetry, using a Gaussian window makes the whole Harris computation rotation-invariant. This is a subtle point that's easy to skip over when you're just reading about the method, but it's the kind of detail that shows up the moment you actually implement it and test it on a rotated image.

**Invariance audit — this table is worth genuinely understanding, because the same style of reasoning gets applied to every feature detector you'll meet later in this course.**

The method for filling in a row like this is always mechanical: take the transformation (rotate the image, brighten it, etc.), work out exactly what it does to the gradients and hence to $M$, and then check whether $R$'s *behavior* (specifically, whether it correctly ranks corners as corners) survives.

The most subtle row is intensity scaling, so let's actually work through it instead of asserting the answer. Suppose every pixel's brightness gets multiplied by some constant $a$ (imagine changing the camera's exposure) — so the image goes from $I$ to $aI$. Since the gradient is essentially a measure of *how much brightness changes over a small distance*, scaling every brightness value by $a$ scales every gradient by $a$ too: $I_x \to aI_x$ and $I_y \to aI_y$. Now look back at how $M$ is built — every entry of $M$ is a product of two gradient values (things like $I_x^2$, $I_xI_y$, $I_y^2$), so multiplying each gradient by $a$ multiplies each entry of $M$ by $a \times a = a^2$. That means $M \to a^2 M$. Now, $\det(M)$ for a $2\times 2$ matrix scaled by a constant $c$ scales by $c^2$ (since determinant is a product of two eigenvalues, each of which picked up a factor of $c$) — so $\det(M) \to (a^2)^2 \det(M) = a^4 \det(M)$. Similarly $(\operatorname{tr}\,M)^2$ also scales by $a^4$. So the entire response scales as $R \to a^4 R$. The key insight: every point's score gets multiplied by the *same* factor $a^4$, so the *relative ranking* of which points are the strongest corners is completely unchanged — but any *absolute* numeric threshold you'd set in advance (like "keep everything with $R > 1000$") would now be wrong, because a darker or brighter version of the same image produces completely different absolute $R$ values. That's why the table below says intensity scaling is only "invariant up to the threshold": correct if you rank/threshold relatively, broken if you use a fixed absolute cutoff.

| Transform | Harris invariant? | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Rotation | ✅ | Eigenvalues of $M$ are rotation-invariant (the ellipse rotates along with the image, but its axis *lengths* — which is what the eigenvalues measure — don't change) |
| Intensity shift $I + b$ (adding a constant brightness offset) | ✅ | Gradients (derivatives) measure *change*, and adding a flat constant everywhere causes no change, so it has zero effect on $I_x$ or $I_y$ |
| Intensity scale $aI$ (multiplying brightness by a constant) | ⚠️ only up to the threshold | $M \to a^2 M$, so $R \to a^4 R$ — the ranking between points is preserved, but a fixed absolute threshold is not |
| Translation (sliding the whole image) | ✅ | Everything in this method is computed locally, from nearby pixels only, so shifting the whole image just shifts where the answer appears, without changing it |
| **Scale (zooming in or out)** | ❌ | A sharp corner viewed up close can look like a smooth, gentle edge once you zoom out (blur it and shrink it) enough — the window that worked at one scale stops working at another |
| Affine / viewpoint changes (viewing the same scene from an angled camera position) | ❌ | The ellipse that represents the gradient distribution gets stretched and skewed unevenly by a change of viewpoint, in a way this basic method doesn't account for |

**Fixing the scale problem — Harris–Laplace.**

Since section 1.4 already introduced scale-space (the idea of blurring an image at many different amounts and searching across all of them, which you should recall as the general fix for "this feature only shows up correctly at one specific zoom level"), the natural fix here is to combine it with Harris. Concretely: compute the Harris response across a whole stack of increasingly blurred versions of the image (a scale-space, exactly like in 1.4), and keep only the points that satisfy *two* conditions simultaneously — (a) the point is a local maximum of the Harris response *within its own blur level* (meaning: among nearby pixels at this same amount of blur, this one scores highest — this is what pins down *where* the corner is), and (b) at that same location, the response is also an extremum (a peak or trough) of something called the scale-normalized Laplacian-of-Gaussian as you scan across different blur amounts (this is what pins down *which* amount of blur/scale is the "right" one for this particular corner — you may recognize this LoG-across-scale idea from section 1.4 as the exact same trick used there for finding the right scale of a blob). In short: Harris finds *where* in the image, and the scale-space search finds *at what zoom level* — put together, you get a detector that survives zooming, which plain Harris alone does not.

**Fixing the viewpoint problem — Harris-Affine.**

For handling changes in camera viewing angle (not just zoom), there's an iterative fix: compute the second-moment matrix $M$ at a point, then use it to "un-warp" the patch — specifically, warp the patch by $M^{-1/2}$ (the inverse square root of the matrix; you don't need to derive this, just know it's a specific mathematical operation that reshapes the patch) so that the gradients inside it become evenly distributed in every direction again (i.e., make the previously skewed ellipse round again). Then recompute $M$ on this reshaped patch, and repeat the whole process until it stops changing. This process effectively cancels out — "normalizes away" — the distortion that a tilted viewing angle introduces, leaving you with a patch shape that's consistent regardless of viewing angle (other methods that achieve a similar goal include MSER and Hessian-Affine, which we won't detail here). This kind of viewpoint-robust matching becomes directly relevant once we get to wide-baseline stereo and 3D vision topics in Module 5.

**Getting more precise than one whole pixel — sub-pixel refinement.**

Corners detected so far are only located to the nearest whole pixel, but for tasks like camera calibration (section 5.3), you often need location accuracy far finer than one pixel. One way to get this: fit a small 2-D curved (quadratic) surface to the $R$ values in the $3\times3$ block of pixels around the detected maximum, and solve for exactly where that curve's true peak sits (which will generally fall *between* pixel centers, giving you a fractional, "sub-pixel" coordinate). OpenCV implements a version of this refinement in the function `cv2.cornerSubPix`. Calibration accuracy in section 5.3 depends heavily on getting this refinement step right.

### Connect it

Here's a genuinely important link to hold onto: **the exact same matrix $M$ that we built here reappears, unchanged, in optical flow** (a topic we'll cover in section 5.7 — optical flow is about estimating how points move between consecutive video frames).

The classic Lucas–Kanade optical flow method needs to solve an equation of the form $M\mathbf{v} = -\mathbf{b}$ for the unknown motion vector $\mathbf{v}$ (how far and in what direction a point moved between two frames), where $M$ is precisely this same structure tensor. Solving that equation for a unique, reliable answer only works when $M$ is "well-conditioned" — plain-language, when $M$ doesn't have any near-zero eigenvalue that would make the solution wildly unstable or ambiguous. And "no near-zero eigenvalue" is *exactly* our corner condition from above! This is actually why the paper that introduced the Shi–Tomasi variant is titled "Good Features to *Track*" — Shi and Tomasi arrived at their corner-detection criterion by directly asking "which points can optical flow reliably track?", rather than starting from an abstract idea of "cornerness" and hoping it happened to be useful.

This also gives us precise vocabulary for something you may have already heard of informally: **the "aperture problem"** — the well-known issue in motion estimation where, if you only look at a small local patch, you often can't tell which way something is truly moving along an edge (only the motion component perpendicular to the edge is visible; motion parallel to the edge is invisible through a small "aperture," hence the name). In our language, the aperture problem is exactly the statement that $M$ is rank-1 (has one near-zero eigenvalue) at an edge. Being able to say, plainly and precisely, "Harris cornerness and Lucas–Kanade trackability are the exact same eigenvalue condition, viewed from two different applications" is a genuinely high-value thing to understand — it's the kind of connection that shows real understanding rather than memorized facts.

$M$ also shows up again later as a "coherency" measure in texture-analysis techniques that use structure tensors, and in SIFT's step for rejecting edge-like keypoints (which we'll cover in section 1.6) — that step uses the ratio of principal curvatures of something called the Hessian matrix, which is a different matrix but plays an entirely analogous role: distinguishing "changes strongly in every direction" (good) from "changes strongly in only one direction" (an edge, to be rejected).

**In your own words:** what does each eigenvalue of $M$ physically measure, and why does "both large" mean corner?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

1. **Be able to actually derive $M$ from the Taylor expansion, step by step** — don't just state the final formula from memory. The fact that $E(u,v)$ turns out to be a quadratic form (a curved bowl), and that its level sets ($E = c$) are ellipses whose axes are the eigenvectors, is the entire geometric picture this whole topic rests on. If you can walk through Steps 1–4 above from scratch, you understand this properly.
2. **Explain exactly why the $\kappa$ term exists**: know that it's a workaround for avoiding a full eigendecomposition — $\det(M)$ and $\operatorname{tr}(M)$ are the two quantities you can get with just a handful of multiplications, and combining them is a clever proxy for "are both eigenvalues large." Then be ready to note that Shi–Tomasi's $\min(\lambda_1, \lambda_2)$ behaves more cleanly at the edge/corner boundary, at the cost of actually needing the eigenvalues.
3. **Know explicitly that plain Harris is *not* scale-invariant**, and know the fix by name (Harris–Laplace). This is a very common mistake — many people incorrectly assume Harris has the same scale-robustness as SIFT (covered next, in 1.6). It does not, unless you explicitly add the scale-space search on top.
4. **Understand the Gaussian-vs-box-window rotation-invariance argument** well enough to explain it from first principles, not just recite "Gaussian windows are used because they're better."
5. **Know the Lucas–Kanade connection cold.** Being able to state, unprompted, that the Harris corner condition and the Lucas–Kanade trackability condition are literally the same eigenvalue statement is the single detail most likely to visibly impress an interviewer on this topic.

### ✅ Mastery check

You compute $M$ over a window and find $\lambda_1 = 4000$, $\lambda_2 = 3$.

**(a)** Classify the point and compute $R$ with $\kappa = 0.05$. What sign do you get, and does the sign alone tell you the right answer?
**(b)** Now the image is multiplied by 0.5 (half exposure). What are the new eigenvalues and the new $R$? What breaks?
**(c)** You feed this point to Lucas–Kanade optical flow. What happens to the estimated motion vector, and which component of it is reliable?

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>
(a) Since $\lambda_1$ is far bigger than $\lambda_2$ (4000 versus 3), this is an <b>edge</b>, not a corner — one direction of fast change, one of essentially none. Computing $R$: $R = (4000)(3) - 0.05(4000+3)^2 = 12000 - 0.05(4003)^2 = 12000 - 0.05(16{,}024{,}009) \approx 12000 - 801{,}200 = -789{,}200$. That's negative, which correctly flags this point as an edge — so yes, in this specific case the sign alone was diagnostic (negative $R$ reliably means edge). But this isn't true in general: for a genuinely flat region, $R$ would come out with a very small magnitude near zero rather than a clearly negative number, so relying on sign alone isn't safe everywhere — you generally need to look at the size of $R$ too, not just whether it's positive or negative.
(b) Halving every pixel's brightness means $I \to 0.5I$, so every gradient also gets halved, and every entry of $M$ (built from products of two gradients) gets multiplied by $0.5 \times 0.5 = 0.25$. So $M \to 0.25M$, meaning each eigenvalue also scales by 0.25: $\lambda_1 = 0.25 \times 4000 = 1000$, and $\lambda_2 = 0.25 \times 3 = 0.75$. Since $R$ scales as the fourth power of the brightness-scaling factor $a$ (we showed $R \to a^4R$ above, and here $a = 0.5$), the new $R = (0.5)^4 \times (-789{,}200) = 0.0625 \times (-789{,}200) \approx -49{,}325$. What breaks: the relative <i>ranking</i> of which points are the strongest edges/corners in the image is completely unaffected (everything just got scaled down by the same factor), but any fixed <b>absolute</b> threshold you'd chosen in advance is now wrong by a factor of 16 — meaning a detector tuned for full-brightness images will silently find far fewer corners in a dimmer image, purely due to exposure, not because the scene actually changed. The fix is to threshold relative to the maximum $R$ found in that particular image, or to normalize image brightness before running the detector.
(c) Because $M$ is nearly rank-1 here (one eigenvalue is essentially zero), this is exactly the <b>aperture problem</b> described above. Only the component of motion pointing <b>along the strong-gradient direction</b> (i.e., perpendicular to the edge, along the eigenvector $\mathbf{e}_1$ associated with the large eigenvalue $\lambda_1$) can actually be measured reliably — the component running parallel to the edge lands in the direction with near-zero eigenvalue, which is nearly unobservable, so Lucas-Kanade's estimate along that direction is essentially undetermined and dominated by noise (in fact the noise in that direction gets amplified roughly in proportion to $1/\lambda_2$, i.e., it explodes as $\lambda_2$ approaches zero). This is precisely why, in practice, Lucas–Kanade tracking is run specifically on Shi–Tomasi/Harris corners rather than on arbitrary edge points — corners are exactly the points where both directions are well-determined.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Implement Harris corner detection completely from scratch, as a pipeline: compute image gradients with Sobel filters (which you've already used in section 1.3) → compute the three per-pixel products $I_x^2$, $I_y^2$, $I_xI_y$ → apply Gaussian-weighted summation over a window at every pixel to build $M$ at every location → compute $R$ from $M$'s determinant and trace → threshold → apply non-maximum suppression → optionally refine to sub-pixel accuracy. Then run the **invariance experiment** described in the table above, and actually measure it rather than just trusting the theory: rotate a test image in 10° steps from 0° to 90°, and at each step measure the *repeatability rate* — the fraction of originally-detected corners that get re-detected within 2 pixels of where they should have landed after the rotation. Then repeat the same experiment, but scaling (zooming) the image from 1.0x up to 4.0x instead of rotating it. You should observe rotation repeatability staying around 85% or higher throughout, while scale repeatability collapses sharply once you pass roughly 1.5x zoom. Seeing that collapse happen on your own plot is far more convincing, and more memorable, than reading the claim in a paragraph like this one.

**Read:** Harris & Stephens (1988) — the original paper, only about 4 pages, very readable. Shi & Tomasi, "Good Features to Track" (CVPR 1994) — pay particular attention to their trackability-first argument (i.e., deriving the corner criterion from "can I track this with Lucas-Kanade?" rather than from an abstract notion of cornerness). Mikolajczyk & Schmid, "Scale & Affine Invariant Interest Point Detectors" (IJCV 2004) for the full details of Harris–Laplace and Harris-Affine.

---

## 1.6 Keypoints & Descriptors: SIFT, ORB 🟡

### Intuition

Let's set up the problem from the very beginning, with no assumed vocabulary.

Imagine you take two photos of the same building — one from the street, one from a rooftop across the road. The building didn't change. But every pixel value in the two photos is different, because the camera moved, the light is different, and the building looks bigger or smaller depending on distance. Now imagine you want a computer to figure out: "this corner of the window in photo A is the *same physical corner* as this corner of the window in photo B." That's the problem this whole section is about. It's called **finding correspondences** — matching up the same physical points across two different images of the same scene.

In the previous section (1.5, Harris corners) you learned how to find "interesting" points in a single image — points like corners, where the image changes sharply in more than one direction, so they're easy to pinpoint precisely (as opposed to, say, a point in the middle of a flat wall, which looks the same as every other point nearby). Harris corners answer the question "where should I look?" But Harris only tells you *where* interesting points are in one image. It says nothing about how to recognize that a point in image A is the same as a point in image B.

Here's the naive idea, and why it fails. You might think: just cut out a small square of pixels around each interesting point (a "patch"), and compare patches directly — pixel by pixel — between the two images. If they match closely, it's the same point. The problem is that the *same physical corner*, photographed from two different positions, produces patches that look nothing alike in raw pixel values:

- If you move the camera closer or farther away, the patch is a different **size** in pixels — this is called a change in **scale**. A corner that spans 10 pixels in one photo might span 40 pixels in the other.
- If you tilt or rotate the camera, the patch is **rotated** — the same corner might point "up-right" in one photo and "down-left" in another.
- If the lighting is different (cloudy vs. sunny, indoor vs. outdoor), the actual brightness numbers in the patch change even though the physical structure — where the edges are — didn't change.

So comparing raw pixels directly is hopeless: two patches of the *same* corner can look completely different numerically, purely because of scale, rotation, and lighting — three things about the *camera and environment*, not about the *scene itself*.

The solution splits the problem into two separate jobs, done by two separate tools:

1. A **detector** finds the interesting points — the *where*. (This is what Harris corners do, and it's what the first stage of the algorithms below does too.)
2. A **descriptor** takes a patch around each point and turns it into a list of numbers — a fixed-length "fingerprint" of that patch — designed so that the *same physical point*, seen from a different distance, angle, or lighting, produces (almost) the *same* fingerprint. That fingerprint is a vector — just an ordered list of numbers, like `[0.2, 0.05, 0.31, ...]` — and once every keypoint has one, "are these the same point?" becomes a simple numerical question: "are these two lists of numbers close to each other?"

So think of "same fingerprint despite scale, rotation, lighting changes" as a *design requirement*, a checklist the engineers had to satisfy. Everything you're about to read in the SIFT algorithm is a sequence of specific engineering tricks, each one solving exactly one item on that checklist. As you read each stage, ask "which nuisance factor (scale? rotation? lighting? noisy location?) is this stage removing?" — that question is the thread that ties the whole section together.

One more piece of vocabulary before we start: this "fingerprint vector for a patch" idea is exactly what a modern deep-learning **embedding** is — a vector that represents an image or image-patch such that similar things get similar vectors. You'll meet the learned version of this same idea in Module 3. The difference is *how* the vector is built: here, humans hand-designed every step; in Module 3, a neural network learns the vector-building process from data, guided by a loss function instead of by an engineer's reasoning. Same goal, two very different ways of getting there.

**Real-life anchor:** this "find matching points between two photos" problem is not academic. Google Lens uses it to recognize objects and landmarks. Phone-based augmented reality apps (like ARKit, which lets you place a virtual couch in your living room through your phone camera) use it to figure out where flat surfaces are by tracking the same points across video frames. Drone mapping software, which stitches thousands of aerial photos into one big map, relies on it to know how the photos overlap. All of these need to match *specific individual points*, not just decide "these two images look similar overall" — which is a much harder and more specific task.

### SIFT — the full pipeline

SIFT stands for **Scale-Invariant Feature Transform**. "Feature" here just means "a keypoint plus its descriptor vector" — the thing you extract from the image. "Scale-invariant" means: even if the object appears bigger or smaller in a different photo (different scale), the algorithm still recognizes it as the same thing. It was published by David Lowe in 2004, and it is one of the most influential algorithms in the whole history of computer vision.

SIFT works in five stages. It's tempting to read these as "five separate things SIFT does," but that's the wrong way to look at it, and it'll make the algorithm feel like arbitrary trivia. The right way to read it is as a *repair chain*: each stage is called in to fix a specific flaw left behind by the previous stage.

- Stage 1 finds you a big pile of candidate points — but far too many of them are junk (noise, or badly-placed).
- Stage 2 cleans that pile up — but the surviving points still have no notion of "which way is up" for that patch, so we can't yet build a consistent description.
- Stage 3 fixes that by giving every point a consistent "up" direction — but we still don't have any actual numbers describing what the patch looks like.
- Stage 4 finally builds that vector of numbers — but the numbers it produces are still sensitive to how bright or dark the lighting is.
- Stage 5 fixes the lighting sensitivity.

Keep that chain in your head as you read. Every choice below stops looking like arbitrary folklore once you see which specific problem it's paying off.

**A quick licensing note, because it matters practically:** SIFT used to be patented, which meant popular software libraries kept it out of their free, easy-to-install versions. That patent expired in March 2020, so today you can just call `cv2.SIFT_create()` in the standard OpenCV library with no extra setup. A related algorithm called SURF (Speeded-Up Robust Features) is *still* patent-encumbered, so it lives in a separate "contrib" (extra, non-default) part of OpenCV that you have to explicitly enable with a flag called `OPENCV_ENABLE_NONFREE`. If you ever try to use SURF and it doesn't work out of the box, that's why.

**Stage 1 — Scale-space extrema detection.**
*The problem this stage solves: we don't yet know where the interesting points are, or — new twist — at what size ("scale") each one should be considered.*

Here "extrema" just means "points that are a local maximum or local minimum" — the biggest or smallest value in their immediate neighborhood, like the peak of a hill or the bottom of a valley in a landscape of numbers.

This stage reuses something you already built in section 1.4: the **Difference-of-Gaussians (DoG) pyramid**. As a quick reminder of what that is: you blur the image by different amounts (more blur = looking at coarser, bigger structure; less blur = looking at fine detail) and subtract pairs of these blurred images from each other. Stacking these differences at multiple blur levels and multiple image sizes gives you a 3D stack of numbers — two spatial dimensions (x, y, i.e. position in the image) plus one "scale" dimension (how blurred/how zoomed-out you're looking). This stack is built specifically so that a blob-like structure (like a corner or a blotch) produces a strong local extremum in this 3D stack, and — crucially — the "scale" location of that extremum tells you the *actual real-world size* of the blob, whether the photo shows it zoomed in or zoomed out. That's how "scale invariance" gets built in: the algorithm searches across sizes, not just across position.

A point becomes a *candidate* keypoint if it's a strict extremum compared to its **26 neighbors** in this 3D stack: 8 neighbors in the same 2D slice (all the pixels touching it, like on a tic-tac-toe grid but 8-connected), plus 9 neighbors in the slice one scale level up, plus 9 more one scale level down (8 surrounding plus 1 directly above/below = 9 each). $8 + 9 + 9 = 26$. If this pixel's value is bigger (or smaller) than all 26 of those neighbors, it's a candidate.

Since you already covered how to build this DoG pyramid in 1.4, this stage is really just "run 1.4, then scan for local extrema in 3D." Nothing new conceptually — you're applying a tool you already have.

*What this stage leaves broken, for the next stage to fix:* two separate problems.
1. **Imprecise location.** The extremum you found is only as precise as your sampling grid. Near the coarser (more zoomed-out, more blurred) levels of the pyramid, each "step" between samples corresponds to several original-image pixels. So the "true" peak of the underlying smooth signal is probably sitting *between* your samples, not exactly on the sample you picked.
2. **Too permissive a test.** Comparing against only 26 neighbors is a fairly weak filter — think of it like a job interview that only checks if you're taller than the 26 people standing closest to you, rather than checking your actual qualifications. As a result, thousands of the candidates that pass this test are actually just noise, or they sit on edges, where "the peak location" is ambiguous (an edge is a ridge, not a hill — many points along the ridge look equally "peak-like," so you can't pin down one precise spot).

**Stage 2 — Keypoint localisation & filtering.**
*This stage fixes both problems left by stage 1: it sharpens the location, and it throws out the bad candidates.*

**Fixing the location.** The discrete extremum we found is just "the biggest sample we happened to compute" — not necessarily the true mathematical peak of the underlying smooth function, which could sit anywhere between samples. To find the true peak, we approximate the DoG function near our candidate point using a **quadratic model** — the same idea as fitting a parabola through nearby points and finding its exact vertex (the top of the parabola), rather than just picking whichever sample happened to be highest.
