Confusing "approximate" with "just optimized" is a common mistake that a strong candidate avoids.
- **You know when the ratio test itself is the wrong tool**: if the images you're matching legitimately contain multiple correct matches for a single point — for example, you're matching against a database that contains duplicate photos, or the scene itself has repeated structure like windows on a building facade — then the second-nearest neighbour might *also* be a genuinely correct match, not a random false one. In that situation the ratio test will actively throw away correct matches, because a true positive with a real "twin" elsewhere looks, mathematically, exactly like the ambiguous case the test is designed to reject. The fix is to exclude candidate matches that come from the same source image or the same cluster before you compute $d_2$, so the "competitor" you're comparing against is actually an independent, unrelated point. Mentioning this edge case in an interview is a strong signal, because it shows you've actually hit this problem while building something, not just read about the happy path.

### ✅ Mastery check

You're matching two photos of the same glass office tower, taken from two different spots on the street. After running the ratio test with $\tau = 0.8$, you're left with only 40 surviving matches out of 3000 original candidates — and RANSAC (the geometric verification step from section 1.8) then fails to find a consistent transformation. Work through this:

**(a)** Why would the ratio test specifically destroy so many matches in a photo like this one?
**(b)** What would you change to fix it?
**(c)** What would you change *instead* if you had the opposite problem — 2000 matches surviving the ratio test, but 70% of them turn out to be wrong?

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>
(a) A glass office tower is full of <b>repetitive structure</b> — window frame after window frame, all built from the same corners and edges, so every window corner has hundreds of near-identical look-alikes elsewhere on the building. That means for almost every true match, the second-best candidate ($d_2$) is nearly as close as the best candidate ($d_1$), so the ratio $d_1/d_2$ sits close to 1 — right at the boundary the ratio test is designed to reject. The ratio test is doing exactly what it's built to do (reject ambiguous matches); the problem is that in this specific photo, the ambiguity is real and widespread, not a rare fluke, so it ends up rejecting the vast majority of matches, including plenty of correct ones.
(b) In order of what to try first: (i) loosen $\tau$ to around 0.9, accepting more ambiguous-looking matches, and lean on <b>geometric verification to do the real filtering instead</b> — RANSAC, when fitting something like a homography (a mathematical description of how one photo's plane maps onto the other, covered in 1.9), can tell correct matches from incorrect ones even among visually identical points, because the wrong matches won't agree on a single consistent geometric transformation, even though appearance alone could never tell them apart; (ii) add a filter that only keeps matches whose surrounding neighbourhoods are spatially consistent with each other before even running RANSAC; (iii) switch to a matcher that looks at the whole set of keypoints together rather than one at a time — modern approaches like SuperGlue and LightGlue use attention (the same mechanism from Transformers) across *all* keypoints simultaneously, which lets them resolve exactly this kind of repeated-structure ambiguity; (iv) use descriptors that look at a larger surrounding area (more context around each point), since more surrounding detail helps tell apart two otherwise-identical corners.
(c) The opposite failure — lots of matches surviving but most of them wrong — means your filters are being <b>too lenient</b>, letting genuinely bad matches through. Fix: tighten $\tau$ down to around 0.7 (stricter cutoff), add the mutual/cross-check filter described above (only keep matches that are each other's best friend in both directions), and — most importantly for RANSAC specifically — run more RANSAC iterations, because the number of iterations needed, $N$, scales as $N \propto 1/w^s$, where $w$ is the fraction of your matches that are actually correct (the "inlier ratio") and $s$ is the minimum number of matched points needed to compute the model you're fitting. With only $w = 0.3$ correct matches and $s = 4$ points needed, you need roughly 560 iterations to be 99% confident of finding a good fit, compared to only about 72 iterations if your inlier ratio were a healthier $w = 0.5$ — a big jump, driven entirely by how contaminated your match set is. It's also worth considering more robust variants like MSAC or MAGSAC++, which are specifically built to cope with situations where most of your data is wrong.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Get an image pair where you already know the exact correct transformation between them (the ground truth) — the **Oxford VGG affine dataset** is built exactly for this (look for its "graf," "boat," and "bark" image sequences; they ship with the ground-truth transformation included, so you can check your matches against the real answer instead of guessing). Try sweeping the ratio test's cutoff $\tau$ across a range of values from 0.5 to 1.0, and at each value, measure two things: "precision" (of the matches you kept, what fraction were actually correct, according to the ground truth) and "recall" (of all the matches that were actually correct, what fraction did you manage to keep). Plot precision against recall as $\tau$ changes — this reproduces the exact experiment and curve from Lowe's original paper. Then repeat the whole exercise comparing SIFT descriptors against ORB descriptors on the same image pairs, and overlay both curves on one plot to see how they differ.

**Read:** Lowe 2004, section 7.1 (this is the original paper section describing the ratio-test experiment and the exact figure you just reproduced above). Muja & Lowe, "Fast Approximate Nearest Neighbors with Automatic Algorithm Configuration" (VISAPP 2009) — this is the original FLANN paper.

---

## 1.8 RANSAC (and MAGSAC++) 🟢

### Intuition

Let's set up the exact problem you're in. Back in section 1.7 you built a list of "candidate matches" — pairs of keypoints, one from each of two photos, that your descriptor comparison thinks are probably the same physical point in the world (like the same corner of a window, seen from two slightly different angles). The word "candidate" is doing a lot of work there: some of these matches are correct (we'll soon give this a name: an **inlier**, meaning "a data point that actually fits the true, correct pattern"), and some are just wrong pairings that happened to look similar by coincidence (we'll call these **outliers**, meaning "a data point that does NOT fit the true pattern — it's noise or a mistake, not signal"). Say you have 500 candidate matches, and maybe only 200 of them are real, correct matches. The other 300 are lies, in a sense — they'll actively pull your answer in the wrong direction if you're not careful.

The task you actually want to do with these matches is called **fitting a model**. Here "model" just means "a mathematical description of how the two images relate to each other" — for example, a formula that says "point (x, y) in image A moves to point (x', y') in image B." If you know that formula, you can warp one image onto the other, which is exactly how phone panorama-stitching works: it lines up two photos by figuring out this point-to-point relationship and then blends them together. The question this section answers is: **how do you find that formula when you don't know in advance which 300 of your 500 data points are lying to you?**

If you're coming from general deep learning, your gut instinct is probably: "just fit a model to all 500 points and use a loss function that's robust to noise." That instinct is not crazy, but it fails badly here, and it's worth understanding exactly why, because the "why" is the whole motivation for the algorithm in this section.

The classic way to "fit a line" (or any model) to a bunch of points is called **least squares**: you pick the model whose predictions minimize the sum of squared distances between the model's prediction and the real data points. Squaring the error means big errors count for a lot more than small ones — an error of 10 contributes 100 to the sum, while an error of 1 contributes only 1. That's normally a reasonable design choice. But now imagine one single point that's wildly wrong — not off by a little, but off by a lot, because it's a bad match entirely unrelated to the real pattern. Because the error gets squared, that one terrible point can contribute more to the total error than all 499 good points combined. To reduce that huge squared term, least squares will happily drag the *entire* fitted line or model toward that one bad point, wrecking the fit for everyone else. Statisticians describe this by saying ordinary least squares has a **breakdown point of 0%**. "Breakdown point" here means: the fraction of the data that can be arbitrarily corrupted before the method's answer becomes arbitrarily bad. A breakdown point of 0% means even one single bad point, in the worst case, can send the answer anywhere at all. So least squares is exactly the wrong tool here.

And there's an even deeper issue than "outliers dominate the loss." With 300 out of 500 points wrong (60% outliers), you don't have "one correct pattern plus some noise around it" — you effectively have **two completely different, unrelated sets of points mixed together in the same pile**, and no single mathematical curve or transformation is a meaningful description of "the trend" of that mixture. It's like trying to compute one "average commute time" from a dataset that secretly contains both car commutes and airplane flights lumped together — the average you get describes neither group.

**RANSAC** (short for "RANdom SAmple Consensus") solves this by flipping the whole strategy on its head. Instead of trying to use all the data and hoping the bad points wash out, it does the opposite: it repeatedly **guesses** a small handful of points that might all be correct, fits a model using only that tiny guess, and then checks how many of the *other* points agree with that guess. If a guess turns out to be lucky (all genuinely correct points), the resulting model will explain a lot of the other data too, and you'll see a lot of agreement. If a guess is unlucky (it grabbed one or more bad points), the resulting model will be nonsense, and almost nothing else will agree with it. You just keep repeating this "guess a tiny sample, fit, check agreement" loop many times, and keep whichever guess got the most agreement.

Here's a concrete everyday analogy. Imagine you're a detective and you've collected 500 eyewitness statements about which direction a car was driving, but you know 300 of the witnesses are unreliable or lying (maybe they saw a different car, or misremember). You can't tell in advance who's lying. So instead of averaging all 500 answers (which would be ruined by the liars), you try this: randomly pick a tiny handful of witnesses — say, just 2 or 3 — and assume, just for a moment, that all of them are telling the truth. Build a "theory of the crime" from just their statements. Then check: how many of the *other* 497 witnesses' statements are also consistent with this theory? If you happened to pick 2-3 honest witnesses, a lot of other honest witnesses will agree with your theory too, because they're all describing the same true event. If you happened to pick even one liar, your theory will be some random nonsense that almost nobody else agrees with. Repeat this process — pick a new random tiny group, build a theory, count agreement — many times, and the theory that ends up with the most agreeing witnesses is almost certainly the true one. That's RANSAC.

Why does RANSAC insist on picking the *smallest possible* group of points each time (called a **minimal sample** — "minimal" meaning "the fewest points mathematically required to pin down the model, no more")? For a line, that's 2 points (two points determine a unique line). For the specific "homography" model you'll see below, it's 4 points. The reason for going minimal, rather than say grabbing 10 or 20 points per guess, is about probability, and it becomes the central derivation of this whole section — hold that thought, we're about to derive it properly. The short version: the more points you require to *all* be correct in a single guess, the rarer it is that a random guess is entirely clean, and that rarity gets dramatically worse the more points you demand. So you want to demand as few as mathematically necessary.

One more useful way to describe RANSAC in one sentence: it's fitting a model by running **repeated small elections** — draw a small random jury, ask them to propose a verdict, then let everyone else vote on whether they agree, and keep the most popular verdict.

Real-life anchor: this exact algorithm is running inside your phone's camera app every single time it stitches together a panorama photo, and it's running inside AR headsets whenever they figure out "where am I, relative to what I saw a moment ago" (this second use is sometimes called "relocalisation").

### The algorithm

Here is the actual procedure, spelled out step by step in pseudocode (a plain-English-ish sketch of code, not any particular real language):

```
best_inliers = ∅
for iteration in 1..N:
    S ← random minimal sample of s correspondences
    M ← fit_model(S)                        # exact, minimal solver
    I ← { c : residual(c, M) < t }          # consensus set
    if |I| > |best_inliers|: best_inliers, best_M ← I, M
refit best_M on best_inliers                # least squares / LM on inliers only
```

Let's translate every symbol here into plain words, because this line-by-line reading is exactly what an interviewer wants to see you do fluently.

- `best_inliers = ∅` — before we start, we haven't found any good group of agreeing points yet, so we initialize an empty set (the symbol ∅ just means "the empty set," i.e. "nothing in it yet").
- `for iteration in 1..N` — we're going to repeat the whole guess-and-check process $N$ times. We haven't said what $N$ should be yet — that's the entire point of the next section, and it needs real math to answer properly.
- `S ← random minimal sample of s correspondences` — "correspondences" is just another word for the candidate matches from section 1.7 (a pair: one keypoint from image A, one from image B, that we're guessing might be the same real-world point). `s` (lowercase) is the **minimal sample size** — the smallest number of matches mathematically required to fully determine the model. We randomly grab exactly `s` of our candidate matches and call that little handful `S`.
- `M ← fit_model(S)` — using *only* those `s` points (nothing else), compute the exact model `M` that fits them perfectly. Because we grabbed exactly the minimal number of points, this fit is not an approximation or an average — there's a unique, exact mathematical solution, computed directly (this is what "exact, minimal solver" means in the comment: a formula that solves for the model exactly from the minimum number of points, no iteration or approximation needed).
- `I ← { c : residual(c, M) < t }` — now we check *every single one* of our original candidate matches (not just the tiny sample `S`, but the full 500) against the model `M` we just built. For each match `c`, we compute its **residual**, which just means "how far off is this data point from what the model predicts?" — think of it as an error or distance measurement. If that error is smaller than some threshold `t` (a cutoff number we pick — more on choosing it later), we say this match "agrees" with the model, and we add it to the set `I`. This set `I` — all the points that agree with a particular guessed model — is called the **consensus set** ("consensus" meaning "general agreement"; this is the set of points voting "yes, I agree with this theory").
- `if |I| > |best_inliers|: best_inliers, best_M ← I, M` — the vertical bars `|I|` just mean "the number of items in the set `I`" (its size, its count). If this round's consensus set is bigger than the best one we've seen in any previous round, we remember this round's model and consensus set as our new best answer so far.
- After the loop finishes, `refit best_M on best_inliers` — this last step matters and is easy to forget. Once we've identified which points are (probably) the genuinely correct ones — the winning consensus set — we throw away the tiny minimal-sample model and instead do a proper least-squares fit (the same kind of fit we said was dangerous earlier!) but now using *only* the points we trust. Least squares is perfectly fine, even great, once the bad points have been filtered out — the danger earlier was mixing good and bad points together, not least squares itself. "LM" here stands for Levenberg–Marquardt, a standard numerical optimization method used for this kind of refitting; you don't need its internals for this section, just know it's "a solver used to do the final precise fit."

So in plain English, the whole loop is: repeatedly grab the smallest-possible random handful of matches, build the one exact model that handful implies, see how many of the *other* matches agree with that model, and keep whichever guess earned the most agreement. Then, at the very end, do one final careful fit using only the trusted, agreeing points.

The value `s`, the minimal sample size, depends on which kind of model you're fitting. You don't need to know what all of these models are yet (some come up in later sections/modules) — just notice the pattern that different geometric relationships need different minimum numbers of points to pin them down: a straight line needs 2 points; a "homography" (a way of describing how one flat image plane maps onto another — you'll build this in section 1.9) needs 4; a "fundamental matrix" (relates two camera views without needing to know the cameras' internal settings — Module 5) needs 7 or 8 depending on which solver you use; an "essential matrix" (like the fundamental matrix, but assuming you *do* know the cameras' internal settings) needs 5; and "PnP" (figuring out a camera's position given some 3D points and where they appear in a photo) needs 3, using a specific method called P3P.

### Deriving the iteration count $N$ — do this on the whiteboard

We left a big hole in the algorithm above: how many times, $N$, should the loop actually run? Run it too few times, and you might never happen to grab a fully-clean sample, so you'll never even see the correct model as a candidate. Run it too many times, and you're wasting time and computation for no extra benefit. The good news is that this isn't a "just try some numbers and see" situation — there is an exact formula you can derive from basic probability, from scratch, with no prior probability-theory background assumed. Let's build it one small step at a time.

Before the derivation itself, here are the two basic probability facts we'll lean on, spelled out so nothing is assumed:

- **Independent events multiply.** If one event doesn't affect the odds of another (they're "independent"), then the probability that *both* happen equals the probability of the first, times the probability of the second. Example: if a fair coin flip has a 50% chance of heads, the chance of getting heads twice in a row is $0.5 \times 0.5 = 0.25$, i.e. 25%.
- **"Not happening" is 1 minus "happening."** Any event either happens or it doesn't — there's no third option — so if the probability something happens is $x$ (where $x$ is a number between 0 and 1, with 1 meaning "certain" and 0 meaning "impossible"), the probability it does *not* happen is $1 - x$. This is called the **complement**.

The classic trick for answering "what's the probability that at least one of many attempts succeeds?" is to instead compute the easier, opposite question — "what's the probability that *every single* attempt fails?" — and then use the complement rule to flip that into the answer we actually want. We're about to use exactly that trick.

Let's define $w$ = the **inlier ratio**, meaning: out of all your candidate matches, what fraction are actually correct? If 200 out of 500 matches are correct, $w = 200/500 = 0.4$. In probability language, $w$ is the probability that if you reach into your pile of candidate matches and pull one out at random, it happens to be a genuinely correct one (an inlier). We write this as $w = P(\text{a randomly chosen match is an inlier})$ — the notation $P(\ldots)$ just means "the probability of the thing written inside the parentheses."

Now let's build up the full derivation, one line at a time, never skipping a step:

- **Step 1 — probability that one entire sample of $s$ points is completely clean.** "Clean" means every single one of the $s$ points we grab happens to be a true inlier, none of them a bad match. Since each individual draw is an inlier with probability $w$, and we're treating the draws as independent of each other (more on this approximation in a moment), the "independent events multiply" rule says: the chance all $s$ of them are inliers is $w$ multiplied by itself $s$ times, i.e. $w^s$.

$$
P(\text{one sample of } s \text{ points is all inliers}) = w^s
$$

  (A technical honesty note: strictly speaking, once you've pulled one match out of the pile without putting it back, the odds for the *next* pull have shifted very slightly, because the pile is now one item smaller — this is called "sampling without replacement." But when you have hundreds of candidate matches and are only pulling a handful (like 4 or 8) each round, that shift is so tiny it doesn't matter, and treating each draw as independent — as if you *did* put it back each time — is an excellent approximation. This is the same simplification used any time you sample a small handful from a big pool.)

- **Step 2 — probability that a given sample is NOT clean (has at least one bad point in it).** We call a sample with at least one outlier in it "contaminated." Since a sample either is entirely clean or it is not (there's no third option), the complement rule tells us directly:

$$
P(\text{a given sample is contaminated}) = 1 - w^s
$$

- **Step 3 — probability that ALL $N$ of our attempted samples are contaminated (we never once get lucky).** Each iteration of the loop draws a fresh independent sample, so once again the "independent events multiply" rule applies — but this time we're multiplying the "contaminated" probability by itself $N$ times, once for each iteration:

$$
P(\text{all } N \text{ samples are contaminated}) = (1 - w^s)^N
$$

  This is the quantity we actually want to be small — it represents total failure, meaning we ran the whole loop $N$ times and *never once* happened to grab a fully clean sample, so the correct model never even got proposed as a candidate.

- **Step 4 — probability that at least one of our $N$ samples was clean (success!).** This is exactly "not total failure," so apply the complement rule one more time to the result of Step 3:

$$
P(\text{at least one clean sample in } N \text{ tries}) = 1 - (1-w^s)^N
$$

  We'll call this quantity our **success probability**, and we get to *choose* how confident we want to be — typically we demand it equal some target $p$ (a common choice is $p = 0.99$, i.e. "I want a 99% chance that somewhere in my $N$ tries, I drew a fully clean sample"). Setting the success probability equal to our target $p$ gives us the equation we need to solve:

$$
1 - (1 - w^s)^N = p
$$

Now we solve this equation for $N$, since $N$ — the number of iterations to run — is the thing we actually want to know, and everything else ($p$, $w$, $s$) is something we either choose or estimate. Rearranging step by step:

First, move the $(1-w^s)^N$ term to the other side and $p$ to this side: $(1-w^s)^N = 1-p$.

Next, we need to get $N$ out of the exponent. The standard tool for "pulling a variable down out of an exponent" is to take the **logarithm** of both sides. A logarithm is the mathematical operation with exactly the property we need here: $\log(a^N) = N \times \log(a)$ — it turns "raise to the power of $N$" into "multiply by $N$." Applying $\log$ to both sides of our equation:

$$
N \log(1-w^s) = \log(1-p)
$$

Finally, divide both sides by $\log(1-w^s)$ to isolate $N$ by itself:

$$
\boxed{\,N = \frac{\log(1-p)}{\log(1 - w^s)}\,}
$$

That boxed formula is the answer: given how confident you want to be ($p$), how many points a minimal sample needs ($s$), and what fraction of your data is trustworthy ($w$), this tells you exactly how many random-sample iterations to run.

Before trusting any formula, it's good practice to sanity-check its signs and extreme behavior, so let's do that. Both $1-p$ and $1-w^s$ are numbers strictly between 0 and 1 (since $p$ and $w^s$ are themselves between 0 and 1). The logarithm of any number between 0 and 1 is always negative (for example, $\log(0.5) \approx -0.69$) — so both the top and bottom of our fraction are negative numbers, and a negative divided by a negative is positive, meaning $N$ comes out positive, as an iteration count obviously must. Good — the formula isn't secretly telling us to run a negative number of loops. Now check an extreme case: as $w$ gets smaller and smaller, approaching 0 (meaning almost none of your data is trustworthy), the denominator $\log(1 - w^s)$ approaches $\log(1 - 0) = \log(1) = 0$. Dividing by a number that's shrinking toward zero makes the whole fraction blow up toward infinity, so $N \to \infty$. In plain English: if essentially none of your matches are correct, you'd need to search forever to have any real hope of stumbling onto a clean sample — which matches common sense perfectly.

One subtlety worth stating explicitly, because it's easy to misread: $p$ is **not** a measure of how accurate the final fitted model will be, and it's not "confidence in the answer" in some vague general sense. It is very specifically the probability that, somewhere across your $N$ random attempts, you happened to draw at least one sample that was entirely free of bad matches. Every other guarantee RANSAC seems to offer rides on top of that one specific probabilistic event actually happening.

**Worked numbers (know at least two of these):**

| Model | $s$ | $w$ | $N$ at $p = 0.99$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| Line | 2 | 0.5 | 17 |
| **Homography** | **4** | **0.5** | **72** |
| Homography | 4 | 0.3 | 567 |
| **Fundamental (8-pt)** | **8** | **0.5** | **1177** |
| Fundamental (7-pt) | 7 | 0.5 | 587 |
| Essential (5-pt) | 5 | 0.5 | 145 |
| Fundamental (8-pt) | 8 | 0.3 | 70,188 |

**Let's walk through the homography row completely by hand, so you could redo this cold on a whiteboard with no notes.** A homography (again: the "how one flat image maps onto another" model from section 1.9) needs 4 matching points to pin it down exactly, so $s=4$. Suppose half of your candidate matches are actually correct, so $w = 0.5$.

First question: how often does one single random draw of 4 points come up entirely clean (all 4 genuine inliers, none fake)? Using Step 1 from the derivation above, that's $w^s = 0.5^4$. Let's compute that explicitly rather than just quoting it: $0.5^4 = 0.5 \times 0.5 \times 0.5 \times 0.5 = 0.0625$. So about one draw in sixteen ($1/0.0625 = 16$) comes up perfectly clean.

Second, that means a given draw *fails* to be clean with probability $1 - 0.0625 = 0.9375$, i.e. 93.75% of individual draws are contaminated. If we run $N$ draws in a row, the chance that *every single one* of them fails is $0.9375$ multiplied by itself $N$ times, i.e. $0.9375^N$ (this is Step 3 above). We want this "total failure" probability to be small — specifically, we want a 99% success rate, so we want only a 1% chance of total failure. That gives us the equation $0.9375^N = 0.01$.

Now solve for $N$ by taking the natural logarithm (written $\ln$; it's just a logarithm using a particular base called $e \approx 2.718$ — any base works for this kind of algebra, since the base cancels out in the end, but $\ln$ is the conventional choice) of both sides: $N \times \ln(0.9375) = \ln(0.01)$. Plugging in the numerical values (you'd get these from a calculator): $\ln(0.9375) = -0.06454$ and $\ln(0.01) = -4.6052$. So:

$$
N = \frac{-4.6052}{-0.06454} = 71.4
$$

Since you obviously can't run a fractional number of loop iterations, you round up to be safe, giving $N = \mathbf{72}$ — exactly matching the table.

It's worth double-checking that this number "feels right" with a rough mental estimate, separate from the precise calculation, as a safety net against arithmetic mistakes. One clean draw shows up about once every 16 tries, and we want to be very confident (close to certain) that we've seen at least one clean draw somewhere in our attempts. A common rule of thumb for "how many tries until you're 99% sure you've seen a rare event with base rate $1/k$" is roughly $k \times \ln(100) \approx k \times 4.6$. Here $k = 16$, so that's roughly $16 \times 4.6 \approx 74$ — very close to the exact answer of 72, which is a good sign our exact math is sound.

**Pause:** predict the 8-point fundamental matrix row at the same $w = 0.5$ before you look back up at the table. Twice the sample size — does $N$ roughly double?

No — it does **not** roughly double, and the reason why is the single most important qualitative fact in this whole section. Looking at the table, going from $s=4$ to $s=8$ takes $N$ from 72 all the way to **1177**, which is a factor of about 16, not 2. Here's why: doubling the sample size $s$ means doubling the *exponent* in $w^s$. Since $w^{2s} = (w^s)^2$, doubling $s$ doesn't double the "clean draw" probability — it **squares** it, and squaring a small number (like our $0.0625$ from before) makes it much, much smaller ($0.0625^2 \approx 0.0039$, roughly 16 times smaller, matching the ratio we see in $N$). And because $N$ scales like $1/w^s$ (roughly — from the formula), a much smaller $w^s$ means a much, much larger required $N$. So the relationship between $N$ and $s$ isn't gentle and linear ("a bit more work for a bit more sample size") — it's **exponential**: every extra point you require in the minimal sample makes clean samples geometrically rarer, and required iterations geometrically more numerous.

**Two things this table screams:**
1. **$N$ grows exponentially in $s$.** This single fact is the entire economic justification for spending extra mathematical effort on **minimal solvers** — clever, specialized formulas that compute a model from the fewest possible points, rather than lazily using a few extra points because it's mathematically easier. For example, there's a well-known method (Nistér's 5-point algorithm) for computing the essential matrix using only 5 points, when a more naive approach might use 8. Looking at the table, 5 points needs only 145 iterations while 8 points needs 1177 — nearly 8 times more work for the "lazier" method. So when you later hit a question in Module 5 like "why bother with a clever 5-point algorithm for the essential matrix when the 8-point algorithm for the (closely related) fundamental matrix is comparatively simple to compute?" — *this exponential blowup* is the real answer.
2. **$N$ also blows up as $w$ (the fraction of good data) drops.** Look at the homography rows: going from $w=0.5$ to $w=0.3$ multiplies the required iterations by about 8×. For the 8-point fundamental matrix, that same drop in $w$ multiplies iterations by about 60×. This is precisely why getting a good inlier ratio *before* RANSAC even starts matters enormously — recall the "ratio test" from section 1.7, a filtering step that throws away likely-bad matches early. Every bit of inlier ratio you can buy upstream, before RANSAC even begins, pays for itself many times over in how few RANSAC iterations you then need.

**Adaptive $N$.** There's an awkward circularity hiding in everything above: our formula for $N$ needs to know $w$ (the fraction of correct matches) as an input — but $w$ is precisely the thing you don't know in advance; discovering roughly how many matches are correct is the whole point of running RANSAC in the first place! The clever way out of this chicken-and-egg problem is to **estimate $w$ as you go, using your own results so far**. Here's the idea: at any point partway through the loop, the size of the best consensus set you've found so far is itself a reasonable lower-bound guess at how many total inliers exist. So the practical procedure is: start by assuming you'll need to run forever (set $N = \infty$ as a placeholder), and after every single iteration, recompute an estimate $\hat w = |I_{\text{best}}| / n$ (read: "our current best guess for $w$ equals the size of our best consensus set so far, divided by $n$, the total number of candidate matches"), and immediately recompute what $N$ *should* be using that updated $\hat w$ plugged into our boxed formula. Stop the loop as soon as the iteration count you've already run reaches this constantly-updating $N$. As the loop finds better and better models, $\hat w$ keeps rising, which makes the required $N$ keep shrinking — so the loop is continuously tightening its own budget as it learns more. On easy problems (where most matches are actually correct), this means the loop can often stop after just a handful of iterations rather than grinding through some large fixed number. Every real, production implementation of RANSAC (including the one inside OpenCV) does exactly this adaptive trick rather than using a single fixed $N$ decided in advance.

### Choosing the threshold $t$

There's one more knob left unexplained in the algorithm from earlier: the threshold `t`, the cutoff distance below which we decide a point "agrees" with a candidate model. Unlike some other tuning constants you'll meet elsewhere in this course (for example, $\kappa$ in the Harris corner detector, which really is mostly hand-tuned by feel), this threshold $t$ *can* actually be derived from first principles — as long as you're willing to make one reasonable assumption about how noisy your keypoint measurements are.

Here's the question to ask, phrased precisely: "if a match genuinely is correct, how far off from the model's exact prediction would we plausibly still expect it to land, purely because our keypoint-detection process isn't perfectly precise?" No detector pins down a keypoint's exact pixel location with zero error — there's always a little bit of jitter, called **localisation error**.

Assume this localisation error behaves like a **Gaussian** (also called "normal") random variable with **zero mean** and **standard deviation $\sigma$**, applied separately to each coordinate (x and y). Let's unpack that assumption in plain words: "zero mean" means the errors aren't biased in any particular direction — they're just as likely to push the detected point left as right, up as down, so on average they cancel out to zero. "Standard deviation $\sigma$" is a number describing the *typical size* of these random wobbles — a bigger $\sigma$ means noisier, more jittery keypoint locations. This is a very standard and reasonable assumption for measurement noise of this kind.

Now here's the useful mathematical fact that connects this noise assumption to our threshold: if you take several independent Gaussian-noise numbers, divide each one by its standard deviation $\sigma$ (this is called "standardizing" them, and it produces what's called a "standard normal" variable — mean 0, standard deviation 1), square each one, and add them up, the resulting sum follows a specific, well-studied probability distribution called the **chi-squared distribution** (written $\chi^2$), with a parameter called **degrees of freedom**, written $m$. "Degrees of freedom" here just means: how many independent noisy numbers did you add together? If you added up 1 squared noise term, that's $m=1$; if 2, that's $m=2$; and so on. In our RANSAC setting, $m$ corresponds to the number of independent directions in which the residual error can vary — we'll pin down exactly what that means for our two cases in a moment.

Since a residual (the "how far off is this point" measurement from the algorithm section) built out of Gaussian noise follows this $\chi^2$ pattern, we can use the known, tabulated properties of the $\chi^2$ distribution to answer: "below what residual value does a genuinely correct point fall, say, 95% of the time?" That's exactly a statement about a **quantile** — a quantile just means "the value below which a given percentage of outcomes fall." So instead of guessing a threshold by feel, we pick a target confidence $\alpha$ (like 95%) for "how often should a true inlier's residual land under our cutoff," and read the correct multiplier straight off the $\chi^2$ distribution's known table:

$$
t^2 = \chi^2_{m, \alpha}\,\sigma^2
$$

In words: the squared threshold $t^2$ equals a known table-lookup constant (which depends on $m$, the degrees of freedom, and $\alpha$, your chosen confidence level) multiplied by $\sigma^2$, the squared noise level you assumed for your keypoint detector.

- $m = 1$ (this is a **point-to-line distance** case — for example, the "epipolar-line residual" you'll meet with the fundamental matrix $F$ in Module 5, where a matching point is checked against how close it lands to a predicted *line*, not a predicted single point): at $\alpha = 95\%$ confidence, the table gives $t^2 = 3.84\sigma^2$.
- $m = 2$ (this is a **point-to-point distance** case — for example, "reprojection error" for a homography $H$, where you check how close a warped point lands to its actual matching point): the table gives $t^2 = 5.99\sigma^2$.

Why does the same idea produce two different constants for these two cases? It comes down to how many independent directions the error is actually free to vary in. An epipolar residual measures the distance from a point to a *line*. A point sitting exactly on that line, but slid anywhere along the line's length, still has zero error — sliding along the line direction costs you nothing. So there's really only *one* meaningfully constrained direction (perpendicular to the line) — hence $m=1$. A reprojection residual, on the other hand, measures distance to a specific *point*, and error can appear independently in both the horizontal and vertical directions — nothing is "free" — so both directions are constrained, hence $m=2$.

Putting a number on it: if you believe your keypoint detector has about 1 pixel of typical localisation noise (so $\sigma = 1$), then for a homography ($m=2$), the principled inlier threshold is $t = \sqrt{5.99 \times 1^2} = \sqrt{5.99} \approx 2.45$ pixels — not an arbitrary round number you picked because it "felt right," but a value derived directly from your noise assumption and your chosen confidence level. **Being able to derive and quote this $\chi^2$-based threshold in an interview is a strong signal of depth** — most candidates, when asked how they picked their RANSAC threshold, will only be able to say "we tuned it by trial and error."

### Variants worth naming

| Variant | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **MSAC / M-SAC** | cost $= \sum \min(e_i^2, t^2)$ instead of inlier *count* | inliers now vote with quality, not just membership; strictly better at no cost |
| **MLESAC** | maximises a likelihood under an inlier/outlier mixture | principled version of MSAC |
| **LO-RANSAC** | run a local optimisation (inner RANSAC on the current inlier set) whenever a new best is found | dramatically reduces required $N$; near-free accuracy |
| **PROSAC** | sample from matches ordered by descriptor quality first | exploits that good-ratio matches are more likely inliers; often 10–100× fewer iterations |
| **DEGENSAC** | detects degenerate (dominant-plane) samples for $F$ estimation | fixes the single worst failure mode of $F$-RANSAC |
| **GC-RANSAC** | graph-cut local optimisation using spatial coherence | current strong baseline |
| **MAGSAC++** 🟢 | **no threshold** — marginalises the model quality over a range of $\sigma$ ("$\sigma$-consensus"), with an iteratively reweighted least-squares inner loop | removes the most fragile hyperparameter; SOTA on IMC benchmarks; available as `cv2.USAC_MAGSAC` |

It's easy to look at this table as seven unrelated tricks to memorize, but it actually tells one connected story, and it's worth reading it that way. Plain vanilla RANSAC (the version we derived above) is deliberately simple, and that simplicity means it throws away useful information in three specific places. First, when counting a consensus set, it treats every agreeing point exactly the same — a point that lands 0.1 pixels from the model's prediction counts identically to one that lands right at the threshold, 2.4 pixels away, even though the first is obviously a much better piece of evidence. MSAC and MLESAC fix this by letting points vote with a graded "quality score" (how close they are) instead of a flat yes/no. Second, vanilla RANSAC treats every candidate match as equally likely to be a good sample to draw — it doesn't use the fact that some matches (say, ones whose descriptor comparison from section 1.7 was a much closer match) are more likely to be correct than others. PROSAC fixes this by drawing the "probably good" matches first. Third, whenever vanilla RANSAC finds a new best guess, it just moves on to try another totally random sample next, without ever trying to polish or improve the promising guess it just found. LO-RANSAC and GC-RANSAC fix this by immediately running a small extra refinement step on any promising new best guess before continuing. Once you understand vanilla RANSAC is leaving these three kinds of information on the table, MAGSAC++ makes sense as simply the last, most ambitious fix: it removes the final hand-picked constant left in the whole system, the threshold $t$ itself, by mathematically averaging (marginalising) over a whole range of plausible noise levels $\sigma$ instead of committing to one.

**Practical note:** since OpenCV version 4.5.0, the functions `findHomography` and `findFundamentalMat` both accept a `method` argument that includes `cv2.USAC_MAGSAC`, `cv2.USAC_ACCURATE`, and similar. In 2026, calling these functions with the plain old `cv2.RANSAC` option, when the improved MAGSAC-based methods are sitting right there for free, is genuinely leaving accuracy on the table for no reason.

**In your own words:** what exactly is the probability $p$ in the iteration formula the probability *of*?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

1. **Derive $N$ live** and immediately draw the two consequences we walked through above: it's exponential in the sample size $s$ (which is *why* clever minimal solvers with small $s$ matter so much), and it explodes as the inlier ratio $w$ drops (which is *why* upstream match quality, like the ratio test from 1.7, matters so much).
2. **Justify the threshold $t$ from a $\chi^2$ noise model**, as derived above, rather than shrugging and saying "we tuned it."
3. **Degeneracy** — this is the sharpest, most interview-impressive point in this whole section, so let's make sure it's fully clear. A **degenerate configuration** means: a specific arrangement of the scene or the sampled points where RANSAC's core assumption — "there is one single dominant, correct model, and a randomly-drawn minimal sample either matches it or doesn't" — silently breaks down, even though the algorithm keeps running and *looks* like it's succeeding. The classic killer example: trying to estimate a **fundamental matrix** (the Module 5 model relating two camera views, which needs $s=7$ or $8$ points) on a scene where everything you can see happens to lie on a single flat plane — like photographing a flat wall or building facade from two angles. In that situation, a much simpler model (a homography, needing only 4 points) already explains *all* of your matches perfectly, because a flat plane really is fully described by a homography. The trouble is, when this happens, there turns out to be a whole family of different possible fundamental matrices that ALSO fit these plane-derived matches perfectly (mathematically, any $F$ of a certain form works — you don't need the formula itself yet, just the consequence). So RANSAC reports back a huge, confident-looking consensus set and a "successful" fit — but the specific fundamental matrix it landed on is essentially arbitrary and geometrically meaningless, because the data genuinely couldn't distinguish between the many candidates. It's a **confident, silent failure** — nothing crashes, nothing looks wrong on the surface, but the answer is garbage. Being able to name **DEGENSAC** (a variant designed specifically to detect and fix this exact failure mode, from the table above) as the practical remedy is a strong way to close out this point in an interview.
4. **RANSAC is probabilistic, not guaranteed.** It's worth stating this plainly rather than letting it stay implicit: the output of RANSAC is a model that is correct with probability $p$ — and $p$ was a number *you personally chose* as a design parameter when picking how many iterations to run. This means the output of RANSAC is technically a **random variable** — a quantity whose value can differ from run to run purely due to randomness. Run the exact same RANSAC procedure twice on the exact same data, and (because the samples drawn each round are random) you can get two slightly different answers.
5. **RANSAC only finds one model at a time.** If your scene actually contains multiple valid models — say, two different physical planes in the picture, each internally consistent — plain RANSAC will just find whichever one has the most support and report only that one. To handle scenes with multiple models, you either run **sequential RANSAC** (find the best model, remove its inlier points from consideration, then re-run RANSAC on what's left, repeating — simple, but the results end up subtly biased) or use dedicated multi-model methods built for this from the start (named here for reference: J-Linkage, Progressive-X).
6. **Always refit on the inliers at the end** — don't skip this final step from the algorithm section. The tiny model computed from just the minimal sample is only ever meant as a rough hypothesis to test agreement with — it's not meant to be your final, precise answer. Your genuinely final model estimate should always come from a proper least-squares (or LM) fit over the *entire* winning consensus set of trusted points. Stopping at the minimal-sample fit throws away almost all of your good data.

### ✅ Mastery check

You are estimating a fundamental matrix from 1000 putative matches with an inlier ratio of 0.35, using the 8-point algorithm.

**(a)** How many iterations for $p = 0.99$? Show the arithmetic.
**(b)** Your budget is 2000 iterations. Give three *distinct* ways to get within budget, and rank them by how much you'd trust the result.
**(c)** The scene is the flat facade of a building. What silently goes wrong, and how would you detect it?

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>
(a) $w^s = 0.35^8 = 2.25\times10^{-4}$. $\log(1 - 2.25\times10^{-4}) \approx -2.25\times10^{-4}$. $N = -4.6052 / -2.25\times10^{-4} \approx \mathbf{20{,}450}$ iterations. Ten times over budget.
(b) Ranked:
 1. <b>Use the 7-point algorithm</b> ($s=7$): $w^7 = 6.4\times10^{-4}$, $N \approx 7{,}155$ — still over. Combine with <b>PROSAC</b> (sample good matches first) and/or <b>LO-RANSAC</b> (local optimisation lets you stop far earlier). This is the trustworthy path: same model, better sampling.
 2. <b>Raise the inlier ratio upstream</b> — tighten the ratio test, add mutual-NN, use a better matcher (LightGlue). Going from $w=0.35$ to $w=0.5$ takes 8-point $N$ from 20,470 to 1,177 — <b>in budget</b>, and the estimate is better because the data is better. Arguably this should be #1.
 3. <b>Lower $p$ to 0.95</b>: $N = \log(0.05)/\log(1-2.25\times10^{-4}) \approx 13{,}300$ — still over, and you've bought a 1-in-20 chance of total failure. Weakest option.
 (Also acceptable: if you have calibrated cameras, estimate the <b>essential</b> matrix with the 5-point algorithm — $s=5$ gives $N \approx 875$, comfortably in budget. That's the best answer of all if calibration is available, and it's the Module 5 link.)
(c) A flat facade is a <b>degenerate configuration</b> for $F$ (see the Top-1% distinction section above for the full plain-English explanation of what this means): all correspondences are related by a single homography, and any $F$ of the form $F = [\mathbf{e}']_\times H$ fits them perfectly for <i>any</i> epipole $\mathbf{e}'$ (an "epipole" is a specific geometric point defined in Module 5 — you don't need its details yet, just that this formula shows there's an entire family of equally-good-looking answers, not one correct one). So RANSAC reports a huge inlier count and high confidence while the epipolar geometry is unconstrained in one degree of freedom — a silent, confident failure. Detection: fit a homography to the same matches; if the homography inlier count is close to the $F$ inlier count, you are in the degenerate case. Fix: DEGENSAC / QDEGSAC, or use the plane-and-parallax formulation, or move the camera to create real parallax.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Implement RANSAC generically (`fit_fn`, `residual_fn`, `s`, `t`, `p`) and use it for both line fitting and homography estimation. Add **adaptive $N$** and verify empirically that the iteration count it converges to matches the closed-form prediction. Then implement MSAC's cost and measure the accuracy difference on synthetic data with known ground truth across inlier ratios 0.2–0.8. Finally, benchmark against `cv2.USAC_MAGSAC`.

**Read:** Fischler & Bolles (1981) — the original, short. Then the MAGSAC++ paper (Barath et al., CVPR 2020) §3 for the $\sigma$-consensus idea. Hartley & Zisserman §4.7 for the $\chi^2$ threshold derivation and the algebraic/geometric error distinction.

---

## 1.9 Homography & Panorama Stitching

### Intuition

Let's start with the everyday thing this whole lesson explains: how does your phone turn ten separate photos into one seamless panorama? You sweep the phone left to right, it takes several pictures, and somehow it lines them up into one wide image with no visible joins. That "lining up" step is exactly what this lesson is about.

In the last lesson (RANSAC) we learned a general trick for fitting a model to messy, partly-wrong data. But we never said what the *model* actually was. This lesson gives you that model: a specific kind of mathematical rule, called a **homography**, that describes how one photo's pixels move to become another photo's pixels — under certain conditions.

Here's the key question this lesson answers: **if I take two photos of the same thing from two different positions, is there a simple, exact mathematical formula that tells me "this pixel in photo A corresponds to that pixel in photo B"?**

In general, the honest answer is *no*. Here's why. Imagine you're standing in a room and you take a photo, then step two feet to the side and take another photo. Nearby objects (like a chair right in front of you) will appear to shift a lot between the two photos. Far-away objects (like a picture on the wall across the room) barely shift at all. This "things at different distances shift by different amounts when you move" effect is called **parallax** — it's the same reason that when you're in a moving car, the fence right next to the road blurs past while the mountains in the distance barely seem to move. Because near and far objects shift by different, depth-dependent amounts, there is no single simple 2D formula that correctly maps *every* pixel from photo A to photo B. The mapping would need to know the exact 3D distance to every single point in the scene, which a flat 2D photo doesn't tell you.

BUT — and this is the whole point of the lesson — there are two special situations where a single, simple formula *does* work exactly, no matter how complicated the 3D scene is:

1. **The scene is flat (planar).** If you're photographing a whiteboard, a painting, a piece of paper, or a building facade — basically anything flat — then every point in the scene lies on the same plane, so there's no "different depths" for things to disagree about. One formula handles the whole image.
2. **The camera only rotates in place, without moving sideways or forward/backward.** Picture standing in one exact spot and just turning your body (or turning your phone) to look left, then right, then up — like a security camera on a swivel mount. If the camera's optical center (the tiny point inside the lens where light rays converge — think of it as the "eye" of the camera) never physically moves through space, then even though the room is full of objects at wildly different distances, there is *still* a single simple formula relating any two of your photos. Why? Because parallax is specifically caused by the camera's position changing. If the position never changes — only the direction it's pointing — there's no parallax to worry about, regardless of how deep or shallow the scene is.

The magic formula that works in both of these special cases is called a **homography**. It's a way of describing "stretch, squash, tilt, and rotate this flat image so it lines up with that flat image" using a single small grid of 9 numbers (a 3×3 matrix — we'll build up to exactly what that means).

Two very practical consequences of this idea, which you've probably experienced without knowing the math behind them:

- You can take a photo of a whiteboard shot at an angle (so it looks like a slanted trapezoid instead of a neat rectangle) and digitally "unslant" it back into a perfect rectangle. This works because a whiteboard is flat — case 1 above.
- Your phone's panorama mode tells you "pivot slowly, don't walk sideways." That's not an arbitrary instruction — it's forcing you into case 2 above (pure rotation, no sideways movement), which is exactly the condition under which a single simple formula can stitch your photos together perfectly.

### The math, from the ground up

Before we touch homographies, we need one piece of background machinery: a special way of writing down pixel coordinates called **homogeneous coordinates**. Don't worry, it's a small trick, and the payoff is big.

**Why we need a new way to write coordinates at all.** Normally you'd write a pixel's location as $(x, y)$ — column $x$, row $y$. The problem is that a lot of useful image transformations (like the perspective effects we care about) can't be written as simple addition/multiplication formulas on $(x,y)$ directly — they involve *division*, and division doesn't play nicely with the clean matrix-multiplication tools of linear algebra. Homogeneous coordinates are a trick that lets us express those "division" transformations as ordinary matrix multiplication, at the cost of adding one extra number to every point.

**The trick itself.** Instead of writing a point as $(x, y)$, we write it with an extra "1" tacked on: $\tilde{\mathbf{x}} = (x, y, 1)^\top$ (the little tilde just marks "this is the homogeneous version," and the $^\top$ means "written as a column, not a row" — pure notation, don't overthink it). The rule for reading a homogeneous point back into a normal pixel location is: **divide the first two numbers by the third one.** So $(x, y, 1)$ means the pixel $(x/1, y/1) = (x,y)$ — no change yet. But here's the important part: $(2x, 2y, 2)$ *also* means the pixel $(2x/2, 2y/2) = (x, y)$. In other words, **multiplying an entire homogeneous coordinate by any non-zero number doesn't change which real pixel it represents.** This "same point, many representations" property is called being defined **up to scale**, and it is going to be the single most important idea in this whole lesson — it explains almost every strange-looking step later on, so hold onto it.

**Why this matters for cameras specifically:** a camera works by projecting 3D rays through a single point (the lens center) onto a flat sensor. Every point along one of those rays lands on the exact same pixel. Homogeneous coordinates are a natural fit for this because a whole ray of 3D points (which differ from each other only by a scaling factor) can be represented by one homogeneous coordinate.

**Now: what is a homography, concretely?** It is a 3×3 grid of numbers — a matrix — that we multiply by a homogeneous pixel coordinate from image 1 to get (up to scale) the corresponding homogeneous pixel coordinate in image 2:

$$
\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' \simeq H \tilde{\mathbf{x}}, \qquad
H = \begin{bmatrix} h_1 & h_2 & h_3 \\ h_4 & h_5 & h_6 \\ h_7 & h_8 & h_9\end{bmatrix}
$$

Here $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ is a pixel in the first image (in homogeneous form), $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}'$ is the corresponding pixel in the second image, and $H$ is the homography matrix we want to find. The $\simeq$ symbol (instead of a plain $=$) is there deliberately — it means "equal after you allow for the scale trick above," i.e., "equal up to scale." This is not a small technicality we can ignore: it is the defining feature of everything in this section, and we already saw why — homogeneous coordinates only pin down a point up to an unknown multiplying constant, so any formula relating them can only ever claim equality up to that same freedom.

**A direct consequence you should notice immediately:** since $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' \simeq H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$, it's equally true that $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' \simeq (2H)\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ — multiplying the *whole matrix* $H$ by any non-zero number gives you a matrix that does exactly the same job. Two matrices that are just scalar multiples of each other represent the *same* geometric transformation.

**This is where "8 degrees of freedom" comes from.** "Degrees of freedom" (DoF) just means "how many independent numbers do you actually need to specify, to pin down one specific instance of this thing." $H$ has 9 entries written on the page, but because any non-zero overall scaling of the whole matrix gives an identical transformation, one of those 9 numbers is "wasted" — it's not telling you anything new about the geometry, it's just telling you which arbitrary scale someone chose to write the matrix at. So effectively there are only **8 independent numbers** describing a homography, even though the matrix has 9 slots. In practice, people remove this redundancy by picking a convention, e.g. forcing the overall scale so that $\|h\| = 1$ (the 9 numbers, treated as a vector, have length 1) or by fixing $h_9 = 1$ (dividing every entry by whatever $h_9$ happens to be). Either way, you're choosing one representative out of the whole family of matrices that do the same job.

Keep this "8, not 9" fact in your pocket — it is going to explain several otherwise-mysterious steps later (why we need a specific minimum number of point-pairs, and why we solve the equations in an unusual way).

**Writing it out without the homogeneous shorthand.** If you actually do the division-by-the-last-coordinate that homogeneous coordinates require, you get the "real" formula for where a pixel $(x,y)$ in image 1 goes in image 2:

$$
x' = \frac{h_1 x + h_2 y + h_3}{h_7 x + h_8 y + h_9}, \qquad y' = \frac{h_4 x + h_5 y + h_6}{h_7 x + h_8 y + h_9}
$$

Notice both $x'$ and $y'$ are divided by the *same* denominator, $h_7 x + h_8 y + h_9$. That denominator is the reason this transformation is called **projective** rather than something simpler. Concretely, it's what produces the "perspective" look — for example, two parallel railway tracks that appear to converge to a point on the horizon in a photo. If that denominator were forced to always equal 1 (i.e., if $h_7 = h_8 = 0$ and $h_9 = 1$), the division would vanish and parallel lines in the world would stay perfectly parallel in the image — no convergence, no perspective effect. Real cameras do produce this convergence effect, which is exactly why we need the more general "there's a denominator" family of transformations to describe what real photographs do.

**A ladder of transformations, from simplest to most general.** It helps enormously to see the homography as the top of a ladder of increasingly powerful (and increasingly numerous-in-parameters) 2D transformations. Each rung can do everything the rungs below it can do, plus something more:

| Transform | How many numbers needed (DoF) | What it can never change about a shape | How it's written as a matrix |
|---|---|---|---|
| Translation (sliding) | 2 | Everything except position — shape, size, angle, orientation all stay identical | $[I \mid \mathbf{t}]$ — identity matrix plus a shift vector $\mathbf{t}$ |
| Euclidean / rigid (sliding + rotating) | 3 | Lengths, angles, areas | $[R \mid \mathbf{t}]$ — a rotation matrix $R$ plus a shift $\mathbf{t}$ |
| Similarity (sliding + rotating + uniform resizing) | 4 | Angles, and the ratio between any two lengths | $[sR \mid \mathbf{t}]$ — rotation scaled by a single number $s$, plus a shift |
| Affine (also allows stretching differently in different directions, and shearing/slanting) | 6 | Parallel lines stay parallel; ratios of lengths *along the same line* stay fixed; the centroid (average position) of a set of points maps to the centroid of the mapped points | $[A \mid \mathbf{t}]$ where $A$ is any 2×2 matrix, with an implicit bottom row $(0,0,1)$ |
| **Projective (homography)** | **8** | **Straight lines stay straight (that's it — parallel lines can now converge); a technical quantity called the "cross-ratio" of four collinear points is preserved** | The full 3×3 matrix, with no restrictions on the bottom row |

Read this table as a story: as you go down the rows, you allow more and more distortion, and correspondingly you *lose* more guarantees about what stays fixed, but you *gain* more descriptive power. A homography is the most powerful of these five — it's the only one that can capture true perspective (the bottom row of the matrix is "free," which is exactly what creates that division-by-something-that-depends-on-$x,y$ we saw above). Affine and below all have a fixed, boring bottom row of $(0,0,1)$, which is precisely why they can *never* produce a division that depends on where the point is — no perspective effect is possible for them, ever.

**Now: how do we actually find $H$ from real photos?**

Here's the setup: we've already run a feature detector (like SIFT or ORB from earlier lessons) on both photos, found matching points between them, and cleaned out the bad matches using RANSAC. So we have a list of pairs — "this pixel in photo 1 corresponds to this pixel in photo 2" — and we want to compute the one 3×3 matrix $H$ that best explains all of these pairs simultaneously. This computation method is called the **Direct Linear Transform**, or **DLT** for short. Let's build it up from first principles.

**Step 1 — state the problem.** We want $H$ such that $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' \simeq H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ for every matched pair $(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}')$ we have. We'd love this to be a "linear" problem, meaning one we can solve with standard linear-algebra tools (which are fast, exact, and don't require guessing a starting point), rather than an "iterative" problem where a computer has to repeatedly guess-and-check its way toward an answer.

**Step 2 — the obstacle: that pesky $\simeq$.** The symbol $\simeq$ is not a normal "equals," remember — it means "equal, up to some unknown scaling factor." If we tried to write this literally as an equation, we'd have to say $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' = \lambda_i \, H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$, where $\lambda_i$ is some unknown number (a different, unknown one for *every single point pair* $i$, since each point could in principle be represented at a different arbitrary scale). That's bad news: every new point pair we add doesn't just give us new information about $H$, it also drags in a brand-new unknown number $\lambda_i$ that we don't care about and would also have to solve for. This would make the problem much bigger and messier than it needs to be.

**Step 3 — the trick that removes the unknown scale.** We need a way to say "these two vectors point in the same direction" *without* having to write down what the scaling factor between them is. Luckily, there's a classic tool from vector algebra for exactly this: the **cross product**. If you haven't seen it: the cross product of two 3D vectors $\mathbf{a} \times \mathbf{b}$ produces a new vector that is zero (the all-zeros vector) exactly when $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$ point in the same or opposite direction (i.e., are "parallel," which includes being scaled versions of each other) — and is non-zero otherwise. Crucially, the cross product doesn't need to know the scaling factor at all — it only cares about *direction*, not magnitude. So instead of writing the messy $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' = \lambda_i H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$, we can write:

$$\tilde{\mathbf{x}}' \times H\tilde{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{0}$$

This says exactly the same geometric thing ("$\tilde{\mathbf{x}}'$ and $H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ point in the same direction") but now it's phrased as a genuine equation — with a real, unambiguous "equals zero" on the right — and it contains no unknown $\lambda_i$ anywhere. And here's the crucial bonus: this equation is *still linear in the unknown entries of $H$*. Why? Because (a) the cross product is a linear operation in each of its two arguments — doubling one of the vectors doubles the cross product — and (b) $H\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ is itself linear in the entries of $H$ (it's just a matrix-vector multiplication, i.e., sums of the entries of $H$ each multiplied by a fixed known number from $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$). So we've achieved something valuable: we removed the scale ambiguity completely, and we didn't have to pay for it by introducing any non-linear terms. This is the entire clever idea behind the DLT — everything after this is just bookkeeping.

**Step 4 — how many actual equations does one point-pair give us?** The cross product of two 3D vectors has three numbers in it (it's itself a 3D vector), so naively you might think each matched point pair gives us 3 separate equations. But it turns out only **2 of those 3 are independent** (meaning: mathematically new information) — the third is automatically satisfied once the other two are. Here's the reasoning, without needing to grind through algebra: a cross product $\mathbf{a} \times \mathbf{b}$ is, by its very definition, always perpendicular to *both* $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$. Being perpendicular to two given vectors is itself one algebraic constraint linking the three output components together — so the three numbers in any cross product can never be fully independent of each other; there's always one built-in relationship among them. Put differently: saying "these two 3D directions are parallel" is fundamentally a statement about matching up *two* independent angular degrees of freedom (think of a direction in 3D space as needing two numbers to specify, like latitude and longitude on a globe) — it's not a three-number statement, even though we wrote it using a three-component cross product.

Writing out two of the three rows of this equation explicitly (the third being redundant, as just explained):

$$
\begin{bmatrix}
\mathbf{0}^\top & -w'\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^\top & y'\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^\top \\
w'\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^\top & \mathbf{0}^\top & -x'\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^\top
\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{h}^1\\ \mathbf{h}^2 \\ \mathbf{h}^3\end{bmatrix} = \mathbf{0}
$$

Here $\mathbf{h}^1, \mathbf{h}^2, \mathbf{h}^3$ are just the three rows of $H$, each written out as a little 3-number chunk — $\mathbf{h}$ overall is what you get if you take the whole matrix $H$ and "unroll" it into one long column of 9 numbers, stacked row after row. We're allowed to treat a matrix as if it were just a plain list (vector) of 9 unknown numbers here precisely *because* the equation we derived is linear in those 9 numbers — linear equations don't care whether you arrange your unknowns in a grid or a single column, the algebra is the same either way. $w'$ here is just the third (homogeneous) coordinate of $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}'$ — usually 1, but written generally.

**Step 5 — how many point-pairs do we need?** Every matched point-pair contributes 2 independent equations (from Step 4). We have 8 unknown numbers to solve for in $H$ (from the DoF discussion above). Basic linear algebra says you generally need at least as many independent equations as unknowns to pin down a solution. So: $8 \text{ unknowns} \div 2 \text{ equations per point} = 4$ points. That's why **4 matched point-pairs** is the minimum needed to compute a homography — provided those 4 points are in "general position," meaning no 3 of them lie on the same straight line (we'll come back to why that caveat matters). This is exactly why, if you recall RANSAC from the last lesson, the "minimal sample size" $s$ used when fitting a homography inside RANSAC is $s = 4$.

Stack up the two-equations-per-point contributions from all $n$ of your matched pairs (using $n \ge 4$, and typically many more than 4, since real matches are noisy) into one big table of numbers called $A$ (with $2n$ rows and 9 columns), and the whole problem becomes: solve $A\mathbf{h} = \mathbf{0}$ for the unknown 9-number vector $\mathbf{h}$.

**Step 6 — solving $A\mathbf{h} = \mathbf{0}$.** This looks like it should be an easy linear algebra problem, but there's a trap. Notice that $\mathbf{h} = \mathbf{0}$ (all 9 unknowns equal to zero) makes the equation $A\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{0}$ trivially, perfectly true — but a homography made of all zeros is useless garbage; it doesn't represent any real transformation. So we can't just ask "solve $A\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{0}$" as-is, because the *most correct-looking* answer is a solution we explicitly don't want.

We fix this using the same scale-ambiguity fact from Step 3 of our earlier reasoning: any non-zero rescaling of the true $\mathbf{h}$ is an equally valid answer, so nothing is lost by *requiring* $\mathbf{h}$ to have a specific fixed length — say, length exactly 1 (written $\|\mathbf{h}\|=1$, meaning if you squared every entry and added them all up, you'd get 1). This single requirement does two things at once: it rules out the useless all-zeros answer (which has length 0, not 1), and it pins down which one specific representative we want out of the whole family of equally-valid rescaled versions of $H$.

With real, noisy, imperfect matched points, $A\mathbf{h}$ generally can't be made *exactly* zero for any choice of $\mathbf{h}$ — there's always some leftover error. So the real question becomes: **out of all the possible unit-length vectors $\mathbf{h}$, which one makes $A\mathbf{h}$ as close to zero as possible** (i.e., minimizes $\|A\mathbf{h}\|$, the length of the leftover-error vector)?

**Step 7 — why the answer is found using the SVD.** The Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) is a standard way of breaking any matrix $A$ into three simpler pieces: $A = U \Sigma V^\top$. You don't need the full theory of the SVD to follow the argument — you just need these facts about it: $U$ and $V$ are "rotation-like" matrices (technically "orthogonal") which means multiplying by them never stretches or shrinks a vector's length, only rotates/reflects its direction. $\Sigma$ is a diagonal matrix (numbers only on the main diagonal, zero everywhere else) whose diagonal entries $\sigma_1 \ge \sigma_2 \ge \dots \ge \sigma_9 \ge 0$ are called the "singular values" of $A$, sorted from biggest to smallest — think of them as measuring how much $A$ stretches vectors along 9 special, mutually-perpendicular directions.

Because $U$ preserves lengths (it's just a rotation), we know $\|A\mathbf{h}\| = \|U\Sigma V^\top \mathbf{h}\| = \|\Sigma V^\top \mathbf{h}\|$ — we can drop the $U$ entirely from our minimization problem since it never changes the length we're trying to minimize.

Now define a new variable $\mathbf{y} = V^\top \mathbf{h}$. Since $V^\top$ is also just a rotation, it preserves length too, so if $\mathbf{h}$ has length 1, then $\mathbf{y}$ also has length 1 — we've just relabeled our unknown, we haven't changed the problem's difficulty. Our minimization is now: minimize $\|\Sigma \mathbf{y}\|$ over unit-length $\mathbf{y}$. Since $\Sigma$ is diagonal, multiplying it by $\mathbf{y}$ just scales each coordinate of $\mathbf{y}$ by the corresponding singular value: $\Sigma\mathbf{y} = (\sigma_1 y_1, \sigma_2 y_2, \dots, \sigma_9 y_9)$, so its length is $\sqrt{\sum_i \sigma_i^2 y_i^2}$.

We need to choose the unit-length vector $\mathbf{y}$ (i.e., $\sum_i y_i^2 = 1$) that makes $\sum_i \sigma_i^2 y_i^2$ as small as possible. Since the $y_i^2$ values must add up to 1, you can think of them as "weights" being distributed across the 9 singular values, and we're computing a weighted average of the $\sigma_i^2$ values using those weights. To make a weighted average as small as possible, common sense says: put *all* the weight on the smallest number available and none anywhere else. That means the best choice is $y_9 = 1$ (all the "weight" on the smallest singular value $\sigma_9$) and every other $y_i = 0$.

Translating $\mathbf{y} = (0,\dots,0,1)$ back through $\mathbf{y} = V^\top \mathbf{h}$, this means $\mathbf{h}$ must be the corresponding column of $V$ — specifically, the column of $V$ that corresponds to the *smallest* singular value $\sigma_9$. This column has a name: it's called the **last right singular vector of $A$** (or equivalently, in software, "the last column of $V$" once the singular values are sorted from largest to smallest, which most SVD implementations do automatically). We then just reshape that 9-number column back into a 3×3 grid to recover our homography matrix $H$.

**What this "recipe" actually means, intuitively.** Don't memorize "take the last column of $V$" as an arbitrary magic incantation. What it's really telling you is: **the direction in which $A$ shrinks vectors the most (smallest singular value) is the direction that is closest to being in $A$'s "blind spot"** — the technical term for this blind spot is the **null space** of $A$: the set of vectors that $A$ maps to exactly zero. If our matched points were perfectly noise-free and perfectly consistent with some exact homography, the true $\mathbf{h}$ would land *exactly* in that blind spot, and its corresponding singular value would be exactly 0. With real, slightly-noisy data, the true answer isn't exactly in the blind spot anymore, but it's still the *closest possible thing* to it — and that's precisely what "the direction with smallest singular value" captures.

**A numerical landmine: Hartley normalization — do not skip this step.**

Before you actually run the DLT on real pixel coordinates, there is a preprocessing step you must not skip, or your answer will be garbage even though your math above was completely correct.

The step is: before building the matrix $A$, transform the pixel coordinates in *each* image so that:
1. Their average position (centroid) sits exactly at the origin $(0,0)$, and
2. The average distance of the points from the origin is $\sqrt{2}$ (which works out to mean the "typical" point is now sitting around $(1,1)$).

Then, after solving for the homography $\hat{H}$ using these rescaled, recentered coordinates, you convert the answer back to work on ordinary pixel coordinates via $\tilde{H} = T'^{-1} \hat{H} T$, where $T$ and $T'$ are the specific little matrices that describe the normalization we applied to each image ("apply the shift-and-rescale to the points, solve the easy version of the problem, then undo the shift-and-rescale on the resulting matrix").

**Why does this matter so much? Let's actually see the mechanism, not just take it on faith.**

Look at what actually goes inside a row of the matrix $A$ we built above. It contains entries that are individual pixel coordinates, like $x$, which for a typical modern photo (say, 4000 pixels wide) can be a number up to around $4000 = 4\times10^3$. It *also* contains entries that are *products* of two coordinates, like $x' \cdot x$, which can be as large as $(4\times10^3)\times(4\times10^3) \approx 1.6\times10^7$. And it *also* contains plain constants like the homogeneous "1." So within a single row of $A$, you have numbers ranging from about $1$ up to about $10^7$ — a spread of **seven orders of magnitude** sitting side by side in the same matrix.

Why is this dangerous? Computers store numbers with limited precision — a standard 32-bit floating point number (float32) can reliably represent only about 7 significant decimal digits, and even a 64-bit float (float64) only manages about 16. When numbers of wildly different sizes are mixed together in the same computation, the tiny numbers effectively get "swallowed" — imagine trying to add $0.0000001$ to $10{,}000{,}000$ using a calculator that only keeps 7 digits: the small number simply vanishes, rounded away as if it weren't there.

There's a formal way to measure "how badly could this kind of rounding mess up my SVD answer," called the **condition number**, written $\kappa(A) = \sigma_{\max}/\sigma_{\min}$ — literally, the ratio of the biggest singular value to the smallest one. Why does this specific ratio matter here of all places? Because remember: our *entire answer* $\mathbf{h}$ comes from the singular vector corresponding to the *smallest* singular value, $\sigma_{\min}$. If $\sigma_{\min}$ is tiny compared to the general size of numbers floating around in $A$ (measured by $\sigma_{\max}$), then rounding error — which behaves roughly like it perturbs every entry of $A$ by an amount proportional to $\varepsilon \cdot \sigma_{\max}$, where $\varepsilon$ is the smallest gap floating-point numbers can represent — can be *larger* than $\sigma_{\min}$ itself. When your rounding noise is bigger than the very quantity you're trying to precisely extract, the direction you compute as "the smallest" is essentially just... whichever direction the rounding noise happened to nudge things toward. Your carefully-derived math gives you an answer that's really just numerical garbage. As a rule of thumb, you lose about $\log_{10}\kappa(A)$ significant decimal digits of accuracy to this effect — and remember you only had about 7 (float32) or 16 (float64) to begin with.

**Let's plug in real numbers to see how bad it actually gets.** Without normalization, on a normal digital photo, $\kappa(A)$ typically comes out around $10^6$ to $10^8$. That means you lose 6 to 8 significant digits — in float32 (7 digits total) that's *all of your precision, and then some*; even float64 (16 digits) is left badly degraded. After Hartley normalization, every coordinate is scaled down to be roughly of size 1 (that's the whole point of the "average distance $\sqrt2$ from the origin" rule), every product of two coordinates is also roughly size 1, and every entry of $A$ ends up roughly the same order of magnitude. This typically shrinks $\kappa(A)$ down to around $O(10)$ — you lose maybe one measly digit instead of your entire precision budget.
