Hartley showed this single preprocessing trick is the difference between the DLT being *practically unusable* and being a reliable, everyday algorithm — and notice that the underlying math (Steps 1–7 above) was completely correct the entire time; the failure, when it happens, is a *pure numerics* problem, invisible if you only look at the formulas on paper. This is a genuinely high-value thing to know cold, because most people who can recite the DLT derivation have never heard of this step, let alone why it's needed.

(One small loose end: why $\sqrt2$ specifically, rather than some other target distance? Because it makes the "typical" rescaled point sit at roughly $(1,1)$ — meaning its two coordinate values and the homogeneous "1" that rides along with it are now all roughly the same size. That equal-sizing of all the ingredients that go into $A$ is the entire goal of this whole normalization step.)

**A second, completely separate problem: algebraic error vs. geometric error.**

Even once you've fixed the numerical precision issue with Hartley normalization, there's a deeper conceptual issue with the DLT approach, which has nothing to do with rounding error.

Recall that DLT finds the $\mathbf{h}$ that minimizes $\|A\mathbf{h}\|$. But stop and ask: **what does that number, $\|A\mathbf{h}\|$, actually represent?** It's the size of the leftover error in the cross-product equation we invented back in Step 3 — a mathematical convenience we introduced purely to escape the unknown-scaling problem. It has no natural units — it's not measured in pixels, or millimeters, or anything you could point to on the actual images. Critically, because the cross-product formula multiplies things by each point's own homogeneous coordinates, two different matched-point-pairs that are *equally* wrong in terms of actual, visible pixel misalignment on your screen can end up contributing very *differently*-sized amounts to this algebraic error — the DLT effectively gives some points more "weight" than others for reasons that have nothing to do with how visually wrong they are. So the DLT is optimizing a real, well-defined, closed-form-solvable quantity — but it is quietly the *wrong* quantity if what you actually care about is "how good does the final stitched image look."

**Pause:** if the algebraic error is the wrong objective, why is the DLT used at all?

Because the *right* objective — actual pixel-distance misalignment, called **geometric error** — is a much harder kind of problem mathematically: it's non-convex, meaning it can have multiple "valleys" (local minima), and finding the true best answer requires an iterative numerical optimizer (a method that starts from a guess and repeatedly nudges it to make things a little better, over and over). The catch with any such iterative method on a non-convex problem is that it needs a *starting guess* to begin from, and if that starting guess is bad, the iterative process can easily get trapped in the wrong "valley" — a locally-good-looking but globally-wrong answer — and never find its way out. The DLT, despite optimizing the "wrong" quantity, has one huge practical virtue: it's fast, it's solved in one closed-form step (no iteration, no risk of getting stuck), and its answer, especially with Hartley normalization applied, is reliably close to the true, correct answer. So the DLT's real job in practice is not "give the final answer" — it's "give a good enough starting guess that the slower, more accurate iterative method can polish into the actual best answer." This two-step pattern — fast closed-form approximate answer first, then slow iterative refinement second — shows up again and again throughout computer vision; you'll meet the exact same pattern again later under the name "bundle adjustment."

The two more precise, geometrically-meaningful error measures that the refinement step tries to minimize are:

- **Symmetric transfer error:** add up, over every matched pair $i$, the squared pixel-distance between where $H$ *predicts* point $\mathbf{x}_i$ should land and where $\mathbf{x}_i'$ actually is, *plus* the squared pixel-distance you get doing the same thing in reverse (using $H^{-1}$, the "undo" version of $H$, to check the other direction too): $\sum_i d(\mathbf{x}_i', H\mathbf{x}_i)^2 + d(\mathbf{x}_i, H^{-1}\mathbf{x}_i')^2$. Here $d(\cdot,\cdot)$ just means "ordinary pixel distance between two points, like you'd measure with a ruler on the image."
- **Reprojection error (considered the gold-standard, most-correct version):** rather than just checking $H$ against the two noisy, imperfect original measurements, this approach also solves for a pair of "corrected," idealized points $\hat{\mathbf{x}}_i$ that are perfectly consistent with some exact homography, and then measures how far the *original noisy measurements* are from those idealized corrected points: $\sum_i d(\mathbf{x}_i, \hat{\mathbf{x}}_i)^2 + d(\mathbf{x}_i', H\hat{\mathbf{x}}_i)^2$.

The standard real-world recipe, then, is: **run DLT with Hartley normalization to get a fast, decent starting estimate of $H$ → then run an iterative optimizer (commonly one called Levenberg–Marquardt, a well-known workhorse algorithm for exactly this kind of "refine a good guess" problem) to polish that estimate by minimizing the true geometric error, using only the "inlier" points RANSAC identified as trustworthy.** This exact division of labor — closed-form initializer, then iterative geometric refinement — is one of the most frequently-reused ideas in all of geometry-based computer vision, and you'll see it again, in a much bigger and more elaborate form, when we get to bundle adjustment later in the course.

### The panorama pipeline, from start to finish

Now that we understand what a homography is and how to compute one from a pair of matched images, let's zoom out and see how a full, real panorama-stitching app (like the one on your phone) strings together everything we've learned in Module 1 so far — feature detection, feature matching, and RANSAC — plus the homography machinery from this lesson, into one complete pipeline.

Here's a useful way to read the list below: every single stage exists because, without it, the *previous* stages leave some specific, visible flaw in the final picture. In particular, the last four stages are all separate answers to the same complaint: "the geometry (the actual alignment) is correct, and the picture still looks wrong." Let's go through them one at a time.

1. **Find and describe distinctive points** (using something like SIFT or ORB, covered in earlier lessons) in every one of your input photos. These are the "landmark" points — corners, blobs, sharp features — that we'll use to figure out how photos relate to each other.
2. **Match points between pairs of photos**, applying the "ratio test" (from the matching lesson) to throw out unreliable matches. If you have a large number of photos $n$ to stitch (say, dozens for a big panoramic mosaic), comparing every photo against every other photo directly would take time proportional to $n^2$, which gets slow fast — so real systems use faster shortcuts (a "vocabulary tree" or similar fast lookup structure) to only compare photos that are actually likely to overlap.
3. **Run RANSAC to fit a homography for each pair of photos** that might overlap, using the DLT + refinement recipe from above. Then there's a practical filtering question: given a pair of photos, how do you decide computationally whether they *actually* overlap in real life, versus just having a few coincidental bad matches? A method by researchers Brown and Lowe answers this with a simple statistical test: count $n_i$, the number of matched points RANSAC accepted as "inliers" (i.e., points consistent with the fitted homography), and $n_f$, the total number of matched features found in the overlapping region. Accept the pair as a genuine overlap only if $n_i > \alpha + \beta n_f$ for some small constants (commonly $\alpha = 8, \beta = 0.3$) — in plain words, "there need to be enough inliers, both in absolute count and as a fraction of the total, before we trust that these two photos really do show the same place."
4. **Jointly refine all photos together using "bundle adjustment."** This means: instead of just stitching photo 1 to photo 2, then photo 2 to photo 3, and so on in a chain, take *all* the photos and *all* the pairwise relationships at once, and adjust every camera's rotation angle and lens "zoom" setting (focal length) simultaneously to make the *total* error across the entire panorama as small as possible. Why is this necessary? If you only ever chain pairwise alignments (align 1→2, then 2→3, then 3→4, ...), any small errors in each individual pairwise alignment slowly accumulate down the chain — like a slightly-off measurement copied many times — and by the time you get to the last photo and try to loop back around to connect with the first one (in a full 360° panorama), the small errors have added up into a big, visible mismatch, and the panorama fails to "close" into a seamless loop. Adjusting everything jointly, all at once, spreads the unavoidable small errors evenly across the whole panorama instead of letting them pile up in one place.
5. **Decide what 3D surface to "wrap" the final image around**, since we're combining images taken while the camera pivoted through different angles, and we need some common flat-ish surface to project them all onto so they line up into one image:
   - **A flat plane.** This is the simplest choice and works fine as long as the total angular sweep of your panorama is under roughly 60–90 degrees. Beyond that, things break down mathematically: think about a ray of light coming into the camera at 90 degrees from straight-ahead (i.e., directly out to the side) — such a ray runs exactly parallel to a flat image plane and mathematically never intersects it at any finite point, meaning its projected position would have to be at infinity. A true 180-degree-wide panorama would therefore require an infinitely wide flat image, which is obviously impossible. This isn't a flaw in any particular algorithm — it's a basic geometric fact about flat planes — so the fix has to be to stop using a flat plane and choose a different-shaped surface to project onto.
   - **A cylinder.** Mathematically, this means transforming each 3D direction $(x,y,z)$ using the formula $(x,y,z) \to (\arctan(x/z),\ y/\sqrt{x^2+z^2})$ — don't worry about memorizing this, the key idea is just "wrap the image around the inside of a cylinder instead of laying it flat," which lets you go all the way around 360° horizontally (like wrapping a label around a soup can) while remaining well-behaved mathematically, as long as you know the camera's focal length (a measure of its zoom/field-of-view).
   - **A sphere.** This lets you cover the full 360° horizontally *and* 180° vertically — a complete "little planet" or full-surround view, useful for things like VR panoramas that need to be viewed by turning your head in any direction.
6. **Straightening the panorama (removing an unwanted overall tilt).** Here's a subtle wrinkle: mathematically, when you piece together rotations, there's an overall "which way is up" freedom left completely unconstrained by the matching process alone — the algorithm has no inherent notion of "level" or "upright," it only knows relative rotations between photos. Brown and Lowe's fix is a clever statistical trick: they look at the direction each individual photo's camera was pointing "sideways" (its local x-axis) across all the photos, and they pick the overall "up" direction to be the one that's least represented among all those sideways directions (technically, the direction that minimizes a quantity called the covariance of those axes). The reasoning behind why this works: ordinary photographers hardly ever deliberately tilt or twist the camera sideways while shooting a panorama — most rotation happens around the vertical axis (panning left-right) — so this statistical trick effectively guesses "up" correctly almost every time by exploiting that common human photography habit.
7. **Exposure / brightness matching (gain compensation).** Different photos in your panorama were very likely taken with slightly different automatic exposure settings — one might be a touch brighter or darker than its neighbor, even though they show overlapping parts of the same scene. This stage solves for one brightness-multiplier number $g_i$ per photo, chosen so that, in the regions where photos overlap, their brightness levels agree with each other as closely as possible.
8. **Finding a "seam" — the boundary line where you switch from using one photo to using the neighboring photo.** In the region where two photos overlap, you have to choose, pixel by pixel, which photo's version to actually display in the final panorama (you can't just show both). Rather than picking an arbitrary straight cut, this stage uses a technique (graph cut, or a related method called dynamic programming — both are optimization techniques for choosing the "cheapest" path through a grid of options) to route the boundary line through regions where the two overlapping photos agree with each other well, and — importantly — to route *around* things like a person who moved between the two shots, rather than slicing directly through them (which would otherwise cut a person's photo in half, one arm from one shot and one arm from the other).
9. **Multi-band blending — smoothing out the seam so it's invisible.**

   This last step deals with a genuine contradiction that's worth spelling out carefully, because it explains why the "obvious" simple fix (just fade smoothly between the two photos near the boundary) doesn't actually work well.

   If you blend the two photos over a *wide* transition zone (fading gradually over, say, 200 pixels), you successfully hide small leftover brightness/exposure differences between the photos — but you introduce a new problem: any small remaining misalignment between the photos (even a pixel or two, which is basically unavoidable in practice) now shows up as a visible **ghost** — a faint double-edge or double-outline — because both photos are contributing real detail across that whole wide strip, and if they don't line up perfectly, you see both versions overlapping like a double-exposure photograph.

   If instead you blend over a *narrow* transition (fading sharply over just a few pixels), fine detail stays crisp and there's no ghosting — but now any leftover exposure/brightness difference between the two photos shows up as an obvious, hard visible seam line, because the transition happens too fast to hide the mismatch.

   So: wide transition hides brightness differences but reveals misalignment ghosting; narrow transition hides ghosting but reveals brightness seams. **One single transition width cannot satisfy both requirements at once** — that's the contradiction.

   The clever resolution is to notice that these two problems live at *different spatial frequencies*. "Frequency" here, borrowed from signal processing, means how quickly the image's brightness changes as you move across it — a "low-frequency" pattern changes slowly and smoothly across a wide area (like a gradual light-to-dark gradient across a whole wall), while a "high-frequency" pattern changes rapidly over a short distance (like the crisp edge of a chair's outline). Exposure/brightness mismatches are inherently *low-frequency* phenomena — they vary slowly and smoothly. Ghosting artifacts, on the other hand, are inherently *high-frequency* — they show up as sharp double-edges. Since these two problems live at different frequencies, we can solve them independently by *decomposing* each photo into a stack of different frequency "layers" (a technique called a **Laplacian pyramid**, which you may recall from the scale-space/pyramid lesson earlier in this module — the same mathematical construction used there to help *detect* good keypoints at various scales is reused here for an entirely different purpose: *representing* an image as separate frequency layers) — and then blend each layer with its *own*, individually-chosen transition width, roughly doubling as you go from high-frequency layers to low-frequency ones (written as transition width $\propto 2^k$ for pyramid level $k$).

   In practice this means: the low-frequency layers (which capture broad brightness/color trends) get blended over a wide transition, successfully hiding exposure differences without causing visible ghosting (since there's no fine detail at that layer to ghost in the first place). The high-frequency layers (which capture fine, sharp detail) get blended over a narrow transition, avoiding ghosting (since there's no room for double-edges to spread out) while whatever brightness mismatch might exist at that fine level is invisible anyway once the low-frequency layers have already fixed the overall brightness. You then simply add all the separately-blended layers back together to get the final, seamless image. This single step is widely considered the most important one for final visual quality in the entire pipeline, and it's a beautiful direct reuse of the scale-space/pyramid idea from earlier in the module — proof that the same mathematical tool can serve two completely different jobs (detecting features vs. blending images) depending on how you use it.

**When does this whole approach break down?** Remember from the Intuition section: a single homography $H$ is only an *exact* description of the relationship between two photos when either (a) the scene being photographed is flat, or (b) the camera underwent **pure rotation about its own optical center**, with zero sideways/forward movement. If the camera *does* move sideways or forward even a little, and the scene is *not* flat, you get parallax (near and far objects shifting by different amounts, as we discussed at the very start) — and no single homography can correctly describe what happened. The visible symptom is ghosting or misalignment specifically in the parts of the overlap where objects are close to the camera (since parallax effects are strongest for near objects) — exactly the "doubled chair in the foreground" scenario the mastery check below asks you to diagnose. This is precisely why panorama apps instruct you to pivot the phone in place around a fixed point, rather than physically walking sideways while shooting — they're trying to keep you inside the "pure rotation" special case where the whole approach remains exact. Modern research has developed workarounds for when you can't guarantee pure rotation — techniques with names like "as-projective-as-possible" (APAP) warping or mesh-based/dual-homography warping, which allow different parts of the image to use slightly different, spatially-varying transformations instead of forcing one single rigid homography onto the whole photo — or, in the most demanding cases, abandoning the single-homography approach altogether and doing full 3D scene reconstruction (an approach called Structure from Motion, which we'll cover in a later module).

### Connecting the dots

The homography matrix $H$ you learned to compute in this lesson isn't a one-off trick — it will reappear later in the course, in the module on 3D geometry, in two different guises: first, as one specific member of a broader family of transformations called the "projective transformation group," and second, in a more specific form, $H = K'(R - \mathbf{t}\mathbf{n}^\top/d)K^{-1}$, that describes exactly how two camera views of the same flat surface relate to each other in full 3D terms (don't worry about parsing every symbol in that formula now — it will be built up properly when we get there). That second appearance connects directly back to something you already learned in the RANSAC lesson: when trying to fit a different, more general geometric relationship (called the "fundamental matrix," used for scenes that are *not* flat), a genuinely flat scene causes that fitting process to become mathematically under-determined — and now you can see *why*, using the language of this lesson: a flat scene is exactly the situation where "depth" stops being a meaningful variable, so any method that relies on depth variation to work has nothing to grab onto. The overall recipe you learned here — get a fast, rough, closed-form answer first (DLT via SVD), then refine it with an iterative optimizer (Levenberg–Marquardt) — is also a template you'll see reused, in bigger and more elaborate forms, for a related 3D-geometry technique called the "8-point algorithm," and again for the "bundle adjustment" technique mentioned earlier in this lesson.

**In your own words:** why does taking a cross product let you write a linear equation for $H$, and why is the answer a singular vector rather than a least-squares solution?

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

If you only remember six things from this entire lesson, make them these — they're the details that separate someone who has *memorized* homographies from someone who actually *understands* them:

1. **Hartley normalization, and being able to explain *why* it's needed** (the conditioning/precision argument, not just "it's good practice"). This is probably the single highest-value fact in this whole lesson — most people who can recite the DLT have never heard of this step.
2. **A homography has 8 degrees of freedom, not 9** — and being able to explain, out loud, that this is *because* of the scale ambiguity, and that this exact fact is *why* we end up solving a homogeneous system ($A\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{0}$) with the SVD instead of an ordinary least-squares solve.
3. **The two, and only two, exact-validity conditions** (the scene is planar, OR the camera undergoes pure rotation with no translation) stated crisply and confidently — and being able to correctly name "parallax" as the specific failure mode when neither condition holds.
4. **The distinction between algebraic error and geometric error**, and understanding that the DLT is only ever meant to be a fast *initializer*, never the final answer.
5. **Recognizing multi-band blending as a direct application of Laplacian pyramids** from the scale-space lesson earlier in the module. Spontaneously connecting these two topics, without being prompted, is exactly the kind of "I see how the whole subject fits together" synthesis that makes an interviewer sit up and pay attention.
6. **Degenerate configurations:** if any 3 of your 4 minimal sample points happen to lie on (or very near) the same straight line, the matrix $A$ becomes "rank-deficient" (loses independent information) and there is no longer a unique, well-determined homography that fits — a good RANSAC implementation should cheaply check for and reject such unlucky samples *before* wasting time trying to fit a homography to them.

### ✅ Mastery check

**(a)** You have 4 exact correspondences and compute $H$ via DLT without normalization, in an image of size $4000\times3000$. Estimate the order of magnitude of the condition number of $A$ and explain the mechanism.
**(b)** Your panorama of a room stitches perfectly on the far wall but shows a doubled chair in the foreground. Diagnose precisely and give two fixes at different points in the pipeline.
**(c)** How many correspondences are needed for an affine transform, and why is the linear system easier?

<details><summary>Answer sketch</summary>
(a) The rows of $A$ contain a mix of very differently-sized numbers: products of two coordinates like $x' \cdot x \sim (4\times10^3) \times (4\times10^3) \approx 1.6\times10^7$, single coordinates like $x \sim 4\times10^3$, and the constant $1$. So the range of sizes present in $A$ spans from about $10^0$ up to about $10^7$, which means the condition number $\kappa(A)$ comes out somewhere around $10^6$ to $10^8$. In float32 (which reliably holds only about 7 significant digits), the singular vector corresponding to the smallest singular value — which is exactly the vector we need for our answer — ends up dominated by rounding noise rather than real signal, so the resulting $H$ is essentially garbage. Even in float64 (about 16 digits), the accuracy loss is severe. Hartley normalization rescales every coordinate to be roughly size 1, which brings $\kappa(A)$ down to roughly $O(10)$ and fixes the problem.
(b) The far wall is roughly flat (planar) and far from the camera, so a single homography $H$ describes it well. The foreground chair sits at a very different distance from the camera than the wall does — so if the camera moved sideways or forward at all between the two shots (rather than purely rotating in place), the resulting parallax means the chair needs a *different* homography than the wall does to align correctly, and no single $H$ can satisfy both at once. RANSAC, when fitting one homography to the whole overlap region, naturally settles on the transformation that fits the dominant, larger area (the wall), leaving the chair mismatched — which shows up as a ghosted, doubled appearance. Two fixes at different points in the pipeline: (i) at the *capture* stage — physically rotate the camera about its own optical center (the "nodal point" inside the lens) rather than moving the whole camera sideways, which eliminates the parallax-causing camera translation at the source, and is the most fundamentally correct fix; (ii) at the *algorithm* stage — instead of forcing one single global homography onto the whole image pair, use a spatially-varying warp (such as the APAP or mesh-warping techniques mentioned above) that can adapt differently in different parts of the image, or use seam-finding (graph cut) to deliberately route the blending seam *around* the chair so that only one of the two photos contributes pixels there at all, combined with multi-band blending to make that rerouted seam invisible. The deepest-level answer: fundamentally, a single homography is simply the *wrong model* for a 3D scene that has real depth variation and where the camera moved — the fully correct model would require actual 3D reconstruction (Structure from Motion plus real depth information), and ordinary panorama stitching is really just a practical decision to accept the "wrong" model in situations where its error happens to be small enough not to matter.
(c) An affine transform has 6 degrees of freedom, and each matched point-pair still contributes 2 equations (the same reasoning as for the homography), so $6 \div 2 = $ **3 correspondences** are needed. The linear system is easier to solve than the homography case because affine transformations always have a fixed bottom row of $(0,0,1)$, meaning the "denominator" that caused all the trouble for homographies is always forced to equal exactly 1. With no division by a variable quantity, the equations relating input points to output points come out *linear and inhomogeneous* — meaning they take the ordinary, friendly form $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ (with a genuine, non-zero right-hand side) rather than the trickier homogeneous form $A\mathbf{h}=\mathbf{0}$ we had to wrestle with above. An ordinary $A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b}$ system can be solved directly with standard least-squares methods, with no scale ambiguity to worry about and no need for the whole SVD/null-space maneuver we used for homographies.
</details>

### 🔨 Build + read

**Build:** Implement a function `dlt_homography(pts1, pts2, normalize=True)` that takes matched point pairs from two images and returns the homography matrix. Then run a direct comparison: compute the homography both *with* and *without* Hartley normalization, using real pixel coordinates from an actual photo pair (not tiny toy numbers), and measure the reprojection error (how far off, in pixels, the predicted points land from the real ones) for each version. You should see a dramatic, very visible difference between the two — this is the experiment that makes the whole conditioning argument click. Once that's working, build a complete **two-image panorama pipeline end to end**: run SIFT to find features → match with the ratio test → run your own RANSAC (from the previous lesson) to find inliers → run your own DLT to get an initial homography → refine it with Levenberg–Marquardt (you can use `scipy.optimize.least_squares` minimizing the symmetric transfer error) → warp one image into the other's frame → blend the overlap using a **multi-band blend with a 5-level Laplacian pyramid**. Finally, compare your multi-band-blended result side by side against a naive simple alpha (straight linear fade) blend — the difference in seam visibility is the whole payoff of this exercise, and seeing it with your own eyes will cement why the frequency-splitting idea matters.

**Read:** Hartley & Zisserman, *Multiple View Geometry*, sections 4.1 through 4.4 (covering the DLT, normalization, and the "gold standard" algorithm) — it's genuinely worth reading properly rather than skimming; it's only about 15 pages, and it's the foundation for essentially everything in the later 3D-geometry module of this course. After that, read Brown & Lowe's paper "Automatic Panoramic Image Stitching using Invariant Features" (IJCV, 2007), which walks through a complete, real, production-grade version of the whole pipeline described above in one place. Finally, Burt & Adelson's original 1983 paper is the classic reference for the multi-band blending technique.

---

## 1.10 Where Classical CV Works vs. Fails — Synthesis

### The honest verdict

Let's start with the question an interviewer is really asking when they bring this up, even if they phrase it differently: *"Deep learning clearly won at recognizing things in images — so why are we still teaching you Harris corners and RANSAC and homographies? Why didn't all of Module 1 just become obsolete?"*

That is a completely fair question, and it deserves a real answer, not a hand-wave. Here it is.

"Computer vision" is not one problem. It is two very different problems that happen to share the word "vision."

**Problem type 1: inference from evidence.** This is questions like "is there a pedestrian in this image?" There is no formula for "pedestrian-ness." You cannot write down an equation that takes pixel values and outputs "yes, human" or "no, fire hydrant." The only way anyone has ever solved this is by showing a system thousands of examples and letting it find the pattern statistically. That is exactly what a neural network is built for — learning a mapping from data because no closed-form mapping exists. Think of it like teaching a child to recognize a dog: you don't hand them a dog-detection formula, you point at hundreds of dogs and non-dogs until they generalize.

**Problem type 2: measurement.** This is questions like "how far away is that wall?" or "where exactly was the camera standing when it took this photo?" These are not guessing games. Given the right inputs (pixel positions of matching points, known lens properties), there is a mathematically correct, precisely computable answer, derived from the geometry of how light travels in straight lines through a lens onto a sensor (this is called *projective geometry* — the study of how 3D points get squashed onto a 2D image plane). It's the same kind of certainty you get from triangulating your position using two known landmarks and a protractor: this is a computation, not a guess.

So here is the answer, stated plainly: **learning is the right tool for problem type 1, and it has mostly not displaced classical geometric methods for problem type 2.** A neural network *can* approximate a distance or a camera position, but it does so by guessing based on patterns it saw in training data — it has no guarantee of being exactly right, no way to report "I am confident to within 2mm," and no way to exploit the fact that the underlying problem is actually pure algebra and optimization with a known-correct solution. Classical geometric methods, by contrast, are *designed* to be exactly right (up to numerical precision), because they are literally solving the geometry equation rather than guessing an approximation to it.

This is why classical computer vision (everything you learned in Module 1 — filtering, edges, scale-space, corners, keypoints, matching, RANSAC, homographies) is not "the old, outdated way of doing vision." It is **the toolkit for the measurement half of vision**, and it lives on, side-by-side with deep learning, inside essentially every serious production vision system you'll encounter — self-driving cars, AR apps, robots, phone cameras.

Below are two tables. The first lists situations where the classical toolkit wins; the second lists situations where it loses badly to learned (deep-learning) approaches. As you read each row, try to classify it yourself first: is this row about *measurement* (classical territory) or about *inference/semantics* (learning territory)? That's the single mental test an interviewer wants to see you apply instinctively.

**Classical wins when:**

| Situation | Why |
|---|---|
| **Metric geometry is the output** (pose, depth, calibration, 3D reconstruction) | "Metric geometry" just means: the answer is a number with real physical units — a distance in meters, an angle in degrees, a camera's exact position. These are well-posed algebra/optimization problems (meaning: plug in the right equations and there's one correct answer to converge to), so a network can only ever approximate that answer, with no guarantee of how close it got. COLMAP (a widely-used open-source Structure-from-Motion tool — "Structure from Motion" means reconstructing a 3D scene from a set of 2D photos) is still the default choice for this task in 2026, precisely because it solves the geometry directly instead of guessing it. |
| **Sub-pixel accuracy required** | "Sub-pixel" means being more precise than the size of one pixel — e.g., saying a corner is at pixel (100.37, 52.81) rather than just (100, 53). Classical checkerboard corner refinement (used in camera calibration, where you photograph a black-and-white checkerboard pattern to work out your camera's internal lens properties) beats any learned detector here, because it's directly fitting a mathematical model to intensity gradients rather than pattern-matching against training examples. |
| **No training data / no GPU / hard latency budget** | "Latency budget" means a strict limit on how long you're allowed to take per computation — e.g., a drone that must react within 30 milliseconds. ORB-SLAM3 (a classical real-time localization-and-mapping system — SLAM stands for Simultaneous Localization And Mapping, meaning figuring out where you are while also building a map of your surroundings, like a robot vacuum does) runs at 30+ frames per second on an ordinary CPU (no graphics card needed) and needs zero training data to start working, because it's built from fixed mathematical rules, not learned weights. |
| **Controlled environment** (factory inspection, document scanning, fiducial markers) | A "fiducial marker" is a specially designed pattern (like a QR code or an AR tag) placed in the scene specifically to be easy to detect — you design the marker to be easy, rather than trying to detect arbitrary real-world objects. When the environment is controlled (fixed lighting, fixed camera angle, known objects), the simple, hand-designed rules of classical CV are enough, and — importantly — you can inspect and prove exactly why they work, which matters a lot in regulated industries. |
| **Interpretability / certification matters** | "Interpretable" means you can explain, step by step, exactly why the system produced its answer — with RANSAC (a classical robust-fitting algorithm covered in 1.8) plus Levenberg–Marquardt (a classical numerical optimization method, often abbreviated "LM"), you can mathematically prove properties like "this will converge" or "this error bound holds." You cannot currently write an equivalent mathematical proof for what a trained neural network (like a ResNet) will do on inputs it hasn't seen. That distinction matters enormously in medicine, aerospace, and self-driving-car safety certification, where regulators want provable guarantees, not just "it worked on our test set." |

**Classical fails when:**

| Situation | Why |
|---|---|
| **Semantics** ("is this a pedestrian?") | "Semantics" here means *meaning* or *category* — what something *is*, as opposed to where it is geometrically. There is no hand-crafted mathematical feature that encodes "human-ness" or "car-ness." This single limitation is the entire reason deep learning took over image recognition: it can learn a representation of "pedestrian" from data in a way no human-designed formula ever managed. |
| **Textureless / repetitive surfaces** | A "keypoint" (from 1.5–1.6) is a distinctive, locatable point in an image — like a corner or a sharply-textured blob. A blank wall has no distinctive points at all (nothing to grab onto), and a tiled floor has *too many* identical-looking points (every tile corner looks the same as every other), so matching becomes hopelessly ambiguous — like trying to find your specific seat in a stadium where every seat looks exactly alike with no row or seat numbers. |
| **Wide baseline / extreme viewpoint** | "Baseline" is the distance or angle between two camera positions looking at the same scene. SIFT (the keypoint descriptor from 1.6, designed to recognize the same physical point in two different photos) tolerates being viewed from up to roughly 30° of angle change before the patch of pixels around a point looks too different to recognize — similar to how you can still recognize a friend's face from a bit off to the side, but not from directly behind them. Beyond that angle, the descriptor's numeric fingerprint of the patch changes too much to match reliably. |
| **Large illumination change** (day↔night, seasonal) | Gradient-based descriptors (SIFT, ORB — anything that works by measuring how brightness changes across small patches, i.e. "gradients") assume that the same physical point looks *photometrically* similar under different lighting — meaning: similar brightness pattern, similar contrast. A daytime photo and a night photo of the same street can have almost no shared brightness pattern (streetlights, shadows, and color all flip), so this assumption breaks completely, the same way a black-and-white photo negative would confuse someone trying to match it visually against the original. |
| **Deformable / non-rigid objects** | "Rigid" means the object's shape doesn't change — like a building or a car body. Classical geometric matching (homographies, rigid transforms) assumes the thing you're tracking preserves its shape between two views. A person's face, a flag flapping in the wind, or a cat walking are *non-rigid* — their shape genuinely deforms — so trying to fit a rigid geometric model (like a single flat-plane transform) to them is using the wrong kind of math entirely, not just a slightly-off approximation. |
| **Motion blur, low light, low resolution** | Classical detectors (Harris, SIFT, ORB) all work by measuring sharp, high-contrast changes in brightness — edges and corners. Motion blur smears those sharp changes into soft gradients, low light adds random noise that swamps the true signal, and low resolution literally throws away the fine detail the detector needs. All three degrade the *contrast* the detector depends on, so detection quality falls off a cliff quickly — like trying to read fine print through fogged-up glasses. |

### What the hybrid reality looks like in 2026

So if neither side "wins" outright, what do real systems actually do? The honest answer, and the one that will make you sound like a practitioner rather than someone reciting Module 1 from memory, is: **modern 3D vision pipelines are layered — learned methods handle the front end, classical geometry handles the back end.**

Here's the pipeline, redrawn with plain labels:

```
learned front-end                 classical back-end
─────────────────                 ──────────────────
SuperPoint / DISK / ALIKED   ┐
LightGlue / LoFTR / RoMa     ├──> RANSAC (MAGSAC++) ──> PnP / 5-pt ──> Bundle Adjustment ──> 3D
learned depth priors         ┘         ^                                      ^
                                       │                                      │
                              still pure geometry              still Levenberg–Marquardt
```

Let's translate every piece of that diagram into plain English, left to right:

- **SuperPoint / DISK / ALIKED**: these are *learned* keypoint detectors — neural-network replacements for the hand-designed Harris/SIFT/ORB detectors you learned in Module 1. They find distinctive points in an image the same way SIFT does, but the "what counts as distinctive" rule was learned from data instead of hand-designed.
- **LightGlue / LoFTR / RoMa**: these are *learned* matchers — neural-network replacements for "ratio test" matching (from 1.7). They decide which point in image A corresponds to which point in image B, but again, the matching logic was learned rather than a fixed distance-threshold rule.
- **learned depth priors**: some modern systems also have a neural network make an initial rough guess at "how far away is each pixel," to help everything downstream converge faster.
- All three of those feed into **RANSAC (MAGSAC++)** — this is exactly the RANSAC algorithm from 1.8 (a method for finding the best-fitting geometric model while ignoring wrong/noisy matches, called "outliers"), just with a smarter modern variant (MAGSAC++) that removes the need to hand-pick a threshold for "how close counts as a match."
- **PnP / 5-pt**: these are classical closed-form algorithms (meaning: solved directly via known equations, not learned) that compute camera position/orientation from a set of matched points. "PnP" = Perspective-n-Point (given n matched 3D-to-2D points, work out where the camera was standing). "5-pt" = the five-point algorithm, a similar idea for two uncalibrated cameras.
- **Bundle Adjustment**: a classical optimization step (mentioned in 1.9/1.10 and expanded in Module 5) that jointly refines *every* camera position and *every* 3D point simultaneously, by minimizing the total reprojection error (the gap between where a 3D point *should* project onto each photo, and where it actually appears). It's solved with Levenberg–Marquardt, a general-purpose classical numerical optimization algorithm — not a neural network.

So: **the "does this patch of image A look like the same surface as this patch of image B" question is now usually handled by a learned network** — because that is fundamentally a pattern-recognition/inference question (problem type 1 from earlier), and learned features cope with lighting and viewpoint change far better than a fixed hand-written rule like SIFT's gradient histograms ever could.

But **nobody has replaced Bundle Adjustment with a neural network**, and this is worth sitting with, because it's the crux of the whole lesson. Bundle Adjustment is a *sparse nonlinear least-squares problem* — a mouthful, but it just means: "minimize a big sum of squared errors, where each error only depends on a small handful of the unknowns, not all of them" (that's what "sparse" means here — most variables don't interact with most other variables). This kind of problem has a known-correct answer, well-studied numerical methods exist to solve it efficiently even with millions of points, and it comes with reliability guarantees. A neural network trying to approximate this would produce a plausible-looking answer with no error bars, no guarantee it actually converged to the true minimum, and none of the mathematical structure that makes the classical solvers fast on huge problems. So the classical method isn't being kept around out of nostalgia — it's genuinely still the better engineering choice for this specific sub-problem.

Even the newest 3D representation technique, **3D Gaussian Splatting** (a 2023-era method for representing 3D scenes as clouds of soft, blob-shaped "gaussians" instead of a mesh or point cloud — covered later in the syllabus, section 5.10), typically doesn't start from scratch. It's usually *initialized* from a point cloud produced by COLMAP — which means, underneath, from SIFT keypoint detection + RANSAC + Bundle Adjustment. Even the most modern generative-3D technique in this field is still standing on Module 1's shoulders.

**The one-sentence version, worth memorizing for an interview:** *"Deep learning replaced the parts of vision that were about recognition and correspondence quality; it has not replaced the parts that are about geometry and optimization, and current systems are explicitly hybrid."*

### 🎯 Top-1% distinction

Here is the trap most candidates fall into, in one direction or the other: they either say **"classical CV is obsolete, everything is deep learning now"** or they say **"real vision doesn't need deep learning, geometry is what matters."** Both statements sound confident, and both are simply wrong — and worse, both signal to an experienced interviewer that you haven't actually built anything at production scale, because anyone who has knows the answer is "both, layered, for different reasons."

The strong answer is the layered picture from above, stated with a concrete comparison you can actually name: contrast **ORB-SLAM3** (a fully classical real-time localization system — hand-designed features, hand-designed matching, classical back-end) against **a learned front-end (SuperPoint/LightGlue) feeding a classical COLMAP back-end**. Being able to name both systems, and say precisely where the boundary between "learned" and "classical" sits in each, is what makes an interviewer sit up — it proves you've actually read real pipelines, not just a summary of the debate.

The second thing that separates a strong candidate: **being able to design the right pipeline for a stated real-world constraint**, on the spot. For example:

- *"Embedded device, 5-watt power budget, must localize itself inside a warehouse"* → the answer is ORB + binary descriptors (BRIEF/ORB — small, fast, CPU-friendly, from 1.6) + a small pre-built map, **not** a Vision Transformer (a large, GPU-hungry learned model you'll meet in Module 6). The power and compute budget rules out anything heavy, and the environment is controlled enough that classical methods are reliable.
- *"Match tourist photos of a cathedral, taken across different seasons"* → the answer is a learned dense matcher like LoFTR or RoMa, **not** plain SIFT, because the huge seasonal lighting/appearance changes (leaves, snow, sun angle) are exactly the failure mode classical gradient-based descriptors can't survive, from the "Classical fails when" table above.

Interviewers ask questions shaped like this specifically because they're testing *judgment under constraints*, not whether you can recite algorithm names. Knowing that RANSAC exists is table stakes; knowing when NOT to reach for it is the top-1% signal.

### ✅ Module 1 synthesis check

Here is the exercise. Take your time with it — it's meant to pull together everything from Module 1, not just this last lesson.

**The scenario:** You have roughly 200 handheld phone photos of the outside of a building, taken over two days. Because it's handheld and spans two days, the photos have varying sun position (morning vs. afternoon light, different shadows) and some photos have motion blur (the photographer's hand shook). Your job: design, end to end, a system that turns these 200 photos into a single textured 3D model of the building.

**What "design end to end" means concretely:** write out every stage of the pipeline, in order, from "raw photos" to "final 3D model." For each stage:

1. Name the specific algorithm or technique you'd use (pull from anything in Module 1, or the hybrid picture above — e.g. "SuperPoint for keypoint detection" is a valid, specific answer; "some kind of feature detector" is not).
2. Say whether that stage is **classical** or **learned**, and *why* you made that choice — tie it back to the inference-vs-measurement distinction from the start of this lesson. Is this stage a "what is this" question, or a "where exactly is this" question?

Then, as a second part: **name the three stages of your pipeline most likely to fail on this specific input** (remember — two days of varying sun position, and some motion blur are both mentioned deliberately, they're hints about where things will break), and for each one, say what you would do to reduce the risk of that failure (a "mitigation").

There's no single correct pipeline here — the goal is to see whether you can reason through the full chain and correctly flag where the stated constraints (lighting change, blur) will bite.

### 🔨 Module 1 capstone build

Two hands-on labs. Do each one in a single sitting — they're each scoped to be finishable in one sustained session, not a multi-week project.

**Lab A — SIFT vs. ORB matching benchmark.**

The goal of this lab is to stop taking anyone's word for "SIFT is more accurate but ORB is faster" and instead *measure it yourself*, so you have a real, personally-verified answer the next time someone asks you "when would you use ORB instead of SIFT?" — which, incidentally, is the single most commonly asked Module 1 interview question, so this lab directly pays off in interviews.

Use the **Oxford VGG affine dataset** — a standard benchmark set of image pairs, each pair designed to stress-test one specific kind of change:
- `graf` = viewpoint change (the camera moved to a different angle)
- `boat` = rotation + scale change (the camera rotated and zoomed)
- `bikes` = motion blur
- `leuven` = illumination change (lighting got brighter/darker)

For each image pair, and for each method (SIFT and ORB), compute:
- **number of keypoints found** — a simple count, tells you how "busy" each detector is on this image.
- **repeatability** — the fraction of keypoints found in image A that are *also* found at the corresponding location in image B (a keypoint is useless if it doesn't show up reliably in both images).
- **precision/recall of matches after the ratio test** (the ratio test is the 0.8-threshold matching-filter technique from 1.7) — precision = of the matches you kept, how many were actually correct; recall = of the correct matches that existed, how many did you actually keep.
- **homography estimation error vs. ground truth** — the Oxford dataset gives you the *true* transform between each image pair, so you can measure exactly how far off your RANSAC-estimated homography (1.9) is from the real one.
- **wall-clock time** for detect/describe/match — literally, how many seconds/milliseconds it took, measured on a real clock, for each stage.

Produce one summary figure (a chart) per dataset (so four charts total: graf, boat, bikes, leuven), each one showing SIFT vs. ORB across your measured numbers.

**Lab B — Panorama from scratch.**

Take 5–8 handheld overlapping photos of a scene, and build a complete panorama-stitching pipeline yourself, stage by stage:

1. **Detect** keypoints in each photo (SIFT or ORB, from 1.6).
2. **Match** keypoints between overlapping photo pairs (ratio test, from 1.7).
3. **RANSAC** to reject bad matches and estimate a rough transform between each pair (1.8).
4. **DLT + normalization** — DLT ("Direct Linear Transform") is the specific linear-algebra method for solving for a homography's 8 numbers from matched points; "normalization" (Hartley normalization) is a numerical-stability trick applied before running DLT, both from 1.9.
5. **LM bundle adjustment over all rotations and a shared focal length** — refine every photo's estimated rotation, and one shared camera focal length, jointly, using Levenberg–Marquardt (the classical optimizer mentioned earlier in this lesson) to minimize total pixel-alignment error across the whole set of photos at once, not just pair by pair.
6. **Cylindrical warp** — remap each flat photo onto a cylinder shape (mathematically, a coordinate transform) so that when you unroll the cylinder, wide panoramas don't distort at the edges the way a flat projection would.
7. **Gain compensation** — adjust each photo's overall brightness so that overlapping regions from different photos (taken at slightly different exposures) match in brightness.
8. **Graph-cut seam** — instead of just gluing photos together along a straight line, use a graph-cut algorithm to find the *best possible curved seam* between two overlapping photos, one that runs through the parts of the image least likely to show a visible mismatch (e.g. through a blank sky rather than through someone's face).
9. **Multi-band blend** — blend the two photos across that seam at multiple spatial frequencies (blur levels) simultaneously — this is what stops a hard visible line from appearing at the seam.

Deliberately shoot one of your photo sets by *walking sideways* between shots (rather than just rotating in place from a fixed spot). This introduces genuine *parallax* — nearby objects shift relative to distant ones between shots, which a simple planar-homography model cannot represent correctly — and you should watch it *produce* visible ghosting artifacts (doubled edges, blurry duplicated objects) in your panorama. Document what you see happening and why. There's a reason this instruction says to cause the failure on purpose: understanding a failure you triggered yourself and diagnosed sticks in memory far more reliably than reading about it in someone else's writeup.

---

## Going Deeper — Papers, Sources and Research Scope

*A quick note before you dive in: "arXiv" is a free, open repository where researchers post papers, often before or alongside formal publication; an "arXiv ID" (like `1912.05909`) is just that paper's unique reference number on the site, and you can paste it into arxiv.org/abs/<ID> to find it directly. Most of Module 1's classic papers predate arXiv's widespread use, so several entries below are "journal-only," meaning you'll need to search the paper's title directly (via Google Scholar or similar) rather than following a link. If any link below 404s (comes back "page not found"), search for the title rather than trusting the exact URL — papers move.*

### A. The canonical papers

Read these in the order given. Pay attention not just to the original papers but also to the "re-examination" papers listed alongside them — papers written later that went back and tested whether the original's claims actually held up. Knowing that a classic result was later shown to be only partly right is genuinely different from — and more valuable than — just having memorized the original claim. It's the difference between having *read* a field critically and having merely *repeated* it.

| Paper | Year / Venue | Why it matters | Where |
|---|---|---|---|
| Canny, *A Computational Approach to Edge Detection* | 1986, PAMI | Edge detection framed as an **optimization problem** — meaning Canny didn't just propose a recipe of steps, he first wrote down three mathematical criteria a "good" edge detector must satisfy (detect real edges, localize them precisely, respond once per edge) and then derived a method that satisfies them. That's why the Canny detector is still standard today while the cruder Sobel-plus-threshold approach isn't — Canny's method is provably optimal under its own stated goals, not just "empirically pretty good." | IEEE PAMI 8(6) |
| Witkin, *Scale-Space Filtering* | 1983, IJCAI | The original short paper arguing that "scale" (how zoomed-in or zoomed-out you're looking at an image) should be treated as its own mathematical dimension you can slide along continuously, not just a fixed setting you pick once. This idea underlies all of 1.4 (scale-space and image pyramids). | Proc. IJCAI |
| Lindeberg, *Feature Detection with Automatic Scale Selection* | 1998, IJCV | This is where the "γ-normalization" trick comes from (a specific mathematical correction, using a Greek letter gamma, that makes blob-detection responses comparable across different scales) and explains why the Laplacian-of-Gaussian (LoG) filter's response peaks exactly at a blob's true size. This paper is the mathematical foundation underneath everything in section 1.4. | IJCV 30(2) |
| Harris & Stephens, *A Combined Corner and Edge Detector* | 1988, Alvey Vision Conf. | Only six pages long. Introduces the "structure tensor" (a small matrix summarizing how brightness changes around a point, from 1.5) and explains why you compute the corner score using $\det - k\,\mathrm{tr}^2$ (the determinant of that matrix, minus a constant $k$ times its trace-squared) instead of the more expensive route of directly computing the matrix's eigenvalues. | Alvey |
| Shi & Tomasi, *Good Features to Track* | 1994, CVPR | Proposes using $\min(\lambda_1,\lambda_2)$ — the smaller of the structure tensor's two eigenvalues — instead of Harris's determinant-based shortcut, arguing from what actually matters for point-tracking (both eigenvalues need to be large, not just their combination). This connects directly to optical-flow tracking, covered later in section 5.7. | CVPR 1994 |
| **Lowe, *Distinctive Image Features from Scale-Invariant Keypoints*** | 2004, IJCV | This is the original SIFT paper (SIFT = the keypoint descriptor from 1.6). Worth reading in full — the ablation studies in section 7 (an "ablation" is an experiment where you remove one design choice at a time to measure its individual effect) are a genuinely great example of how to justify each design decision on its own evidence, rather than just asserting the final recipe works. | IJCV 60(2) |
| Mikolajczyk & Schmid, *A Performance Evaluation of Local Descriptors* | 2005, PAMI | Established the standard methodology for how to fairly compare different descriptor methods against each other — the contribution of this paper is really the *evaluation protocol itself*, which the whole field then adopted. | PAMI 27(10) |
| Rublee et al., *ORB: An Efficient Alternative to SIFT or SURF* | 2011, ICCV | Introduces rBRIEF (a refined, "rotation-aware" version of the fast binary descriptor BRIEF, from 1.6) and, more interestingly, a small learning step used at design time to pick which pixel-comparison tests are most useful (least redundant with each other) — a clever mix of a hand-designed method with a tiny bit of data-driven tuning baked in. | ICCV 2011 |
| **Fischler & Bolles, *Random Sample Consensus*** | 1981, CACM | Two pages long, and still exactly correct today. This is the original RANSAC paper (1.8). Read the original rather than a summary blog post — the way it frames the idea ("fit a model first from a minimal random sample, then check who agrees with it" — as opposed to trying to fit using every point at once and hoping outliers don't ruin it) is clearer here than in almost anything written about it since. | CACM 24(6) |
| **Hartley, *In Defense of the Eight-Point Algorithm*** | 1997, PAMI | Technically about a topic from Module 5 (the eight-point algorithm for estimating camera geometry), but it's worth reading now because it's the clearest single demonstration in all of computer vision that a numerically well-conditioned "bad" algorithm can beat a numerically unconditioned "good" one. ("Numerically conditioned" roughly means: the numbers involved are scaled sensibly so that floating-point arithmetic doesn't quietly destroy your precision.) This is exactly the same "normalization" idea that shows up in the DLT homography method from 1.9. | PAMI 19(6) |
| Barath et al., *MAGSAC++* | 2020, CVPR (1912.05909) | A modern improvement to RANSAC that removes the need to manually choose an "inlier threshold" (the cutoff distance for deciding whether a point counts as agreeing with a fitted model) by mathematically averaging over many possible thresholds instead of picking one. This is now the default RANSAC variant used inside OpenCV (a widely-used open-source computer vision library). | arXiv 1912.05909 |
| Brown & Lowe, *Automatic Panoramic Image Stitching* | 2007, IJCV | Walks through the entire panorama pipeline end to end — matching, RANSAC, bundle adjustment, and blending. This paper is essentially a fully worked-out version of the Lab B capstone build above, written up properly by the people who built the real system. | IJCV 74(1) |
| **DeTone et al., *SuperPoint*** | 2018, CVPRW (1712.07629) | The learned (neural-network-based) successor to SIFT, trained in a self-supervised way (meaning: it doesn't need humans to hand-label "correct" keypoints — it generates its own training signal, in this case by warping images with known synthetic transforms, called "homographic adaptation"). Read it specifically to see which parts of the classical SIFT pipeline got kept, and which got replaced by learning. | arXiv 1712.07629 |
| **Sarlin et al., *SuperGlue*** | 2020, CVPR (1911.11763) | Reframes the matching step (1.7) as an "optimal transport" problem (a mathematical framework for optimally moving/pairing up two sets of points) solved with attention (the same attention mechanism used in Transformers, which you already know from general deep learning), replacing the classical ratio-test + RANSAC combo. This paper is a good concrete illustration of just how much of Module 1's matching stage has now been taken over by learned methods. | arXiv 1911.11763 |

### B. The single best source, per hard topic

This isn't a link dump — it's one carefully chosen resource per concept, picked because it genuinely explains that specific concept better than the alternatives do.

- **Scale-space, and why the Gaussian's width $\sigma$ (the Greek letter "sigma," used throughout Module 1 to mean "how blurry/wide the Gaussian blur kernel is") is the only sensible choice of blurring kernel (1.4).** Szeliski, *Computer Vision: Algorithms and Applications*, 2nd ed., §3.5 — a free PDF at `szeliski.org/Book`. Read it alongside the Lindeberg 1998 paper above for the fuller version of the γ-normalization argument that Szeliski compresses into a shorter treatment.
- **Convolution, filtering, and separability (1.2)** — "separability" means a 2D blur can be computed as two cheaper 1D blurs (one across rows, one across columns) instead of one expensive 2D operation, because the math works out to be equivalent. Shree Nayar's *First Principles of Computer Vision*, the "Image Filtering" module on YouTube, builds the signal-processing intuition here better than any textbook.
- **Canny's three criteria (1.3).** Go back to the original 1986 paper's Section II — it's genuinely more readable than its reputation as a "hard classic paper" suggests.
- **Harris corners (1.5).** Szeliski §7.1.1 for the mathematical derivation, then the original 1988 Alvey paper above for the reasoning behind the $k$ constant in the corner-score formula.
- **SIFT, in exact implementation detail (1.6).** The `VLFeat` SIFT tutorial (`vlfeat.org/api/sift.html`) — it spells out the exact orientation-histogram construction and the descriptor-normalization steps that Lowe's original paper leaves somewhat implicit (assumed knowledge rather than spelled out).
- **RANSAC's iteration count (1.8)** — "iteration count" means how many random samples RANSAC needs to try before it's statistically confident it found a good model. Hartley & Zisserman, *Multiple View Geometry*, 2nd ed., §4.7 (pages 117–121) — the adaptive-iteration-count table on page 119 is the single thing worth memorizing from that section.
- **Homography, and the DLT method for solving for one (1.9).** Hartley & Zisserman §4.1 (pages 88–93). The normalization discussion in §4.4 is the practically important part — it's the difference between an implementation that works reliably and one that silently produces garbage due to numerical instability.

### C. Reference implementations worth reading

Reading real production code (not toy example code) is one of the fastest ways to see which details of a textbook algorithm actually matter in practice.

- **`opencv/opencv` → `modules/features2d/src/sift.simd.hpp`.** Look specifically for the function `calcSIFTDescriptor` — pay attention to the "trilinear interpolation" (a smooth way of spreading a gradient measurement across neighboring histogram bins, rather than dumping it entirely into one bin) into the 4×4×8 histogram (4×4 spatial cells, each holding an 8-direction gradient histogram — this is where SIFT's "128-dimensional" descriptor size comes from: 4×4×8 = 128), and the two-stage normalization (scale the whole descriptor vector to length 1 → clamp/cap any single value above 0.2 → scale to length 1 again). That clamping step is a one-sentence footnote in the original paper, but it's the actual trick that makes SIFT robust to sudden lighting changes.
- **`opencv/opencv` → `modules/calib3d/src/ptsetreg.cpp`.** Look at `RANSACPointSetRegistrator::run` — notice how the number of iterations RANSAC will run is *recalculated on the fly* as it finds better and better models, rather than being a single fixed number decided before starting.
- **`colmap/colmap` → `src/feature/sift.cc`.** A production-grade, GPU-accelerated SIFT implementation — useful for seeing which details matter once you're running this at real scale (things like guided matching, tuning the ratio-test threshold, and cross-checking matches in both directions).
- **`magicleap/SuperGluePretrainedNetwork`.** Read `models/superglue.py` — specifically the "Sinkhorn iterations" (a numerical algorithm for solving the optimal-transport matching problem mentioned above) and the "dustbin channel" — a clever trick that gives every keypoint the option to match to "nothing" instead of being forced to match to some (possibly wrong) point in the other image. That dustbin idea is arguably the single most elegant trick in all of learned feature matching.
- **`rmislam/PythonSIFT`.** A clean, dependency-free SIFT implementation in plain NumPy, easy to actually read line by line. Read it *after* you've written your own SIFT implementation, specifically to check your handling of DoG (Difference-of-Gaussians, from 1.4) octaves (an "octave" is one doubling of scale in the image pyramid).

### D. Open research questions

To be upfront about this: most of what's in Module 1 is settled, well-understood science at this point — these are the genuinely still-open threads, where reasonable experts disagree or where the honest answer is "we don't fully know yet."

1. **Do learned detectors actually beat SIFT once the images look different from anything in their training data ("distribution shift"), or only on the specific benchmark images they were tuned against?** *Why this is still open:* the standard benchmarks (like HPatches) are mostly flat/planar scenes, well-lit, with only mild lighting changes. SuperPoint and SuperGlue win clearly on those benchmarks — but the picture under night-vs-day, bad weather, or across-seasons comparisons is genuinely murkier, and different evaluation papers disagree with each other. *A minimal experiment you could actually run:* fix one matching pipeline, swap in SIFT vs. ORB vs. SuperPoint vs. a modern dense matcher, test all four on the "Aachen Day-Night" dataset and a season-varying dataset, and measure final camera-pose error (not just raw match count, which can be misleading). This needs only inference (no training), so a laptop GPU is enough. **This is feasible as a personal project.**
2. **Of RANSAC's practical success, how much comes from the random sampling strategy itself, versus how much comes from the "local optimization" refinement step that runs afterward?** *Why this is still open:* "LO-RANSAC" (Locally-Optimized RANSAC — a variant that adds an extra refinement pass after finding a good-looking model) produces large accuracy gains, but modern RANSAC variants usually bundle several improvements together at once, making it hard to isolate exactly which change is doing the work — this is the classic "ablation is never clean" problem. *A minimal experiment:* implement plain/vanilla RANSAC, LO-RANSAC, and MAGSAC++ all inside one shared test harness, then specifically turn the local-optimization step on and off to isolate its individual effect, using synthetic data where you control exactly what fraction of points are outliers. CPU only, no GPU needed. **Very feasible — genuinely a good weekend project that ends with a real, publishable plot.**
3. **Is the "ratio test" (the 0.8 threshold rule for filtering matches, from 1.7) actually mathematically optimal, or just a convenient rule of thumb that stuck?** *Why this is still open:* Lowe's original 0.8 threshold has been used essentially unchanged for about twenty years, and its only justification in the original 2004 paper is a single histogram plot, not a derivation. *A minimal experiment:* work out, mathematically, what statistical test the ratio test is implicitly approximating (a "likelihood-ratio test," under some assumed model of how descriptor distances are distributed), then measure empirically where that approximation starts to break down. **Feasible, and a genuinely under-examined question — there isn't a settled answer in the literature.**
4. **Does classical feature matching still win anywhere, if you compare methods on truly equal computational footing?** *Why this is still open:* most comparisons in papers quietly ignore that SIFT can run entirely on a CPU, while learned methods usually assume a GPU is available — that's not a fair fight if the real-world question is "which is better for my specific hardware." *A minimal experiment:* fix a strict latency budget on one specific real device, then ask which method actually wins *within* that budget, rather than which wins with unlimited compute. This connects directly to capstone project #4 in `Supplement-Capstone-Projects.md`. **Feasible.**
5. ~~Building a better corner detector.~~ **This one is considered closed** — don't spend research time here. The remaining error in modern pipelines lives in the descriptor and the matcher, not in the corner/keypoint detector itself.
