## Module 1 — Interview Rapid-Fire Summary

The table below is intentionally dense — it's meant as a fast pre-interview refresher once you already understand every concept, not a first introduction. Right after the table is a plain-English walkthrough of every piece of notation and shorthand used in it, row by row, in case any of it doesn't immediately click.

| Concept | The one thing to say |
|---|---|
| Colour spaces | HSV decouples intensity but Hue is circular and undefined at low saturation; LAB is perceptually uniform so Euclidean distance ≈ perceived difference |
| Gamma | sRGB is non-linear; blending/resizing is only correct in linear light |
| Gaussian | separable, no Fourier side lobes, semigroup $G_{\sigma_1}*G_{\sigma_2} = G_{\sqrt{\sigma_1^2+\sigma_2^2}}$, unique scale-space kernel |
| Canny | DoG → NMS (thins to 1 px) → hysteresis (exploits connectivity); optimal under Canny's 3 criteria |
| Scale-space | $\partial G/\partial\sigma = \sigma\nabla^2 G$ ⇒ DoG $\approx (k-1)\sigma^2\nabla^2 G$; $\sigma^2$ normalisation is mandatory; Gaussian is unique by the causality axiom |
| Blob size | detected $\sigma$ ⇒ radius $\sqrt{2}\sigma$ |
| Harris | structure tensor $M$; $R = \det - \kappa\,\mathrm{tr}^2$; rotation-invariant, **not** scale-invariant; same $M$ governs Lucas–Kanade trackability |
| SIFT | DoG extrema → sub-pixel fit → contrast + edge rejection → orientation histogram → $4{\times}4{\times}8$=128-D → normalise/clamp 0.2/normalise |
| ORB | oriented FAST (Harris-ranked) + rBRIEF (learned decorrelated binary tests), 32 bytes, Hamming distance |
| Matching | ratio test $d_1/d_2 < 0.8$ removes ~90% of false matches, loses ~5% of true; L2 ≡ cosine for normalised vectors |
| RANSAC | $N = \log(1-p)/\log(1-w^s)$; exponential in $s$ ⇒ minimal solvers; $t^2 = \chi^2_{m,\alpha}\sigma^2$; always refit on inliers |
| Homography | 8 DoF; DLT + **Hartley normalisation** + SVD null space; valid only for planar scenes or pure rotation |
| Panorama | RANSAC $H$ → BA → cylindrical warp → gain compensation → graph-cut seam → **multi-band (Laplacian pyramid) blend** |
| Classical vs DL | learned front-end (features/matching), classical back-end (RANSAC, PnP, BA); hybrid is the 2026 answer |

**Plain-English gloss, row by row** — read this once alongside the table above, then the table alone should read as a genuine refresher rather than a wall of symbols:

- **Colour spaces.** HSV = Hue/Saturation/Value, a color representation that separates "what color" (Hue) and "how vivid" (Saturation) from "how bright" (Value/intensity) — useful because you can change brightness without disturbing color. "Circular" means Hue wraps around like a clock face (0° and 360° are the same color, red), which trips up naive averaging. "Undefined at low saturation" means when a color is nearly gray, its Hue value becomes meaningless/noisy — think of trying to say what "hue" a nearly-white pixel is. LAB is a color space explicitly designed so that a straight-line (Euclidean) distance between two color values matches how different they actually *look* to a human eye — useful whenever you need "color similarity" to behave intuitively.
- **Gamma.** "sRGB" is the standard color encoding your camera/screen actually stores and displays pixel values in, and it applies a non-linear (curved, not straight-line) mapping from real-world light brightness to stored pixel numbers, for historical/perceptual reasons. If you blend or resize images directly in that stored (non-linear) representation, the math comes out subtly wrong — you first have to convert to "linear light" (undo the curve) before averaging brightness values, then convert back.
- **Gaussian.** "Separable" (explained above, under section B) means a 2D Gaussian blur splits into two cheap 1D blurs. "No Fourier side lobes" means that in the frequency domain (a way of viewing an image/signal as a sum of waves of different frequencies), the Gaussian's shape has no unwanted secondary bumps ("ringing artifacts") the way some other blur shapes do. The "semigroup" property $G_{\sigma_1}*G_{\sigma_2} = G_{\sqrt{\sigma_1^2+\sigma_2^2}}$ says: blurring an image with one Gaussian, then blurring the result with a second Gaussian, gives you exactly the same result as blurring once with a single bigger Gaussian of a specific combined width — a clean, useful mathematical property most other blur kernels lack. This full bundle of properties is why the Gaussian is the *only* mathematically well-justified choice of blur kernel for building a scale-space (the sequence of increasingly-blurred versions of an image used throughout 1.4).
- **Canny.** DoG = Difference of Gaussians (subtracting two different blur levels of the same image to approximate edge/blob detection). NMS = Non-Maximum Suppression, a thinning step that keeps only the single strongest edge pixel in a local neighborhood, turning thick blurry edge regions into crisp 1-pixel-wide lines. "Hysteresis" is Canny's two-threshold trick: a pixel counts as an edge if it's either clearly strong on its own, OR it's weak but directly connected to a strong edge pixel — this "exploits connectivity" (the fact that real edges form continuous lines) to avoid gaps.
- **Scale-space.** This row states, in one line of math, why the Gaussian blur's derivative with respect to $\sigma$ (how you're changing scale) equals a scaled version of the Laplacian (a second-derivative operator measuring how sharply brightness curves), and that this relationship is *why* the Difference-of-Gaussians (DoG) trick is a fast, good approximation to the more expensive Laplacian-of-Gaussian calculation used for blob detection. "$\sigma^2$ normalization is mandatory" refers back to the Lindeberg paper above — without dividing by $\sigma^2$, responses at different scales aren't comparable to each other, so you couldn't fairly say "this blob is bigger than that one." "The causality axiom" is the mathematical requirement that blurring should never *create* new fake detail that wasn't in the original image — Gaussian blur is the only kernel proven to satisfy this.
- **Blob size.** If a blob detector reports its strongest response at scale $\sigma$ (i.e. this blob's optimal amount of blur was sigma), the blob's actual real-world radius is $\sqrt{2}\sigma$ — a fixed conversion factor derived from the math of the Laplacian-of-Gaussian filter.
- **Harris.** The "structure tensor" $M$ is a small 2×2 matrix (from section 1.5) built out of local brightness-gradient measurements around a pixel, and its two eigenvalues tell you whether that point looks like a flat region, an edge, or a corner. Harris's original shortcut, $R = \det - \kappa\,\mathrm{tr}^2$ (determinant minus a constant times the trace squared), avoids the more expensive step of directly computing those two eigenvalues. Harris corners are "rotation-invariant" (still detected correctly if the image is rotated) but explicitly **not** "scale-invariant" (a real corner can fail to be detected if the image is zoomed in or out too much) — that limitation is exactly why SIFT was later built on top of a proper scale-space instead. This same matrix $M$ also determines whether a point is trackable frame-to-frame in the Lucas–Kanade optical-flow method (covered later, in section 5.7) — same math, two different uses.
- **SIFT.** The pipeline, in order: find local extrema (maximum or minimum points) in the DoG pyramid → refine each one's location to sub-pixel precision by fitting a small mathematical model → throw out low-contrast points and points sitting right on an edge (edges are ambiguous, not "corner-like" enough to be reliable) → build a histogram of local gradient orientations to assign the keypoint a consistent orientation (so matching works even if the image is rotated) → build the final descriptor from a 4×4 grid of cells, each holding an 8-bin gradient-direction histogram (4×4×8 = 128 numbers total, hence "128-D," meaning 128-dimensional) → normalize that 128-number vector to length 1, clamp any single value above 0.2 (a fixed cap that limits how much any one strong gradient can dominate, improving robustness to sudden bright highlights), then normalize to length 1 again.
- **ORB.** "Oriented FAST" = the FAST corner detector (a fast classical corner-finding rule) with points ranked/filtered using a Harris-like score, plus an assigned orientation (so, like SIFT, it's robust to image rotation). "rBRIEF" = a rotated, learned-and-decorrelated version of BRIEF, a method that builds a descriptor purely from simple brighter/darker pixel-pair comparisons rather than gradient histograms — producing a compact 32-byte binary code per keypoint, compared using Hamming distance (literally: count how many individual bits differ between two binary codes — very fast to compute on a CPU, unlike the floating-point math SIFT needs).
- **Matching.** The "ratio test" (1.7) compares a keypoint's best match distance ($d_1$) to its second-best match distance ($d_2$); if $d_1/d_2$ isn't clearly smaller than the second-best (specifically, below the threshold 0.8), the match is thrown out as too ambiguous. Empirically this removes about 90% of the false/wrong matches, at the cost of also losing about 5% of the matches that were actually correct — a favorable trade-off in most cases. "L2 ≡ cosine for normalized vectors" means: once a descriptor vector has been scaled to length 1 (normalized), measuring plain Euclidean/straight-line distance (L2) between two descriptors gives you mathematically the same ranking as measuring the angle between them (cosine similarity) — so for normalized SIFT descriptors, you don't need to pick between the two, they agree.
- **RANSAC.** The formula $N = \log(1-p)/\log(1-w^s)$ computes how many random samples ("iterations," $N$) RANSAC needs to try, to reach a desired confidence $p$ (e.g. 99%) of having picked at least one all-inlier sample, given $w$ = the fraction of points that are actually inliers (correct matches) and $s$ = how many points are needed to fit one model (e.g. 4 points for a homography). Because $N$ grows exponentially as $s$ increases, there's strong pressure to use "minimal solvers" — algorithms that need the smallest possible number of points to fit a model, to keep $N$ small and RANSAC fast. The threshold formula $t^2 = \chi^2_{m,\alpha}\sigma^2$ is how you rigorously derive the cutoff distance for "counts as an inlier," based on the expected noise level $\sigma$ and a statistical table ($\chi^2$, "chi-squared," a standard probability distribution) rather than guessing a number. "Always refit on inliers" means: after RANSAC finds its best candidate set of inlier points, you should re-fit your final model using *all* of those inliers together (a more accurate, stable fit) rather than just keeping the rough model from the original minimal random sample.
- **Homography.** "8 DoF" (degrees of freedom) means a homography — the mathematical transform mapping one flat plane's view to another's, from 1.9 — has exactly 8 independent numbers that need to be solved for. DLT (Direct Linear Transform) is the method that solves for those 8 numbers from matched point pairs; "Hartley normalization" is the numerical-stability preprocessing step (referenced in the Hartley paper above) that should always be applied before running DLT; "SVD null space" refers to the specific linear-algebra technique (Singular Value Decomposition) used to actually solve the resulting system of equations. The important caveat at the end: a homography is only a mathematically valid model when the scene is genuinely flat (planar) or the camera only rotated in place without moving sideways — apply it to a non-planar scene with camera translation, and you'll get the parallax-ghosting problem mentioned in the Lab B capstone build above.
- **Panorama.** The full pipeline in shorthand: estimate a homography ($H$) between overlapping images via RANSAC → refine everything jointly with Bundle Adjustment (BA, described earlier in this lesson) → warp images onto a cylinder shape to avoid edge distortion → gain compensation to match brightness across photos → find the best seam between overlapping images using a graph-cut algorithm → blend across that seam using multi-band blending (blending separately at multiple levels of a Laplacian pyramid — a stack of images capturing different levels of fine detail — so no visible hard line appears at the seam).
- **Classical vs DL.** The one-line summary of this entire lesson's hybrid picture: modern systems use a *learned* front end for recognizing/matching features (because that's fundamentally a pattern-recognition question), and a *classical* back end for geometry and optimization (RANSAC, PnP, Bundle Adjustment — because those have provably correct answers that classical math already solves well). This hybrid split is presented as the accurate, current (2026) answer to "is classical CV dead?" — it is not dead, it just moved to a different part of the pipeline.
